1 Executive Summary

1.1 Introduction

1.1.1 FEED Objectives, Conclusions and Recommendations
The objective of this project was to conduct a Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) study for capturing
carbon dioxide (CO₂) from cement plant flue gas using non-aqueous solvents (NAS) with a 95% capture
efficiency. The study aimed to develop AACE (Association for the Advancement of Cost Engineering)
Class 3 cost estimates for a commercial-scale CO₂ capture system with a capacity of 2.4 million tonnes of
CO₂ per year (t-CO₂/yr). The results will provide Cemex and the U.S. Department of Energy with reliable
data to support investment decisions, evaluate economic feasibility, and guide future project development
and implementation.
The study was led by RTI International, the CO₂ capture technology developer, with KBR Inc. serving as
the engineering, procurement, and construction (EPC) contractor, and SLB Capturi acting as the Owner’s
Engineer and technology licensor. The project was funded by the U.S. Department of Energy, with cost-
sharing contributions from RTI International, Cemex, and SLB Capturi.
The proposed capture facility is designed for a nominal CO₂ capture capacity of 2.4 million tonnes per year,
based on a 95% capture efficiency from both kiln flue-gas streams and the natural gas–fired boiler that
supplies steam to the capture process. Actual CO₂ recovery will vary with the cement plant’s operating
hours and capacity utilization.
The estimated total capital expenditure (CAPEX) for the NAS CO₂ capture plant integrated with Cemex’s
Balcones cement plant is $849 million. This includes $558 million in direct costs, $54 million in indirect
costs, $51million in engineering costs, $24 million in Owner’s costs, and $104 million in contingency.
Another $18 million accounts for miscellaneous freight and hauling costs and $42 million for escalation.
Fixed operating costs include annual operations and maintenance (O&M) labor, administrative and support
staffing, as well as costs associated with maintenance materials, property taxes, and insurance. The total
annual fixed operating costs were estimated at $11.4 million.
The total annual variable operating costs were estimated at $35.8 million. In addition, fuel and purchased
power costs were estimated at $61.6 million annually. Total annualized operating costs were estimated to
be $109 million, resulting in a breakeven CO₂ capture cost of $101.6 per tonne of CO₂.
The RTI NAS CO₂ capture plant requires 46 MW of electrical power and approximately 610 MMBtu/hr of
natural gas for steam generation.
The current design includes an acid wash system to control emissions requiring ongoing amine makeup and
RTI International has developed an amine recovery process that is planned for inclusion in standard design
packages for future projects. It was not incorporated into the current FEED design, as its development
progressed in parallel with the FEED. When the amine recovery is implemented, the levelized cost of
capture is projected to decrease by at least ~9%.
The FEED study did not identify any major project risks. The solvent reclamation process, which remains
under development, was not included in the current design but its integration into the system is planned for
the detailed design phase.

 8

The current design employs a hybrid air and water cooling system. This configuration significantly affects
both capital cost and electricity consumption. The cost of CO₂ capture could be substantially lower by 13%
at sites where cooling water is readily available. These savings are primarily driven by lower CAPEX for
exchangers, reduced fan power consumption, and shorter flue gas ducting, while additional CAPEX and
power for the larger cooling tower partially offset these benefits. Note: The cost of capture comparison is
conservative, as it does not account for potential reductions in civil works and structural steel associated
with the smaller plot area required for water cooling. Including these savings would likely further reduce
the total cost of capture for water-cooled system.

1.1.2 Host Site and Design Considerations
The Balcones plant is in New Braunfels, Comal County, TX. Total cement production at Balcones is 2.5
million tons per year. The plant has 2 kilns. Kiln #1 has 4 preheater stages with a design capacity of 3,125
tons clinker/day and kiln #2 has 5 preheater stages with an inline calciner with a design capacity of 3,600
tons clinker/day. The flue gas from the preheater section is used for drying the raw materials in the raw
grinding mill before sending it to the baghouse filter for dust removal and then to the stack. The CO2
capture system takes flue gas from the process location after the baghouse. Total CO2 emissions at nominal
capacity is 2.2 MTPA. The CO2 capture plant captures 95% of the CO2 in the combined flue gas from the
cement kilns and the natural gas-fired boiler used to generate steam for solvent regeneration.
The project employs RTI’s NAS solvent process, selected for its low regeneration energy requirements and
high CO₂ regeneration pressure, which together reduce overall operating costs. The design basis was
established using measured site data, including two years of Continuous Emissions Monitoring System
(CEMS) measurements of flue gas flowrates, compositions, temperature and pressure conditions, as well
as site-specific weather data and regulatory environmental limits. A single train can treat the total flue gas
from the cement plant.
The CO₂ capture facility is designed to operate using electricity supplied from the grid, while steam
generated on site by the natural gas–fired boiler provides the thermal energy required for the solvent
regeneration process.

1.2 Process Overview, Host Site Integration and Schedule

1.2.1 Process Overview
A schematic of the process flow diagram is provided in Figure 1. RTI’s CO2 capture process uses RTI-
NAS, a formulation of hydrophobic amine in an organic diluent with low water content (<10 wt.%). The
CO2 capture plant consists of a direct contact cooler (DCC) with integrated SO2 polishing, a flue gas blower,
an absorption column with intercoolers, an advanced emissions control technology and acid wash to control
amine emissions, a regeneration column, associated pumps, storage and receiver vessels, and a CO 2
compression, dehydration, and polishing train. The solvent is continuously recirculated between an absorber
and a regenerator column. Flue gas from a cement plant and the natural gas-fired boiler used to generate
steam for solvent regeneration for the capture plant, after scrubbing SO2 and cooling the flue gas, enters the
absorber column where 95% of the CO2 is captured. CO2 absorption occurs at low temperatures in the
absorber column. The amine in the NAS solvent reacts with CO2. The CO2-rich solvent is regenerated in
the regenerator using LP steam from the natural gas-fired boiler. During the solvent regeneration process,

 9

the solvent is heated, and the reverse reactions predominate to release CO2. The CO2 released in this process
at 65.3 psia (4.5 bar), is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bar) and sent for sequestration. The process
is water-balanced by operating the water wash temperatures close to the dew point temperature of the gas
leaving the DCC, such that the total water entering the CO2 capture process is balanced by the total water
leaving with the treated gas and the CO2-product gas. There is no makeup water required for the NAS
process.

 Figure 1: Schematic of the RTI NAS Process

1.2.2 Technical Design and Integration Challenges
The CO₂ capture plant at Cemex Balcones faced several design challenges that were reviewed and resolved
during the FEED phase. A primary process design constraint was the limited availability of raw water,
which significantly influenced the selection of cooling systems. The amount of water recovered from the
DCC and the supply from the Balcones Cement Plant is able to supply 20% of the requirement for cooling
water makeup. Following an evaluation of various cooling options, a hybrid system combining air cooling
and water cooling was selected. Water cooling is applied in the absorber wash section and DCC trim cooler,
while air coolers—sized for 80°F ambient temperature—handle the majority of the heat rejection. The use
of alternative cooling systems to compensate for limited water availability, has an impact on the overall
cost of CO₂ capture, as detailed in the cost analysis section of this report.
To further minimize water consumption, the design incorporates a steam-integrated CO₂ compression
system, in which boiler feed water is used to generate steam instead of relying on water-cooled interstage
compressors. This configuration eliminates the need for cooling tower makeup water but results in higher
interstage temperatures and a corresponding 33% increase in compression power demand.

 10

1.2.3 Site Arrangement
The inclusion of air-cooled heat exchangers, which require approximately four acres of space, necessitated
relocating the capture facility from its originally identified location closer to the main plant. The proposed
CO₂ capture plant will be located on a greenfield site situated south of the New Braunfels facility, occupying
a plot of approximately 15 acres. Additional Cemex-owned property to the south and west of the proposed
site may be utilized for construction laydown and staging areas. Figure 2 presents a 3D model of the existing
cement plant, including the flue gas duct routing over the rail spurs and Wald Road.

 Figure 2: 3D model view with integrated scan of the existing cement plant, showing the flue gas duct
 routing over the rail spurs and Wald Road.
Figure 3 shows the plant with the proposed plot area for the capture plant across the road from the cement
plant. The flue gas ductwork will be routed over both the rail spurs and the Wald road to connect the cement
plant to the new CO2 capture unit.

 11

1.2.4 Schedule
The Level 1 project schedule is presented in Figure 4. The overall project duration is estimated at
approximately 36 months, from engineering authorization through commissioning. Detailed engineering is
expected to continue for approximately 24 months. Construction is scheduled to begin about eight months
after detailed engineering starts, extending for 24 months and overlapping with engineering activities for
roughly 16 months to enable early field execution. The project would conclude with a three-month
commissioning phase, resulting in a total anticipated duration of approximately 36 months.

Figure 4: Level 1 Schedule for the RTI NAS CO2 Capture Project

1.3 Project Cost Estimate

1.3.1 Capital Cost Estimates
The total estimated project costs for the Carbon Capture Plant have been calculated based on material
quantities, labor hours, wage rates, vendor quotes, and indirect cost allowances. The estimate includes Total
Direct Costs (TDC), indirect field costs, owner's indirect costs, escalation, and contingency.
The direct field costs encompass labor hours, average wage rates, labor costs, material costs, and their
respective contributions as percentages of the Total Direct Cost (TDC). In addition to these direct costs, the
estimate includes indirect field costs, owner’s indirect costs, and engineering/home office services. To
arrive at the Total Installed Cost (TIC), the estimate was further adjusted for escalation and contingency.
The CAPEX cost estimates were developed by KBR based on detailed technical documentation, including
datasheets, requisition packages, specifications, and other relevant materials, to support the procurement of
vendor budgetary quotes. These quotes were evaluated against the associated design specifications to
determine scope inclusions and exclusions. For any excluded items, appropriate allowances were applied
to complete the estimate. Vendor quotes were obtained for all critical mechanical equipment, representing
94% of the total mechanical equipment cost. Additional quotes were also gathered for most electrical and
instrumentation equipment.

 13

Generally, the lowest technically acceptable vendor quotes were selected for inclusion. However, in cases
where there was uncertainty regarding the accuracy or completeness of quotes, average pricing was used
as a conservative approach. Contractor quotations were also included for various infrastructure components
such as the power overhead feeder, gatehouse, warehouse, ECR building, control and change room building,
maintenance building, laboratory, and compressor sheds. The combined value of vendor budgetary quotes
and contractor estimates makes up approximately 66% of the total project equipment and material costs.
The remaining bulk materials and equipment costs were estimated using the ACCE (Association for the
Cost Engineering) methodology, with adjustments based on KBR’s historical cost data. Capital spares were
also incorporated into the estimate in accordance with KBR’s projections.
The project area south of Wald Road and the length of the low duct supports area will need to be over-
excavated by 9’ since the soil (expansive soils) is not suitable for structural backfill, as per the Geotechnical
Report. This excavated soil will need to be disposed of at an approved landfill off-site. Imported non-
expansive soil fill will need to be brought into the site and compacted to replace the over-excavated
expansive soil. The costs for the imported fill, soil transportation, and soil disposal cost were estimated by
KBR based on previous projects in the area.
The estimated total capital expenditure (CAPEX) for the NAS CO₂ capture plant integrated with Cemex’s
Balcones cement plant is $849 million. Summary of the major cost breakdown is presented in Table 1.

 Table 1: Summary of the Cost Breakdown

Both opportunities show strong potential for cost and efficiency improvements and are recommended for
further evaluation and optimization during the detailed design phase to confirm their technical and
economic viability.

1.3.2 Operating Cost Estimates
Operating cost estimates were developed from FEED data, incorporating detailed projections for
consumables, waste treatment, and utilities such as natural gas and electricity for CO₂ capture and
compression. Energy costs were escalated using Cemex’s internal forecasts, while labor costs were based
on required operations and maintenance staffing levels and local wage rates, capturing both fixed and
variable components.
Fixed operating costs include annual operations and maintenance (O&M) labor, administrative and support
staffing, as well as costs associated with maintenance materials, property taxes, and insurance. The total
annual fixed operating costs were estimated at $11.4 million.
Total annual variable operating costs include all consumables necessary for the operation of the facility,
such as NAS solvent, makeup amine, water and wastewater treatment chemicals, caustic soda, sulfuric acid,
activated carbon, de-oxo catalyst, and triethylene glycol (TEG). For other consumables—including sulfuric
acid, caustic soda, and TEG—unit prices were sourced from the U.S. Department of Energy’s baseline
study on retrofit CO₂ capture processes at cement plants [1]. The variable operating costs also include the
disposal of waste materials, including spent TEG, used adsorbents, and spent catalysts.
The total annual variable operating costs were estimated at $35.8 million. In addition, fuel and purchased
power costs were estimated at $61.6 million annually. Total annualized operating costs were estimated to
be $109 million.
With implementation of the process for amine recovery from the acid wash, the total annual variable
operating costs are estimated to be reduced by 36% to $26.9 million.

1.3.3 Economic Analysis
A comprehensive pro forma financial analysis of the CO₂ capture project was conducted by Cemex to
evaluate the project’s economic viability and long-term financial performance. CAPEX estimates were
derived from the AACE Class 3 cost assessment conducted during the FEED study performed by KBR.
Operating cost projections were developed using data from the FEED phase, including detailed estimates
for consumables, waste treatment, and utility consumption. This includes natural gas requirements for steam
generation and electricity usage for the operation of the CO₂ capture and compression systems. To reflect
future operating conditions, natural gas and electricity costs were escalated based on internal Cemex
forecasted rates. Labor costs were estimated by identifying the required operational and maintenance
staffing levels and applying prevailing wage rates for each role. This methodology allowed for the
development of both fixed and variable labor cost projections that reflect local labor market conditions and
the technical complexity of operating the facility.

 16

Plant availability was assumed to be 90%. The CO₂ capture plant was designed with a capture capacity of
2.4 million metric tonnes per year, based on the nominal production capacity of the cement kilns and
expected variations in process flow rates. The average operating capacity of the plant is assumed to be 86%,
which, when accounting for the 90% availability, results in an overall capacity factor of 74%.
Overall cost of capture, excluding transport was estimated to be $101.6/t-CO2. This does not include the
cost for transportation and sequestration. Inclusion of the amine recovery from acid wash waste stream
technology reduces the cost of capture to $93.0/t-CO2.
CO₂ transportation costs were estimated using a recent internal quote from a comparable pipeline
infrastructure project. This baseline was adjusted to reflect the specific conditions of the Balcones CO₂
capture project, including the proximity of the proposed sequestration site. For the purposes of this analysis,
a conservative assumption of 20 miles of pipeline was used to estimate associated transportation costs.
CO₂ storage costs were derived from SLB’s internal estimates based on analogous storage projects. These
estimates include the cost of CO₂ injection and the installation and operation of measurement, monitoring,
and verification (MMV) systems in accordance with regulatory and environmental compliance
requirements. However, the storage cost estimate excludes financing costs, long-term liability provisions,
and pore space fees, which are expected to vary based on final site selection and future regulatory
frameworks.
Together, the transportation and storage cost estimates inform the broader calculation of the levelized cost
of CO₂ capture and storage (CCS), supporting a comprehensive financial evaluation of the carbon
management value chain for the Balcones project.
The analysis incorporates the benefit of federal 45Q tax credits, which offer a per-metric-ton incentive for
captured and permanently sequestered CO₂. Under the most recent guidance provided by the Inflation
Reduction Act, the value of the 45Q credit has been increased to $85 per metric ton for CO₂ stored through
secure geological sequestration, which is the basis used in this analysis.
To complete the analysis, Cemex incorporated company-specific financial assumptions related to financing
structure, cost of capital, and tax considerations to model project cash flows over its operational lifespan.
These inputs were used to determine the levelized cost of capture, as well as key financial metrics, including
the internal rate of return (IRR), net present value (NPV), and payback period.
Results from the business case analysis (BCA) indicates a negative value for the project net present value
(NPV); therefore, further incentives to make the project feasible will be needed in the future. Additional
incentives could be achieved through a combination of (1) further DOE/similar grants to fund carbon
capture plant construction and reduce exposure to high CAPEX, and (2) higher tax incentives to mitigate
project OPEX and/or other endeavors outside of taxes. The BCA indicates that for the project to break even,
given the current 12-year 45Q period, the credit value would need to be approximately $175/MT
sequestered compared to the current $85/MT. Levelized revenues of 45Q credits do not reflect their full
value due to intermediation costs and the fact that the project is evaluated over 25 years while the tax credit
is only valid for 12 years. If the 45Q period were extended to 24 years, the required credit value would be
around $120/MT for the project to break even.
The cost of cement production is projected to increase with the addition of the capture plant. At present,
there is no green premium for decarbonized cement in the United States.

 17

1.4 Lifecycle Analysis
A life cycle assessment (LCA) was conducted for the RTI-NAS carbon capture and sequestration (CCS)
system retrofitted to the Cemex Balcones Cement Plant, based on results from the FEED study targeting
95% capture of direct CO₂ emissions. The cradle-to-gate system boundary included cement plant
operations, CCS infrastructure, and capture and sequestration processes. Environmental impacts were
normalized per metric tonne of cement produced for ASTM Type I/II and Type IL cement products.
This project was modeled using OpenLCA software, utilizing the EcoInvent 3.10 Cutoff System Processes
and NETL databases for life cycle inventory (LCI) data. The study employed the TRACI 2.1 impact
assessment method, incorporating the latest global warming potential (GWP) factors from the
Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Fifth Assessment Report (AR5).
Figures 6 and 7 present the 100-year GWP (AR5) results for both Type IL and Type I/II cement products.
Each figure compares the baseline system (without CCS) and the proposed system (with CCS):
 • Baseline direct CO₂ emissions are shown in orange and indirect CO₂ emissions in blue.
 • The proposed system includes gray for additional CCS-related emissions and green for CO₂
 captured.
 • The final proposed product system (last column) shows direct emissions in orange and indirect
 emissions in light blue.
The proposed CCS configuration achieves:
 • 66.1% reduction in total GHG emissions for Type IL cement, and
 • 66.5% reduction for Type I/II cement.

Under current grid conditions, the proposed CCS system alone reduced cradle-to-gate greenhouse gas
emissions by approximately 66%. Incorporating co-generation increased reductions to 74%, while
renewable power use achieved 76%-77% reductions.

Figure 6:GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT cement
 basis for Type IL cement

 18

 Figure 7: GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT
 cement basis for Type I/II cement
The FEED CCS system is estimated to save ~500 kg CO₂e/MT of Type IL cement and ~543 kg CO₂e/MT
of Type I/II cement compared to baseline operations. As the electrical grid and transportation systems
continue to decarbonize, additional GHG reductions are anticipated. Decreasing embodied carbon in
supporting processes will further enhance the overall environmental performance of the CCS-enabled
cement production system.

1.5 Technoeconomic Analysis
A detailed technoeconomic analysis (TEA) was conducted for CO2 capture at Cemex’s Balcones plant using
RTI-NAS technology, based on data from the AACE Class 3 level FEED study. The TEA follows the DOE
methodology for estimation of levelized cost of capture for retrofit applications in cement plants.
Key results for the base cases and the Cemex Case are summarized in Table 3. Two scenarios are presented
for each case: without amine recovery and with amine recovery, highlighting the impact of amine solvent
recovery on capture costs. All DOE cases use DOE assumptions for financial and operational parameters;
the Cemex Case uses Cemex-specific financial and utility parameters. Costs exclude transport and storage
(T&S).
Table 3: Summary of TEA results

Sensitivity analysis identified the most influential parameters:

 19

 • Capacity factor and CAPEX had the highest impact, with a ±15% change significantly affecting
 the cost of capture.
 • Utility prices (natural gas and electricity) had the lowest impact; a 30% increase raised the cost of
 capture by only 4%.
 • Solvent price (NAS) also had a minor impact, with a 30% increase resulting in a 5% rise in capture
 cost.
These results highlight the economic viability of the RTI-NAS CCS retrofit, the potential benefits of co-
generation, and the importance of plant utilization and capital investment on capture cost.

2 Project Overview

2.1 Introduction
RTI International, with Cemex Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., participated in a joint effort to perform a Front-
End Engineering Design (FEED) study for carbon dioxide (CO2) capture from cement flue gas utilizing the
Non-Aqueous Solvent (NAS) CO2 capture process developed by RTI. The FEED study evaluated the
integration of RTI’s CO2 capture system at an existing cement plant, the Cemex Balcones cement plant at
New Braunfels, Texas.
The CO2 capture plant is designed to remove at least 95% of CO2 from the flue gas from the Balcones
cement plant kilns K1 and K2, and the associated natural gas-fired boiler for steam generation. The plant is
designed for a nominal capacity of 2.4 MPTA (million tonnes per annum) scale CO 2 capture system
integrated with a cement facility. The captured CO2 has a purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, polished to
meet pipeline specifications, and compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara) to be made ready for sequestration.
The CO2 capture plant consists of a direct contact cooler (DCC) with integrated SO2 polishing, a flue gas
blower, an absorption column with intercoolers, a regeneration column, associated pumps, storage and
receiver vessels, and a CO2 compression, dehydration, and polishing train. CO2 product end use
(sequestration, EOR, etc.) will be determined in future phases.
The primary objective of the FEED was to develop an Association for the Advancement of Cost
Engineering (AACE) Class 3 cost estimate for a nominal 2.4 million tonnes per annum (MTPA) CO₂
capture system integrated with a cement production facility.
The purpose of this report is to summarize the project design basis, the key design decisions reviewed and
implemented during the FEED study and the resulting engineering design package and cost-estimation.

2.2 Project Team and Organizational Structure
RTI International served as the prime contractor to DOE-NETL, responsible for overall project leadership
and CO₂ capture technology integration. Other partners on the project included Cemex, KBR and SLB.
Cemex acted as the host site partner, providing critical input on plant operations and site-specific integration
requirements. KBR led the front-end engineering design (FEED), while SLB served as the Owner’s
Engineer, offering independent technical oversight and feedback throughout the study. SLB is also the
technology licensor for RTI’s NAS technology for CO2 capture. Cost-share for the project was provided by
RTI International, Cemex and SLB.
Figure 8 presents the organizational structure for the overall project, while Figure 9 outlines the structure
specific to the FEED study, including the discipline leads.

 21

Figure 8: Organizational Structure for the Project

Figure 9: Organizational Structure for the FEED Study

The project was executed under a single budget period and comprised five main tasks:
 • Task 1 – Project Management and Planning
 • Task 2 – Project Design Basis Definition

 22

 • Task 3 – Basic Design Package (BDP)
 • Task 4 – Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) Study
 • Task 5 – Technology-to-Market (T2M) Strategy

Task 1: Project Management and Planning
Task 1 encompassed all project management and coordination activities. RTI International served as the
prime contractor and was responsible for ensuring that the project met its technical, schedule, and budgetary
objectives. Biweekly meetings were held with all project partners (Cemex, KBR and SLB) to monitor
progress, align on priorities, and make decisions regarding project direction. Additionally, RTI maintained
regular communication with the DOE Project Manager to provide formal status updates and report on
project milestones.
Task 2: Project Design Basis Definition
In Task 2, RTI worked with Cemex to establish a unified design basis for the FEED study. This task also
involved identifying any permitting requirements, environmental reviews, and internal or corporate
approvals necessary to initiate construction of the CO₂ capture facility.
Task 3: Basic Design Package (BDP)
The objective of Task 3 was to develop a Basic Design Package (BDP) for the proposed CO₂ capture plant,
which would serve as the foundation for the FEED effort in Task 4. RTI worked closely with KBR to
complete the BDP. Key deliverables included process flow diagrams (PFDs), heat and material balances
(H&MBs), preliminary equipment sizing based on the balance of plant, a process simulation model, and
supporting technical data. These were informed by prior solvent development work and CO₂ capture
technology evaluations.
Task 4: Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) Study
The EPC contractor led the execution of the FEED study, which included the development of detailed
discipline-specific engineering documents and an AACE Class 3 capital cost estimate (CAPEX). An
independent third party, Saltegra LLC, was selected to conduct a Hazard and Operability (HAZOP)
analysis. A constructability review was performed by Pinnacle Results LLC, with active participation from
all project partners.
Task 5: Technology-to-Market Strategy
In Task 5, RTI used the FEED deliverables and cost estimates to develop operational expenditure (OPEX)
projections and conduct a techno-economic analysis (TEA) of the proposed CO₂ capture system. RTI
collaborated with Cemex to perform a life cycle analysis (LCA), accounting for CO₂ emissions produced,
captured, and avoided—including emissions from steam production required to operate the capture facility.
The project team also conducted an environmental, health, and safety (EH&S) risk assessment for the
capture system. Finally, Cemex developed a business case analysis (BCA) to evaluate the commercial
viability of the proposed process.

2.3 Host site and Design Considerations
The Balcones plant is in New Braunfels, Comal County, TX. Total cement production at Balcones is 2.5
million tons per year. The plant has 2 kilns. Kiln #1 has 4 preheater stages with a design capacity of 3,125
tons clinker/day and kiln #2 has 5 preheater stages with an inline calciner with a design capacity of 3,600

 23

the water wash for the treated gas leaving the absorber. However, using alternative cooling due to the lack
of cooling water impacts the overall capture cost" as shown in the cost analysis sections of this report.

2.4 Process Overview

2.4.1 Overview of Process Design
A schematic of the process flow diagram is provided in Figure 11. RTI’s CO2 capture process uses RTI-
NAS, a formulation of hydrophobic amine in an organic diluent with low (<10 wt.%) water content to
capture >95% of CO2 from a cement plant and the associated natural gas-fired boiler for providing steam
for solvent regeneration. The solvent is continuously recirculated between an absorber and a regenerator
column. Flue gas from a cement plant and the natural gas-fired boiler used to generate steam for solvent
regeneration for the capture plant, after scrubbing SO2 and cooling the flue gas, enters the absorber column
where 95% of the CO2 is captured. CO2 absorption occurs at low temperatures in the absorber column. The
amine in the RTI-NAS solvent reacts with CO2. The CO2-rich solvent is regenerated in the regenerator
using LP steam from the natural gas boiler. During the solvent regeneration process, the solvent is heated,
and the reverse reactions predominate to release CO2. The CO2 released in this process at 65.3 psia (4.5
bar), is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bar) and sent for sequestration.
The process is water-balanced by operating the water wash temperatures close to the dew point temperature
of the gas leaving the DCC, such that the total water entering the CO2 capture process is balanced by the
total water leaving with the treated gas and the CO2-product gas. There is no makeup water required for the
NAS process.
RTI’S NAS technology has the potential to reduce the thermal regeneration energy demand to ~2.1-2.3
GJ/t-CO2 captured. Key attributes of the RTI-NAS solvent are:
 • Low energy required for solvent regeneration resulting in low steam consumption
 • High working capacity resulting in low liquid/gas ratio
 • High solvent thermal and oxidative stability resulting in longer service life
 • Low corrosivity
A single train is needed to treat the flue gas from the cement plant. The process equipment comprises an
integrated direct contact cooler (DCC) and a SO2 polisher, a flue gas blower, the absorption column with
the intercoolers, an advanced emissions control technology and acid wash to control amine emissions, the
regeneration column, associated pumps, storage and receiver vessels, and a CO2 drying and compression
train.

Figure 11: Schematic of the RTI CO2 Capture Plant

 25

The CO₂ capture facility is organized into three main process units, encompassing both Inside Battery
Limits (ISBL) and Outside Battery Limits (OSBL) areas.
 • ISBL includes the core process systems responsible for CO₂ separation, solvent regeneration, and
 product gas compression.
 • OSBL includes supporting utilities, chemical storage, wastewater treatment, and systems required
 for safe and continuous operation of the ISBL.

The three major process units are:
 • Unit 01 – CO₂ Capture (ISBL): Flue gas conditioning, solvent absorption, and regeneration

 • Unit 02 – CO₂ Compression and Polishing (ISBL): Multi-stage compression, dehydration, and final
 conditioning
 • Unit 03 – Utility and Offsite Systems (OSBL): Steam generation, water treatment, chemical
 storage, cooling systems, air/nitrogen, and effluent handling
To clearly delineate project scope and interfaces with the host site, the battery limits and system boundaries
are defined in the following section.

2.4.2 Battery Limits and System Boundaries Process:
This section defines the physical and operational boundaries between the CO₂ capture facility and the host
site, identifying all points of interface for utilities, raw materials, products, and effluents. These boundaries
help establish:
 • Ownership and responsibility

 27

 • Integration requirements
 • Utility provisioning assumptions
The battery limits are divided into ISBL and OSBL areas, with detailed stream parameters summarized in
Appendix B.7 – Battery Limit Summary.
Inside Battery Limits (ISBL)
The ISBL encompasses all equipment, piping, and controls required for:
 • Flue gas conditioning and CO₂ absorption (Unit 01)
 • Solvent regeneration and heat recovery in the lean-rich heat exchanger
 • CO₂ compression, dehydration, and final polishing (Unit 02)

ISBL battery limits terminate at the following tie-in points:
 • Flue gas inlet from the host plant
 • CO₂ product outlet, ready for pipeline delivery or storage
 • Utility tie-ins: steam, electricity, water, instrument air, and nitrogen
Outside Battery Limits (OSBL)
The OSBL includes all systems that support ISBL operations, including:
 • Unit 11 – Water Systems: Raw, potable, and demineralized water, with limited makeup water from
 the host and internal recycling
 • Unit 12 – Boiler and Steam System: Powered by natural gas
 • Unit 14 – Solvent and Chemical Storage
 • Unit 15 – Cooling Water System: Hybrid cooling towers designed for reduced water use
 • Unit 16 – Instrument Air and Nitrogen Supply
 • Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
 • Unit 20 – Stack and Emissions Relief System
 • Unit 22 – Utility Header and Distribution

Key OSBL interfaces include:
 • Water supply point from host (limited)
 • Electricity supply from grid (not from host)
 • Natural gas supply for steam generation
 • Effluent discharge point to wastewater treatment
 • Material receiving points for bulk chemicals and solvents
Host Site Interfaces
To minimize integration risk and ensure project independence, the following operational interfaces are
clearly defined:
 • Flue gas feed: Delivered at the ISBL battery limit
 • CO₂ product: Sent to pipeline or storage at defined export point
 • Steam is self-generated on-site. Electricity is supplied from the grid, and the host plant provides a
 limited amount of water.
 • Operations: The CO₂ capture facility is operated by dedicated personnel, separate from host plant
 staff

 28

2.4.3 Major Process Blocks (ISBL):
Each unit described in this section is summarized for clarity and overview purposes. Process descriptions
for each main block are provided in the Appendix B.1.

2.4.3.1 Direct Contact Cooler
The combined flue gas from Kiln 1, Kiln 2, and the natural gas-fired boiler, at 272 F and 14.36 psia, is
routed to the Feed Direct Contact Cooler (V-0111), where it is cooled and conditioned prior to CO₂
absorption. In this unit, the gas stream is contacted with a caustic solution, which facilitates the removal of
sulfur dioxide (SO₂). The water in the recirculation loop passes through the DCC Circulation Filter (F-
0111), the Feed Scrubber Wash Air Cooler (E-0112), and the Feed Scrubber Wash Cooler (E-0111) before
returning to the top of the DCC. During the cooling process, excess water vapor condenses from the flue
gas. This condensate is collected and sent to the Wastewater Treatment Plant (WWTP), where it is treated
and reused elsewhere in the process. The combination of gas cooling and moisture removal significantly
reduces the specific volume of the flue gas, thereby lowering the power demand of the downstream Flue
Gas Blowers (K-0111 A/B). The cooled and conditioned flue gas exits the top of the DCC and is conveyed
by the blowers to the Absorber Column (C-0121) for CO₂ capture.

2.4.3.2 Absorber Column
The Absorber Column (C-0121) is a vertically packed, integrated unit designed for CO₂ removal from flue
gas. It consists of three main sections: the CO₂ absorption section, followed by a water wash section and an
acid wash section for downstream treatment. Cooled flue gas from the Flue Gas Blowers (K-0111 A/B)
enters the bottom of the absorber and flows upward in a counter-current direction to the lean amine solvent,
which is introduced at the top of the CO₂ absorption section. As the gas rises through the packed Absorption
beds, CO₂ is absorbed into the solvent. Interstage cooling is applied between the beds to remove the heat of
absorption and maintain optimal solvent loading efficiency. The partially loaded solvent is collected in the
Absorber Interstage Surge Tanks (T-0121, T-0122), from which it is circulated by the Absorber Interstage
Circulation Pumps (P-0121 A/B/C and P-0122 A/B/C) through the Absorber Interstage Air Coolers (E-
0121, E-0122). These air coolers reduce the solvent temperature before it is reintroduced into the column
above the next packed stage. At the bottom of the column, the CO₂-rich solvent is collected and discharged
by the Rich Solvent Pumps (P-0124 A/B/C). The treated flue gas, now with significantly reduced CO₂
content, continues upward into the water wash and acid wash sections for final cleanup before exiting the
absorber.

2.4.3.3 Absorber Wash Sections and Acid Wash
The upper section of the Absorber Column (C-0121) is designed to minimize amine emissions through a
multi-stage gas cleaning sequence. This section consists of: two water wash sections, an activated carbon
bed located between the two wash sections and a final acid wash section for polishing.
First Water Wash Section - The first water wash section is located directly above the CO₂ absorption zone.
It captures the bulk of entrained amines from the treated flue gas through contact with a circulating water
stream. The amine-rich condensate from this section is returned to the absorber sump for recovery and
reuse.

 29

Activated Carbon Bed - The water from the second wash section is routed through this carbon bed in a
recirculation loop, effectively removing dissolved amines from the system. The carbon bed is regenerated
with steam, which desorbs the captured amines. The resulting steam condensate and recovered amines are
cooled via the Activated Carbon Bed Cooler (E-0133) and returned to the absorber sump for reuse.
Second Water Wash Section - The second water wash section, supplied with water purified by the activated
carbon bed, achieves deeper scrubbing of the treated gas. The improved water quality enhances amine
removal efficiency and reduces solvent losses.
Acid Wash Section - The final stage in the gas treatment sequence is the acid wash section, where the flue
gas contacts sulfuric acid-dosed wash water. This step chemically neutralizes any remaining amine vapors
via acid-base reaction. The pH of the acid wash is maintained to ensure effective amine removal.
After this final polishing step, the fully treated flue gas is routed to the stack (Unit 20) for atmospheric
discharge, compliant with environmental emissions limits.

2.4.3.4 Lean/Rich Solvent Exchanger
CO₂-lean solvent from the Regenerator Column (C-0151) is routed through the Lean/Rich Solvent Heat
Exchanger (E-0141), where it transfers heat to the incoming CO₂-rich solvent from the Absorber, delivered
by the Rich Solvent Pumps (P-0124 A/B/C). This integration reduces reboiler duty and improves energy
efficiency. After heat exchange, the lean solvent flows to the Lean Solvent Surge Tank (T-0141), and from
there, it is cooled in the Lean Solvent Air Cooler (E-0142) before being sent to the top of the Absorber
Column (C-0121). During normal operation, approximately 10% of the lean solvent flow is diverted
through the Particulate Filter (F-0141) and Carbon Filter (F-0142) to remove solids and degradation
products, maintaining solvent quality. The filtered stream is then recombined with the main flow upstream
of the absorber.

2.4.3.5 Regenerator
A slipstream of CO₂-rich solvent from the Absorber Column enters the upper packing stage of the
Regeneration Column (C-0151), along with reflux from the column top. The remaining CO₂-rich solvent
flows through the Lean/Rich Heat Exchanger (E-0141) and enters the lower packing stage of the
Regeneration Column. The regenerated CO₂-lean solvent from the column bottom is partially sent to the
Lean Solvent Surge Tank (T-0141) via the Lean/Rich Heat Exchanger, while the remainder is sent to the
Regenerator Reboilers (E-0152 A/B/C/D), which use LP steam for solvent regeneration. Vapors from the
reboilers return to the column, and CO₂ gas from the column top is cooled in the Regenerator Overhead
Condenser (E-0151) before entering the Condensate Drum (V-0151). Captured CO₂ is sent to the Unit 02
CO₂ Compressor Package, while reflux is recycled to the column. In the event of downstream upsets, vented
gas is routed to the stack.

2.4.3.6 CO2 Compression, Dehydration and Polishing
The CO₂ Compressor Package (K-0211) compresses CO₂ from the Unit 01 Regenerator Column and
integrates polishing and TEG dehydration to meet pipeline specifications. The system consists of a five-
stage integrally geared compressor with heat exchangers and suction/knock-out drums. Heat from the
intercoolers is recovered using boiler feedwater (BFW), generating LP steam for the utility header.

 30

Intermediate process steps include O₂ Polishing and TEG Dehydration between stages 3 and 4 of
compression.
The O₂ Polishing Reactor (V-0221) reduces O₂ content in the CO₂ stream to <10 ppmv using a catalyst that
reacts with hydrogen. Hydrogen is supplied via electrolysis in the vendor-provided Electrolyzer Package
(A-0232), which uses demineralized water sourced from OSBL.
The TEG Dehydration Package (A-0221) removes moisture from the CO₂ stream, with TEG. The polished
and dehydrated CO₂ is returned to stage 4 of the compressor for final cooling and compression before being
delivered to the pipeline.

2.4.4 Major Process Blocks (OSBL):
This section provides a summary of the OSBL units that supply utilities to the CO₂ capture plant. Process
descriptions for each main block are provided in the Appendix B.1.

2.4.4.1 Unit 11 - Water Treatment Unit
The Water Treatment Unit supports plant operations by supplying raw, demineralized, and potable water
through three main systems:
 • Raw Water System: Receives treated wastewater and raw well water, stored in the Service Water
 Tank (T-1101). It supplies water to the service water header, cooling water system, and the
 Demineralized Water Package. The system includes fire protection pumps (P-1102 A/B) compliant
 with NFPA 20, with one diesel-driven and one motor-driven pump, and a jockey pump (P-1103
 A/B) for maintaining pressure during normal operation.
 • Demineralized Water System: Treats service water in the Demineralized Water Package (A-1101)
 to remove salts, reducing corrosion risk. The treated water is stored in the Demineralized Water
 Tank (T-1102) and distributed to the Deaerator, Electrolyzer Package. The system includes
 nitrogen blanketing and internal recycling for backwash and neutralization.
 • Potable Water System: Supplies potable water from an offsite municipal source, stored in the
 Potable Water Tank (T-1103) and pumped to users via the potable water header.

2.4.4.2 Unit 12 - Boiler and Steam System
Boiler and Steam System provides low-pressure (LP) steam to various plant users and includes a
 Deaerator system, boiler feedwater (BFW) treatment, and steam generation.
 • Boiler Feedwater System: Demineralized water from Unit 11 is chemically treated for oxygen
 scavenging and introduced into the Deaerator (V-1201), where LP steam and steam condensate
 strip dissolved gases. The deaerated BFW is pumped by BFW Pumps (P-1201 A/B) to the Natural
 Gas Boiler Package (A-1203) and other BFW users. Additional chemical injection is used for pH
 and scaling control.
 • Boiler System: The boiler produces LP steam using natural gas and BFW. The generated steam is
 routed to the LP steam header. Boiler blowdown is sent to the Blowdown Drum (V-1202), where
 flashed steam is recovered to the LP steam header, and liquid blowdown is sent to the wastewater
 treatment unit (Unit 18). Flue gases are directed to the Unit 01 Direct Contact Cooler.

 31

 • Condensate Recovery: Steam condensate from the LP system is collected in the Condensate Flash
 Drum (V-1203). Flashed gases are vented, and the recovered condensate is returned to the
 Deaerator via the Condensate Pumps (P-1203 A/B), improving water efficiency.

2.4.4.3 Unit 14 – Solvent and Chemical Storage Utilities
Solvent and Chemical Storage Utilities provides storage, transfer, and handling of solvents and key
chemicals required for plant operation. It includes three main subsystems:
 • Solvent Storage System: Amine solvent and formulated solvent are delivered by truck and stored
 in the Solvent Storage Tank (T-1401) and Solvent Sump (V-1401), respectively. Both vessels are
 nitrogen-blanketed using backpressure regulators. Solvent is filtered and transferred via Solvent
 Transfer Pumps (P-1401 A/B and P-1402) to the Lean Solvent Surge Tank in Unit 01. Internal
 solvent drains also feed into the Solvent Sump for reuse.
 • Caustic Storage System:
 Caustic solution, delivered by truck, is stored in the Caustic Storage Tank (T-1402) and
 transferred via Caustic Transfer Pumps (P-1403 A/B) to the Unit 01 Direct Contact Cooler for
 SO₂ neutralization. The tank includes provisions for safe venting.
 • Sulfuric Acid Storage System:
 Sulfuric acid is trucked in and stored in the Sulfuric Acid Storage Tank (T-1403), then pumped
 by Sulfuric Acid Transfer Pumps (P-1404 A/B) to the acid wash section of the Unit 01 Absorber
 Column.

2.4.4.4 Unit 15 – Cooling Water System
Cooling Water System consists of a vendor-supplied Cooling Water Package (A-1501), featuring a cooling
tower that uses makeup water from the Unit 11 Service Water Tank. The cooled water is distributed via the
utility cooling water header to various users across the facility. Blowdown from the cooling system is routed
to Unit 18 for wastewater treatment.

2.4.4.5 Unit 16 – Air and Nitrogen Systems
Air and Nitrogen Systems provides compressed air and nitrogen gas for plant operation and safety
functions. It includes two subsystems:
 • Air Compressor System:
Compressed air is generated by two vendor-supplied packages: the Primary Air Compressor (K-1601) and
Secondary Air Compressor (K-1602), managed by a split header pressure control system. The Secondary
Compressor automatically starts if header pressure drops below a setpoint. Air is stored in the Air Receiver
(V-1601), which also serves as an emergency backup for instrument air. The compressed air is then dried
via the Instrument Air Dryer Package (A-1602) before distribution to the plant and instrument air headers.
 • Nitrogen Gas System:
Nitrogen is produced by the Nitrogen Generation Skid (A-1601) and supplied to high-pressure users at 145
psia. A pressure control valve reduces pressure to 45 psia for low-pressure nitrogen users. Nitrogen is used
for inerting and blanketing applications throughout the facility.

 32

2.4.4.6 Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
Unit 18 treats wastewater from both ISBL and OSBL sources using a vendor-supplied Wastewater
Treatment Package (A-1801). The system handles various streams, including:
 • Boiler blowdown (Unit 12),
 • Demineralizer waste and backwash (Unit 11),
 • Cooling water blowdown (Unit 15),
 • Acid wash effluent (Unit 01),
 • Process water from the Direct Contact Cooler,
 • Electrolyzer blowdown (Unit 02),
 • Knock Out Drum aqueous phase (Unit 20),
 • General contact sewer drains.
Treated water is either recycled to the Unit 11 Service Water Tank (T-1101) or discharged offsite at
battery limits.

2.4.4.7 Unit 20 – Stack
Unit 20 manages non-routine gas releases through a dedicated stack system. Relief and depressurization
gases from across the facility are routed via the main relief header to the Knock Out Drum (V-2001), which
prevents liquid carryover.
Separated gases flow to the Stack (A-2001), which is connected to the treated flue gas outlet from the Unit
01 Absorber Column—making the Absorber stack the sole emission point. A Continuous Emissions
Monitoring System (CEMS) at this location tracks emissions from both Unit 01 and Unit 20. During Unit
02 upsets, captured CO₂ is also vented via this stack.
Aqueous liquids from the KO Drum are intermittently pumped to Unit 18 for treatment, based on level
control.

2.5 Operating and Contracting Strategy
The ownership and operational arrangements for the CO₂ capture facility are currently under evaluation and
have not been finalized. The following outlines the current considerations and assumptions used for the
purposes of the Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) study.
Ownership and Operations
  • For the purposes of this FEED, the facility has been modeled as a stand-alone plant operated by a
 dedicated team, separate from host plant personnel.
Utility and Water Supply
 • The capture facility’s utilities are largely self-supplied.
 • Steam will be generated on-site using a dedicated natural gas-fired boiler.
 • Electric power will be supplied from the grid, independent of the host site.
 • Water supply is partially provided by the host site.
 • The majority of process water is recycled within the capture facility to minimize fresh water
 usage.
 • A hybrid cooling system is incorporated to optimize water consumption and reduce reliance on
 makeup water.

 33

Operations and Staffing
 • The capture plant is assumed to operate independently of the host facility.
 • A dedicated operations and maintenance (O&M) team will be responsible for day-to-day
 operation, monitoring, and maintenance.
 • Staffing assumptions for the O&M team are reflected in the operating cost estimates
Commercial and Contractual Considerations
 • Final decisions on ownership, O&M contracting, and commercial agreements (e.g., site lease,
 utility connections) will need to be addressed prior to detailed engineering or final investment
 decision (FID).
 • The FEED package is designed to support a range of ownership models, including:
 o Independent third-party owner/operator model
 o Special purpose vehicle (SPV) structure
 o Build-Own-Operate-Transfer (BOOT) arrangements

 34

3 Design Basis and Scope Definition
The project design basis is provided in Appendix A1. Details of the site-specific conditions and
environmental requirements are provided in this section.

3.1 Site Characteristics
The Balcones Plant is in a hot humid subtropical region and has limited water availability.

The proposed location for the CO2 capture plant is on a greenfield plot, South of the New Braunfels facility,
with a plot plan of about 15 acres. Additional Cemex property is available to the south and west of the plot
plan for potential laydown areas. Figure 13 shows an aerial view of the Balcones plant and Figure
14 shows the plant with the proposed plot area for the capture plant across the road from the cement plant.
The flue gas ducting will need to go over the rail spurs and the road. Figure 14 provides another
view of the proposed plot.

Figure 13: Image of the Balcones plant showing the plot area for the CO2 capture plant

 35

 Figure 14: Proposed Plot for the CO2 capture plant
3.2 Site Ambient Conditions
New Braunfels, TX, sits at an elevation of approximately 645 feet and experiences a humid subtropical
climate with hot summers and mild winters. Average temperatures range from lows of ~40°F in January to
highs near 97°F in July and August. Annual rainfall averages around 35 inches, with wetter months in
spring and fall. The region sees occasional frost but rarely experiences snow.

4 Design Challenges and Key Decisions
This section identifies the challenges and important design decisions incorporated in the FEED study.

4.1 Limited Water Supply
One of the process design challenges is the lack of raw water availability and its impact on cooling systems.
The Cemex Balcones Cement Plant is able to supply 191 GPM of raw water from the on-site wells. An
additional ~400 GPM of condensate from the Feed Direct Contact Cooler (DCC) is available for use as
cooling water. This presents some challenges as the CO2 capture process requires cooling of the solvent in
the absorber intercooler, as well as absorber wash columns and direct contact cooler to cool and condition
the flue gas. In addition, the CO2 compression process also requires intercooling between the compression
stages. The lack of cooling water availability was addressed by using hybrid cooling with air cooling for
most part and water-cooling for trim cooler in the DCC and the water wash for the treated gas leaving the
absorber.
The dry bulb temperature (DBT) for New Braunfels, TX for 2021-2022 is shown in Figure 15. The high
summer design temperature presents a challenge, as both air and water cooling are constrained by the
limitations of the log mean temperature difference (LMTD). Figure 16(a) shows the monthly mean
coincident wet bulb temperature (MCWBT) plotted against the ambient dry bulb temperature (DBT) for
New Braunfels, TX in 2022. The maximum MCWBT observed was 80°F, which, with an approach
temperature of 15°F, indicates that process streams could be cooled to approximately 95°F using water
cooling alone. However, due to limited water availability, a hybrid cooling strategy incorporating both wet
and dry cooling would be required. Figure 16(b) presents the DBT distribution and cumulative distribution
for the same year.

Figure 15: Dry Bulb Temperatures for 2021-2022

 40

 Figure 16: (a) ) Mean coincident wet bulb and dry bulb temperature distribution (2022) (b) DBT
 histogram and cumulative frequency (2022)

The facility was originally designed for the ASHRAE 99.6% dry bulb temperature of 106.7 °F, resulting in
high cooling requirements and increased equipment costs. To reduce cooling loads and associated capital
expenditures, alternative ambient air temperatures were evaluated for the design of the air coolers.
The CO₂ capture rate is affected by the temperatures of the absorber intercoolers and the trim cooler. For
the air-cooled heat exchangers, these temperatures are determined by the selected approach temperature
and the ambient dry bulb temperature. An approach temperature of 15°F (9.4°C) was used in the design.
A computationally efficient surrogate process model, based on a rate-based model using NAS technology
was developed to predict plant operation based on historical dry/wet bulb temperatures and kiln flue gas
data as inputs. Under design conditions the model predicted a 96.4% average capture rate. The results of
the optimization study using the 80°F (26.7 ℃) design temperature and the 2021–2023 hourly data are
shown in Figure 15.

 41

Figure 17: Results of the optimization study depicting the mean capture rate of 95% using the 80 ℉ (26.7
 ℃) design temperature for the air coolers.
