5 Discipline Design Summaries

5.1 Process Engineering
This section presents the process engineering basis and associated documentation developed for the CO₂
capture facility. It includes the design philosophies, process configuration, operating strategies, safety
systems, and supporting documentation required to define the technical scope for both ISBL (Inside Battery
Limits) and OSBL (Outside Battery Limits) systems.
The process design is centered around a water-lean amine-based CO₂ capture system, followed by
compression, dehydration, and polishing units. Utility systems including water treatment, cooling, air
systems, chemical storage, and nitrogen supply are captured in the OSBL scope.

5.1.1 Design Decisions

5.1.1.1 Process Design Rate
One of the challenges in the process design was determining the normal and maximum operating cases for
the facility due to the variability in operating data provided for the Cemex Balcones Cement Plant. PI data
for the Cemex site depicted daily fluctuations in plant operations and further variability from Kilns 1 and 2
tripping.
The facility is required to capture > 95% of the CO2 produced in the cement plant kilns and natural gas
boiler, as mandated by the Department of Energy (DOE). Given this CO2 capture requirement and
variability in the PI data, it was challenging to select a design rate for the facility. Designing for the peak
PI data results in overdesigning the facility for most of the year, which further increases plot footprint and
cooling system requirements. However, designing for the average PI data results in under capturing when
the kilns are operating at instantaneous, above average rates. As a result, selecting the facility’s design rate
is a balance of managing peak operating rates without oversizing equipment.
Cemex provided hourly-averaged CEMS data for kiln #1 and kiln #2 over the period from July 2021 to
June 2023. The data included flue gas composition, temperature and pressure conditions and flowrates. The
data revealed significant variability in the flue gas flow rates for each kiln, which posed challenges in sizing
the CO2 capture plant. This variability in operating data required careful consideration to determine the
normal and maximum operating cases for the facility at the Cemex Balcones Plant. In this FEED study,
RTI with support from KBR selected the normal and maximum operating cases that resulted in appropriate
design conditions for equipment sizing and achieved >95% CO2 capture on an annual basis. It was
determined that the capture plant should be designed to address the 90th percentile of the total flow as the
design "max" case, while the "mean" or 50th percentile value would represent the "normal" case. All
equipment was designed to accommodate the design "max" case conditions, and a "normal case" was
adopted to provide data for the "average conditions."
The design "max" case led to the plant being sized to capture 2.4 MTPA of CO2 emissions from both the
kiln and the captive natural gas boiler. The nominal capture capacity is 86%, which, when combined with
the 90% plant availability, results in an overall capacity factor of 74%.

 45

5.1.1.2 Use of Hybrid Cooling
The Balcones Plant is in a hot humid subtropical region and has limited water availability The amount of
water recovered from the Direct Contact Cooler (DCC) and the supply from the Balcones Cement Plant is
able to supply 20% of the requirement for cooling water makeup. Following an evaluation of various
cooling options, a hybrid system combining air cooling and water cooling was selected. Water cooling is
applied in the absorber wash section and DCC trim cooler, while air coolers—sized for 80°F ambient
temperature—handles the majority of the heat rejection. The use of alternative cooling systems to
compensate for limited water availability, has an impact on the overall cost of CO₂ capture, as detailed in
the cost analysis section of this report.

5.1.1.3 Choice of Compressor Type
Due to limited site water availability, an integrally geared CO₂ compressor with heat integration was
selected in place of a conventional water-cooled unit. In this configuration, the heat of compression is
recovered and used to raise low-pressure steam (LPS), thereby eliminating the need for a dedicated cooling
water system. While the heat-integrated compressor requires approximately 33% higher power than a
conventional design, it significantly reduces cooling water demand. Compressor power requirements can
be optimized by increasing the number of compressor stages with accompanying intercooling. In
collaboration with the compressor suppliers, it is recommended that this aspect be further evaluated to
determine the optimum number of stages and intercoolers.

5.1.1.4 Choice Deoxygenation and Dehydration
To meet pipeline CO2 purity specifications, the design incorporates a regenerable oxygen adsorbent system
to remove trace oxygen impurities from the CO2 product gas. The adsorbent is periodically regenerated
using hydrogen produced by the electrolyzer. In parallel, triethylene glycol (TEG) dehydration is employed
to control moisture content and ensure that the treated CO2 meets water specification limits for pipeline
transport.

5.1.1.5 Particulate Management and Filtration
The feed flue gas streams contain trace amounts of particulate matter that must be removed prior to
downstream processing. Based on characterization data indicating low particulate loading, a gas filter
upstream of the DCC was not included in the design, but instead, particulate removal is achieved through
the Feed Scrubber Circulation Filter. The filter is an automatic self-cleaning unit installed on the process
water discharge from the DCC bottoms. The circulation filter receives process water from the Feed
Scrubber Wash Circulation Pump, removes entrained particulate matter, and returns the filtered water after
cooling to the DCC. A small slipstream from the pump discharge is sent to Wastewater Treatment Plant
(WWTP). This configuration provides reliable removal of residual particulates while minimizing
equipment count and pressure drop in the flue gas path.
A few other filter designs were considered during the FEED study but ultimately not pursued for the reasons
described below.
 1. Locating the filter on the flue gas overhead from the DCC, upstream of the Flue Gas Blower.
 Potential disadvantages of this design are:
 • The filter will need to be large to handle the flue gas flow rate and be designed for minimal
 pressure drop.

 46

 • The filter may need to be designed as a bag house filter, which is already employed by the
 cement plant so a second bag house filter may provide minimal benefit.
 2. Designing the filter as a cartridge filter instead of an automatic self-cleaning filter. Initial
 discussions with vendors reviewed the following cartridge filter designs.
 • A single filter housing containing multiple cartridge filters in parallel designed to treat the
 full process water stream from the DCC. This design required frequent filter changeouts
 and several filters in parallel and therefore was considered an impractical design.
 • Another design option located the cartridge filter on a slipstream (e.g., 10%) of the DCC
 bottoms process water instead of treating the full stream. While this option reduces the
 number of filters, there are potential clogging issues in the downstream plate and frame
 exchanger if the filter does not adequately remove solids to meet the exchanger’s
 particulate matter limitations.
The final option using an automatic self-cleaning filter was selected as the most suitable design since this
type of filter can treat the full DCC process water stream. Although the collection efficiency is less than
that of a cartridge filter, the automatic self-cleaning filter design has a larger capacity and requires less
parallel equipment so it was considered the preferred design option.

5.1.2 Process Engineering Summary
Process Flow Diagrams (PFDs) were developed for the major CO₂ capture systems, including the Direct
Contact Cooler (DCC), Absorber, and Regenerator. Utility Flow Diagrams (UFDs) were also prepared for
the Outside Battery Limit (OSBL) systems to capture supporting infrastructure such as water, steam,
nitrogen, and chemical handling systems. The full set of PFDs and UFDs is included in Appendices B.3
and B.5, respectively.
Heat and Material Balances (HMBs) for the Inside Battery Limit (ISBL) systems were developed using
RTI’s process simulation model. Two design cases were evaluated: the Normal Case and the Max Case.
These HMBs were subsequently updated by KBR to reflect refined estimates of pressure drops and
equipment performance. The updated ISBL HMBs are provided in Appendix B.4. Utility and mass
balances for the OSBL systems were also developed by KBR and are presented in Appendix B.6, capturing
utility consumption, generation, and recycling rates across the facility.
A ISBL/OSBL Equipment List was also developed to define the major process and utility equipment
identified in the FEED scope. This list reflects the equipment associated with both ISBL process units and
OSBL utility systems, as shown in the PFDs and UFDs. The complete equipment lists are provided in
Appendix B.8 (ISBL) and Appendix B.9 (OSBL).
A summary of utility demands, design cases, and supply sources is included in Appendix B.10 – Utility
Summary, which supports sizing and design of the OSBL utility systems.
Operating requirements for catalysts and chemicals, are provided in Appendix B.11 – Catalyst and
Chemical Summary.

 47

5.1.3 Hazard and Operability Study/Process Hazard Analysis (HAZOP)

5.1.3.1 Introduction
An independent firm, Saltegra Consulting LLC, specializing in Process Hazard Analysis (PHA), was
selected to facilitate the PHA for the Balcones CO₂ Capture Plant. The PHA was held over a total of six
days, combining both in-person and remote sessions. The PHA team include representatives from RTI ,
KBR, SLB, Cemex as well as a facilitator and scribe familiar with the HAZOP methodology. The in-person
workshop was held at the Research Triangle Park, North Carolina on May 20 – 24, 2024 followed by remote
sessions on May 29, 2024. The analysis utilized both the HAZOP (Hazard and Operability Study) and
Checklist methodologies to systematically identify and evaluate potential hazards associated with the
design and operation of the CO₂ capture process.
The primary objectives of this review were to:
 • Identify events, which potentially have a significant impact on the safety of personnel and
 operations. These concerns included effects on surrounding populations as well as on operating
 personnel and equipment damage.
 • Assess the risk level associated with each safety hazard.
 • Suggest changes or further study to overcome the potential unsafe deviations with unacceptable
 risks.
 • Record the causes, risk level, and considerations of the safety hazards in a report and submit this
 report to location management.
The HAZOP process followed a node-based approach, using preliminary Piping and Instrumentation
Diagrams (P&IDs) as the basis for identifying logical system divisions. Each node typically corresponded
to a major unit operation, subsystem, or piping section where distinct flow, pressure, or control behaviors
could occur. The nodes covered both ISBL and OSBL systems, including the absorber, regenerator,
compression train, and utility tie-ins.

5.1.3.2 PHA Scope
The PHA was conducted as part of the FEED phase to identify and evaluate potential process safety hazards
associated with the proposed facility design. The scope of the assessment included:
 • All process units, equipment, and associated systems within the defined FEED battery limits.
 • Normal operating conditions as well as non-routine modes such as startup, shutdown (normal and
 emergency), maintenance, and transient conditions.
 • Interfaces with existing facilities where tie-ins or operational dependencies are present.
 • Potential loss of critical utilities, including firewater, steam, nitrogen, instrument air, and power.
 • Instrumentation failure modes and their contribution to potential process upsets or hazardous
 conditions.
 • External event impacts, such as high ambient temperatures, seismic events, or other natural
 hazards relevant to the region or site conditions.
 • Review of relevant historical incidents from similar facilities or industry sources.

In addition to standard HAZOP analysis, the PHA also included the following supplemental evaluations:
 • Human Factors Checklist – assessing working conditions and potential human error risks.
 • Facility Siting Checklist – evaluating equipment placement and layout against applicable safety
 standards and siting criteria.

 48

 • External Events Checklist – considering exposure to and resilience against natural or external
 hazards.
The analysis addressed potential consequences to personnel safety, public health, and equipment integrity,
assuming failure of safeguards for consequence development purposes. A qualitative assessment of existing
or proposed safeguards was carried out for each identified scenario.
The PHA was organized around 28 process nodes, defined according to system functionality and process
boundaries.

5.1.3.3 HAZOP Methodology
The PHA was conducted using the HAZOP Guideword methodology, supplemented with What-if analysis
for general issues, to ensure comprehensive hazard identification and risk analysis (HIRA).
The evaluation included:
 • Application of standard HAZOP deviations such as No/Low Flow, High Flow, Reverse Flow,
 Misdirected Flow, Low/High Level, Pressure, Temperature, and Contamination.
 • Consideration of special deviations relevant to abnormal or transitional operations, including
 startup, shutdown (planned and emergency), maintenance, and transient conditions.
 • Review of existing or proposed engineering and administrative safeguards for each deviation
 scenario.
 • Evaluation of potential failure modes, including instrumentation malfunction, utility loss, and
 external events (e.g., extreme weather, seismic activity).
 • Use of supplemental checklists to address broader risk considerations:
 o Human Factors – Evaluation of ergonomic, procedural, and cognitive risks.
 o Facility Siting – Assessment of hazard proximity, equipment spacing, and layout-related
 risks.
 o External Events – Analysis of resilience to site-specific natural hazards.

Possible causes for each deviation were identified, followed by an assessment of the worst credible
consequences, assuming failure of safeguards. The team qualitatively assessed the severity and likelihood
of each consequence using a project-specific risk matrix. Existing safeguards were reviewed for adequacy
and effectiveness and a risk ranking was assigned based on the severity and likelihood. Where risks were
deemed unacceptable or where safeguards could be enhanced, recommendations were developed. This

 49

5.1.5 Isolation Philosophy
An Isolation Philosophy has been developed to guide the specification and placement of isolation devices
across process and utility systems. It provides the design basis for isolating equipment or plant sections to
ensure safe maintenance, inspection, commissioning, and operational activities. The philosophy applies to
both ISBL and OSBL systems and is consistent with industry standards for safe access and plant
segregation.
The full Isolation Philosophy is provided in Appendix B.13.

5.1.6 Heat Tracing and Winterization Philosophy
A Heat Tracing and Winterization Philosophy was developed to ensure that process and utility systems are
adequately protected against freezing conditions and meet operability requirements under site-specific
ambient conditions. The philosophy defines the criteria for identifying lines and equipment requiring heat
tracing (electrical or steam) and insulation, as well as guidelines for winter operation and maintenance.
The complete heat tracing and winterization philosophy document is provided in Appendix B.14.

5.1.7 Environmental Permitting
Cemex identified all permits expected to be required for construction and operation of the CO2 capture
plant. Since this is a FEED study, the permitting process was not initiated and no regulator organizations
were contacted. Instead, all permitting concerns were evaluated, and a comprehensive list was created with
the required permit and the permitting agency. The list of permits/approvals expected is provided in Table
7.
Under the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) Greenhouse Gas Reporting Program (GHGRP),
cement plants are required to report their total CO₂ emissions before accounting for any reductions achieved
through carbon capture and storage (CCS) or other mitigation technologies. This requirement is established
under 40 CFR Part 98, Subpart H – Cement Production. The EPA has proposed amendments to this subpart
as part of an ongoing rulemaking process to reconsider certain GHGRP requirements; however, until a final
rule is issued, facilities must continue to report gross CO₂ emissions in accordance with the existing
regulation. Meetings were held with KBR and Cemex environmental team to discuss relocation of the
CEMS upstream of the tie-in points for the two stacks to allow Cemex to monitor the emissions from the
cement kilns with the CO2 capture unit running, as required by under the National Emission Standards for
Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) set by the U.S. Each kiln needs to be monitored separately as they
had different emission limits. The CEMS needs to be installed at an accessible location where the pollutant
concentration or emission rate measurements are directly representative of the total emissions, and the
measurement location should be (1) at least two equivalent diameters downstream from the nearest control
device, the point of pollutant generation, or other point at which a change in the pollutant concentration or
emission rate may occur and (2) at least a half equivalent diameter upstream from the effluent exhaust or
control device. The option of a combined CEMS after the two ducts join into a single duct leading to the
capture plant was discarded due to requirement to monitor each kiln individually. The team reviewed
various locations and identified a suitable location in the duct work from each of the kiln to the central duct
before the flue gas from the natural gas boiler is added. This location is consistent with the EPA
requirements listed above. Having a dedicated CEMS for each kiln meets the requirement for each kiln
being monitored separately. Other options considered was to move the CEMS from the current location to

 53

the new location but was discarded as it would present challenges to monitor the plant emissions when the
CO2 capture plant was not running Instead, it was decided to have a valve in the duct between the main
baghouse and the present stack so that we can go back to the main stack when the capture plant is down.
This allowed Cemex to measure the plant emissions both with and without CCS at any given time.

 54

5.2 Mechanical Engineering

5.2.1 Design Decisions
The process design challenges manifested themselves in the mechanical equipment design. Of all the
process design challenges mentioned above, the water availability and process design rates had considerable
influence on equipment design / selections which are highlighted below.
The mechanical equipment costs represent approximately 45% of the Total Direct Costs, so decisions on
process design margin, design rates, site constraints such as cooling water all contributed to increased
equipment costs.

5.2.1.1 Air Cooler Mechanical Options Analysis
Due to the limited availability of cooling water, this project employed the use of air-cooled heat exchangers
(ACHE) as noted above in the process cooling system section. This cooling system was a hybrid of two
types of heat exchangers. Both air cooled heat exchangers and gasketed plate and frame exchangers were
required for complete plant cooling.
The design basis of the ACHE was to utilize 1” tubes (welded 316 SS) with multiple tube passes to achieve
the highest heat transfer coefficients. ACHE fans are manual adjustable pitch, preset at the factory. The
maximum ambient temperature for sizing the air-cooled exchangers was set at 80°F, representing a design
compromise between equipment sizing constraints and the ability to achieve a 95% annual average CO₂
capture rate. This approach balances thermal performance with capital cost and footprint, while accounting
for typical site temperature profiles.
Each ACHE is provided with header walkways and ladders for access and maintenance of the heat
exchanger tube bundle. Actuated Louvers were provided as an option for winter and turn down cases to aid
in maintaining the process temperature. Each fan motor is variable frequency drive (VFD) compatible for
additional temperature control.
Pressure drop through each exchanger was evaluated in tandem with associated pumps or applicable
compressor. This was to ensure sufficient head and horsepower in the pump was available while
maintaining and economical design. Refer to pump selection philosophy below for additional details.
This ACHE design may be improved by increasing the tube diameter up to 1.5” diameter, additional tube
rows(layers), and increasing tube passes. By applying these modifications, the number of air-cooled heat
exchanger bays and overall horsepower consumption would be reduced.

5.2.1.2 Regenerator Reboiler Design
NAS is water-lean and does not require stripping steam for regeneration. Due to its low water content,
minimal steam is generated during regeneration, making a traditional thermosyphon reboiler unsuitable. To
address this, a once-through forced recirculation reboiler was selected. The solvent is withdrawn from the
bottom section of the regenerator packing and flows to the reboiler by gravity. The system relies on an
elevation difference between the solvent withdrawal point and the reboiler return connection to the column.
This elevation provides sufficient hydrostatic head to overcome pressure drops across the reboiler and
ensure continuous circulation without the need for a pump.

 55

The Regenerator Reboiler’s large heat duty (685 MMBTU/hr) poses a challenge because designing the
reboiler as a traditional shell and tube exchanger requires 8 parallel exchangers and results in a large plot
footprint.
To reduce plot footprint and number of equipment, a welded plate and frame style exchanger was
considered as an alternate design. Specifically, this project selected the Alfa Laval Compabloc exchanger,
of which only 4 parallel units are needed.
The welded plate and frame exchanger design offers several advantages over the shell and tube exchanger
design, including:
 • High heat transfer rates and lower liquid hold-up.
 • Reduced footprint and maintenance activities since shell and tube exchangers require additional
 plot space for pulling out tube bundles axially for maintenance and replacement.
 • High shear rates, which reduce fouling, and high turbulence, which allows for effective and
 economical chemical cleaning in place (CIP).
 • Easy accessibility of side panels, which can be removed for visual inspection and high-pressure
 wash cleaning, and disassembly of the plate pack, which can be immersed in chemical for cleaning.

5.2.1.3 Pump Selection Philosophy
Early in the design process, API 610 was identified as the most appropriate construction standard for pumps
operating within the unit battery limits. This decision was then later supported by the pump suppliers who
indicated limited references for pumps in amine service not bult to API 610. ANSI/ASME constructed
pumps were also considered acceptable for services inside and outside the battery limits provided, the
process conditions were compatible with available hydraulic capabilities; however, they were not used
within this FEED.
The quantity of pumps for each service was determined based on rated capacity and available net positive
suction head (NPSH). Minimum-flow (recycle) controllers were incorporated across services to
accommodate the wide capacity range between normal and rated flow conditions. Allowable operating
ranges and final selection will be confirmed by pump vendors during detailed design.

5.2.1.4 Absorber And DCC Design
The Absorber Column baseline design was based upon a concrete structure which has a long history of
success in the Flue Gas Desulfurization industry and more recently CO2 capture industry. KBR worked
closely with leading manufactures of large Absorber and DCCs to validate their design offerings technically
for the specific operating conditions, column footprint and height requirements.
Alternative materials of construction were evaluated and compared to the concrete construction with liner
plate. Steel designs were found to be technically viable and had comparable budgetary cost estimates to the
concrete option with liner plate. However, further investigation is recommended to establish a more
definitive design baseline and cost comparison between concrete and steel construction options.

5.2.1.5 Cement Plant Flue Gas Mechanical Tie-In
A new flue gas duct would be installed to direct exhaust gas from Kiln #1 and Kiln #2 to the CCS. The
existing flue gas duct design layouts from Kiln #1 and Kiln #2 were dramatically different which resulted
in developing custom tie-in strategies for each kiln stack tie-ins. The underlying design goal was to isolate
capture plant disruptions from having an impact on the reliability of the cement plant and vice versa.

 56

 Figure 21: Snapshot of the 3D model showing layout of major equipment, flue gas duct routing, and the
 air coolers selected due to site water limitations

  Figure 22: 3D model view with integrated scan of the existing cement plant, showing the flue gas duct
 routing over the rail spurs and Wald Road.

5.2.3 Mechanical Equipment Summary
The mechanical equipment summary provides an overview of the major equipment required to support the
CO₂ capture process, based on process and utility service requirements established during the FEED phase.
Equipment covered in this section includes vessels, heat exchangers, blowers, compressors, air coolers,
pumps, and key utility packages. Equipment sizing and specifications were developed using process
simulation outputs, heat and material balances, and vendor data where available.

 58

Key process equipment such as the Direct Contact Cooler (DCC), Absorber Column, and Regenerator
Column were sized based on the combined flow from the two kilns and the natural gas boiler flue gas,
targeting 95% CO₂ capture efficiency. Provision was made for up to a 50% turndown ratio to allow
operation with one kiln offline.
The redundancy philosophy for rotating equipment varied by type. An N+1 inline configuration was applied
to all critical pumps—including lean amine circulation, solvent transfer, and cooling water pumps—to
ensure uninterrupted operation during maintenance or equipment downtime. For example, three pumps each
sized at 50% capacity were specified to allow full-capacity operation with one pump offline. In contrast,
large equipment such as the gas blower and CO₂ compressors were designed without N+1 redundancy but
incorporated appropriate design margins and availability considerations.
The hybrid cooling strategy utilized mechanical draft air coolers for primary cooling duty, supplemented
by a water cooled exchanger utilizing a cooling tower where additional cooling water is required.
Steam-integrated CO₂ compressors were selected over electrically driven units to optimize energy
integration and reduce electrical demand.
The natural gas-fired boiler supplies low-pressure steam for solvent regeneration and other auxiliary loads.
Final equipment specifications and vendor selection will be completed during the detailed engineering
phase.

5.2.4 Equipment Layout and Access
The overall layout of the CO₂ capture facility was developed to optimize equipment placement for efficient
process flow and operational accessibility. A preliminary 2D plot plan, provided in Appendix C.1,
illustrates the spatial arrangement of major process equipment, pipe racks, and tie-in locations. This layout
guided the routing of piping and utility systems while considering sufficient space for maintenance and
equipment operation.
Complementing the plot plan, a 30% level AVEVA E3D 3D model was created to support spatial
coordination and provide a visual representation of equipment placement and interconnecting piping.
Snapshots from the 3D model, shown in Section 5.2.2. Figure 21 and Figure 22, highlight the positioning
of major equipment and ductwork routes, including key interfaces such as the flue gas duct crossing over
rail spurs and Wald Road.
The current level of detail provided by the plot plan and 3D model was sufficient to support the development
of AACE Class 3 level cost estimates. While detailed equipment access provisions and maintenance
clearances will be developed during detailed engineering, the current layout ensures adequate space for
operator access, equipment installation, and future maintenance activities.

5.2.5 Capital Spares for Estimation
A Capital Spares list has been developed by KBR and is included in Appendix C.2. This list includes
critical spare equipment, parts, components, or assemblies that are considered essential for ensuring the
continuity of plant operations. These items have been included as a line item within the mechanical scope
of the estimate.

 59

Capital spares do not include commissioning spares or 2-year operational/maintenance spares. Instead, the
list focuses solely on equipment and components that meet one or more of the following criteria:
 • Complete warehouse spares (e.g., pumps, motors, valves) identified through reliability modeling,
 value engineering, or risk analysis, but not required to be installed
 • Equipment expected to fail in a manner making repairs economically unattractive (i.e., repair
 costs exceed 50% of replacement cost)
 • Proprietary equipment with limited or sole-source suppliers, or long lead times mandated by
 licensors or design complexity (e.g., compressor rotors)
 • Customized or specially fabricated items requiring extended procurement or fabrication lead time
 • Critical items for which no installed spares are provided.

This list was compiled using vendor input, prior project experience, and equipment-specific risk analysis.
The final definition and procurement of capital spares will be refined during detailed engineering in
consultation with vendors and the client.

 60

5.3 Piping Engineering
The Piping Engineering discipline encompasses the detailed design, material selection, stress analysis, and
constructability considerations for all process and utility piping within the carbon capture facility. This
section summarizes the key piping deliverables developed during the FEED phase, providing an overview
of the piping system layout, specifications, and engineering analyses. Supporting documentation and
detailed design packages are provided in the associated appendices for reference.
The piping engineering scope for the carbon capture facility was developed to define the layout, material
selection, flexibility, and constructability of process and utility piping systems for both ISBL and OSBL
areas. Design development during the FEED phase adhered to applicable codes and standards (e.g., ASME
B31.3), ensuring safe, operable, and maintainable systems that support long-term reliability.
A preliminary plot plan layout was developed to define the spatial arrangement of major process equipment,
pipe racks, and tie-in locations. This guided the routing of piping systems and ensure adequate accessibility
for operations and maintenance. The plot plan is provided in Appendix C.1.
The plot plan layout underlying the piping routing logic is shown in Section 5.2.2, Figure 20. For spatial
context and visualization of duct routing, refer to Figures 12 and 13 in that same section. These views
illustrate the flue gas duct paths to the Direct Contact Cooler (DCC) and over external interfaces, including
the rail spurs and Wald Road.
A 30% 3D model of the facility was developed using AVEVA E3D. This model included major ISBL and
OSBL equipment, primary piping routes, pipe racks, and structural elements. It supported early-stage spatial
coordination, clash detection, and layout validation, and served as a visual reference for generating
preliminary isometric drawings and conducting the stress analysis. The model will be further developed
during detailed engineering phase to incorporate small-bore piping, secondary supports, and final
equipment arrangements.
Piping material selection was based on service type, pressure/temperature conditions, corrosion allowances,
and process fluid characteristics. An Index of Piping Material Classes and the corresponding Piping
Materials Specifications Binder were prepared to define the design basis for each class and support
consistent material selection across the facility.
To support procurement and detailed design, a consolidated Piping Line List was developed, identifying all
process and utility lines included in the FEED scope. A Piping Specialty Items List was also compiled to
document non-standard components such as strainers, hose stations, and expansion joints, which are critical
for performance, safety, and maintainability.
A Preliminary Stress Analysis was performed for selected critical lines to evaluate thermal expansion,
nozzle loads, and support requirements. The analysis was based on early routing and design conditions and
informed initial layout and support strategies. Additionally, a Piping Tie-In List was developed to define
the interface points between new systems and existing plant infrastructure. This list includes tie-in
descriptions, service, size, and associated shutdown or coordination requirements., and is included in
Appendix D.1.
KBR utilized the 3D model to extract piping quantities and generate isometric line counts for the FEED
estimate, rather than developing formal isometric drawings at this stage. This model-based approach
provided a comprehensive representation of the piping scope, suitable for quantity take-off and early
material estimating, in line with the FEED design maturity.

 61

5.4 Civil and Structural Engineering

5.4.1 Geotechnical Analysis
A geotechnical study was conducted to characterize the subsurface conditions at the proposed site for the
CO₂ capture facility. The investigation included borehole drilling, soil sampling, laboratory testing, and
groundwater level monitoring. The results provided essential data on soil bearing capacity, settlement
potential, and foundation design parameters. This information was used to support the design of foundations
for major process equipment, buildings, and pipe racks, as well as to inform site grading, drainage, and
construction planning.
The geotechnical assessment and topographical survey were performed by Braun Intertec Corporation, an
external engineering firm selected by Cemex. A 2D plot plan was provided by KBR. The proposed site for
the CO2 capture plant is located approximately 1600 feet west of the intersection of Wald Road and North
Solms Road in New Braunfels, Texas. The site is bounded to the north by Wald Road and railroad tracks,
to the east by an undeveloped area, to the south by North Solms Road followed by a ponded area, and to
the west by the intersection of North Solms Road and Wald Road. The site is traversed by two natural gas
pipelines along the south side of the site. The site appears to slope slightly from the southeast to the
northwest. An aerial view of the site is shown in Figure 13.
Subsurface conditions were evaluated by drilling three (3) soil borings to a depth of 35 ft. Soil samples
were collected at 2½-foot vertical intervals to a depth of about 10 feet, and at 5-foot intervals beyond this
depth. Subsurface investigations revealed fat clay (CH) and lean clay (CL) soils extending to the
termination depth of 35 feet across the site. Laboratory analysis indicates the clays are moderate to highly
expansive, with potential for shrink-swell behavior due to changes in moisture content. As a result, removal
of existing expansive soils and replacement with imported, non-expansive fill is recommended, particularly
within proposed building pad areas. While native soils are not suitable as select fill, they exhibit good
compaction characteristics and may be reused in non-structural areas, such as open spaces or, conditionally,
pavement zones depending on their expansion potential.
Two soluble sulfate tests were conducted per Tex-145-E. The results indicate low sulfate concentrations,
permitting the use of standard mix designs and construction methods without special sulfate mitigation.
Free water was not observed during or immediately after drilling. However, water conditions at the time of
construction may vary due to rainfall, irrigation, drainage, and seasonal changes. Contractors should assess
site-specific moisture and water conditions immediately prior to excavation and plan for appropriate water
control measures as needed.
The Topographical Survey, conducted as part of the geotechnical investigations, is provided separately in
Appendix E.1.

 62

Figure 23: Plot area location for the Capture Plant

5.4.2 Stormwater Runoff Plan
The stormwater runoff plan has been developed based on site-specific conditions, including subsurface
characteristics outlined in the geotechnical report. Due to the presence of expansive clay soils with limited
infiltration capacity, the plan emphasizes surface drainage and controlled conveyance rather than infiltration
systems. The runoff plan drawing illustrates the proposed drainage layout, including grading, swales, and
stormwater collection points designed to manage runoff in compliance with applicable regulations. The
stormwater runoff plan drawing is provided in Appendix E.2.

5.4.3 Spill Containment
The spill containment strategy summarizes the known effluent and effluent sources that will be connected
to the drainage system during normal operations, maintenance operation and off-design (upset, start-up
and shutdown) conditions. It also identifies the basis for tank bunds or dikes, including the equipment
contained within them and their sizing criteria.
A number of effluent streams arise, and the function of drainage system is to handle these waste streams to
satisfy the following objectives:

 63

 • Meet statutory and regulatory discharge requirement.
 • Maintain segregation of hazardous and non-hazardous areas.
 • Drain minor spills and limit the potential and extent of pool fires.
 • Effectively remove fire water and storm water from plant areas.
 • Prevent escalation of fire by minimizing drainage system interconnection.
 • Contain and recycle, where practical, process drainage to minimize liquid effluent discharges.
 • Safety- To protect personnel, plant, and equipment by preventing spillage release to environment.
 • Environment- To minimize and/or to prevent direct discharge of environmentally hazardous
 streams to environment by directing these streams to appropriate treatment units.

5.4.3.1 Drainage System
A combination of closed and open drainage systems are considered to manage different effluent types.
 1. Closed drains are used for hazardous materials like solvents and contact sewers, ensuring safe
 routing to treatment or disposal systems. Solvent systems feature a closed discharge to a Solvent
 Sump for isolation during maintenance, with recirculation back into the process system. Contact
 sewers handle process effluents like wash water, caustics, and acids, directing them to a Waste
 Water Treatment Plant (WWTP) for recycling or external discharge.
 2. Open drains are utilized for non-hazardous effluents, such as stormwater from clean areas, allowing
 for straightforward inspection and maintenance.

5.4.3.2 Tank Secondary Containment
Additionally, secondary containment—including bunds or dikes around storage tanks and equipment—is
provided to contain potential spills and prevent environmental release.
Four dikes are considered for this facility, one main dike for the ISBL equipment in amine solvent service
and three minor dikes in the OSBL for the various chemical storage areas – Solvent trains, Caustic Storage
area and Acid Storage areas.
Sizing for the tank dikes is based on the following requirements. These guidelines apply to both main and
minor dikes.
 • Containment is based on the sum of the following items:
 o Maximum tank volume in the dike
 o 25 year/24 hour rainfall (8.8 inches)
 o 6 inch freeboard
 • Maximum wall height is set at 4 feet.
Material quantities and specifications are detailed separately in the CSA Material Takeoffs (Appendix
E.3).

5.5 Electrical Engineering

5.5.1 Electrical Engineering Studies Summary
The Electrical Engineering discipline for the project focused on developing system designs, and specifying
equipment to meet the overall facility requirements. To support FEED cost estimation, the project team
leveraged the 30% complete 3D model, to determine with some level of accuracy, the cable lengths and
cable tray routings. This approach eliminated the need for fully developed traditional routing drawings at
this stage, while still providing sufficient detail for material take-offs and layout validation.
A Hazardous Area Classification study was also performed as part of the electrical scope. Based on process
safety considerations and material properties, only the Electrolyzer area was classified as hazardous,
designated as Class I, Division 2. All other areas are deemed non-hazardous. This classification directly
influences the selection of electrical equipment and installation methods within the designated zone. The
Hazardous Area Classification drawing is provided in Appendix F.1.
The Electrical Load List, which outlines all connected and operating loads, is included in Appendix F.2.
The Cable and Cable Tray Routing Drawings, extracted from the facility layout form the basis for cable
length and tray quantity estimates used in the FEED cost model.

5.6 Instrumentation & Controls (I&C) Engineering
The primary objective of the I&C discipline is to establish a comprehensive control strategy for the CO 2
capture system at the Balcones Cement Plant. This framework is designed to ensure safe, efficient, and
compliant operations throughout the entire process.

5.6.1 Control Narrative Framework
The control narrative outlines the principles and functionalities of the control systems implemented for the
CO2 capture process. Key elements include:
 • Distributed Control System (DCS): The DCS is central to monitoring and managing various
 components, including the Direct Contact Cooler (DCC), absorption columns, and CO2
 compression systems.
 • Automated Dampers: Flue gas flow to the CO₂ capture system is managed by automated dampers
 operated through the plant’s basic process control system (BPCS). Real-time communication
 between the BPCS and the CO₂ capture control system allows coordinated operation between the
 existing kiln/plant controls and the new capture system.
 • Critical Measurements: Key process parameters such as flue gas supply inlet pressure (PI-01004,
 PI-01037), DCC inlet temperature (TI-01005), and SO2 levels (AI-01008) are continuously
 monitored to ensure operational integrity.

5.6.2 Detailed Instrumentation Overview
 1. Flue Gas Pre-Treatment: Incorporates automated dampers and a suite of instruments for monitoring
 temperature, pressure, and flow rates, ensuring accurate process control.
 2. Direct Contact Cooler (DCC): Monitors inlet pressure, temperature, and flue gas composition, with
 built-in safety interlocks that can shut down dampers under high-pressure conditions.

 65

 3. Absorption Units: Absorption columns feature differential pressure measurements (PDI-01040)
 and level indicators (LI-01046) to maintain efficient solvent flow and CO2 absorption. Safety
 alarms trigger automatic shutdowns as needed.
 4. CO2 Compression and Polishing: The CO2 compressor package (K-0211) interfaces with the DCS,
 monitoring both outlet pressure and temperature while providing immediate alarm alerts for
 abnormal conditions.
 5. Additional Support Systems: Utility systems facilitate service feeds (water and air) with regulated
 controls, while Safety Instrumented Systems (SIS) ensure emergency shutdowns are executed
 based on predefined criteria to uphold safety.
The control narrative specifies hardwired signals for critical commands and feedback between the CO 2
capture system and Cemex’s existing BPCS, enhancing the system's reliability. Future phases of
engineering will refine the control strategies to incorporate improvements based on operational experiences,
including the implementation of advanced control algorithms and predictive maintenance tools.

 66

Accordingly, the usage of portable fire extinguishers as primary means of firefighting is proposed to address
the localized fire scenarios mainly from Class A, B and C.

5.7.3 Firefighting System And Equipment

5.7.3.1 Portable Dry Chemical Fire Extinguisher
For a typical NAS facility, the multipurpose ABC dry chemical (ammonium phosphate) portable fire
extinguishers with the capacity of 9kg are provided at the open process area and inside the central control
room (CCR). The provision is based on the prescriptive requirements stated in NFPA 10, as per the
following summary:
1) Considering the Class-B fire as the base case in the open process area, the travel distance to reach
 portable extinguishers at the open process area is taken as 50ft (15m) from hazards. This is complying
 with the recommended travel distance for 80-B rating, considering the “Extra” hazard present in this
 facility (as per Table 6.3.1.1 of NFPA 10).
2) The minimum capacity for 80-B:C rating of ABC dry chemical (ammonium phosphate) is 7.7kg (Table
 H.2 of NFPA 10). However, current provision capacity is 9kg, hence this is more than the minimum
 requirement.
3) One (1) no. also is provided inside the CCR as to extinguish potential Class A fires (paper, rubber and
 plastics).

5.7.3.2 Portable Carbon Dioxide (CO2) Fire Extinguisher
The provision of CO2 portable extinguisher is based on the prescriptive requirement stated in NFPA 10.
Considering the electrical fire hazard (Class C) presents in the Central Control Room (CCR), 1 no. of
portable CO2 extinguisher is provided with the capacity of 5kg. Considering the type of hazard presents in
the CCR, the hazard will be classified as “Ordinary” given the size, numbers of energized equipment and
occupancy. The “Ordinary” will fall under 10-B:C rating, hence the minimum capacity required is 4.5kg.
The current provision capacity meets the minimum requirements.

5.7.3.3 Fire Hydrant (Optional)
The provision of fire hydrants is meant for secondary firefighting in which it will be utilized by trained
plant first intervention team / fire brigades / fire departments personnel as firefighting support. The intention
is mainly to prevent fire escalation beyond the facility limits that could impact nearby facilities and the
public.
The fire hydrants, if installed, shall be compatible with the equipment specified for use by plant first
intervention team or fire brigades / fire departments (local authority fire response groups). In this
perspective, it should be designed in accordance with the code used/applied by the local authorities/fire
marshal.
A redundant underground fire water loop with HDPE piping and sectional valves supports an 800,000-
gallon fire water tank and dual pumps (electric and diesel), ensuring reliable fire suppression capability.
Hydrants are spaced strategically throughout the site, supplemented by elevated and hydrant-mounted
monitors delivering targeted spray coverage.

 68

Sprinkler protection is planned primarily for the laboratory building, with wheeled Purple K fire
extinguishers distributed across process areas. Fireproofing is deemed unnecessary at this stage due to the
low risk of pool or jet fires.
Detection and alarm systems include CO2 and H2 gas detectors interfaced with the DCS, a comprehensive
fire alarm system with building-specific panels and emergency communication capabilities, and a
recommended VESDA system for early smoke detection in electrical and control buildings.
Design compliance aligns with IFC 2021, applicable NFPA standards, and relevant API guidelines.

5.8.2 Constructability Review Summary.
The proposed location for the CO2 capture plant is a 15-acre greenfield plot south of the New Braunfels
facility. Additional Cemex property to the south and west is available for potential laydown areas.

5.8.2.1 General
During the constructability review, several design/construction challenges were considered with their
impacts to construction access, laydown areas, and construction sequencing. These design challenges were
grouped into two categories: those due to site conditions or requirements, and those due to equipment size.
Recommendations from the constructability are summarized below.

5.8.2.2 Design Challenges Due to Site Conditions
Cooling Water Constraints: Due to limited make-up water availability at the New Braunfels facility,
cooling water studies were conducted to determine the appropriate utility configuration for process cooling.
The cooling system optimization study resulted in utilizing air coolers, with 146 bays of cooling, and an
estimated total plot area of approximately 4 acres of the total 15-acre facility plot plan (not including
retention pond or laydown areas).
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING

 70

High Water Table: Cemex advised during the constructability review that the proposed plot area has a
high-water table of 7 feet. The project team agreed to minimize underground services to the maximum
extent possible and only consider items such as equipment drains and grey water services. A high-water
table could also potentially require an engineered drainage system, such as a well point system. This system
may require a collection pond.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
Carbon Capture Plant Located Off-Site: Due to the limited available area at the existing Cemex plant
for the carbon capture plant, the CC plant is remotely sited across Wald Road. This requires routing a large
10 ft x 10 ft cross sectional flue gas duct approximately 1,500 feet, across the road, to the DCC/absorber
columns.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
MSHA vs. OSHA Regulations: The Cemex New Braunfels site falls under the jurisdiction of the Mine
Safety and Health Administration (MSHA). The Project Team acknowledged there could be considerable
differences between MSHA and OSHA regulations which may impact design, construction,
commissioning, and eventual operations of the CC plant. MSHA or OSHA jurisdiction will need to be
investigated and established to ensure design and construction safety implications are clearly understood
by the detail design and construction stakeholders.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: SAFETY

5.8.2.3 Design Challenges Due to Equipment Size
Absorber Column:
The field erection strategy including laydown and sequencing would require careful planning and execution
as the absorber column is closely located with other critical processing equipment. The field fabrication of
the Absorber Column must be supported by a detailed construction schedule. The erection strategy must
consider the long-term impact on the rest of the project.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
Packaged Boilers: Steam demand can be met with (2) 50% packaged boiler however, this size is at the
upper end of the boiler supplier’s packaged boiler design capacity, each 25.5 ft H x 22.3 W x 52 ft L. Each
boiler weighs approximately 150 tons. The logistics study finds this size boiler is not truckable and
therefore, smaller size boilers in a (3) x 33% configuration will have to be investigated. Cost impacts based
upon previous studies are close between these two boiler configurations.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS
Interchanger HX Units: HXs are required between the absorber column and regenerator. The HXs banks
are in a section of the facility that facilitates construction access and laydown. Stacking of the plate and
frame HXs was considered by the project team but determined to present concerns with maintenance access
during the cleaning of the HX plates and subsequent draining of wastes and cleaning solvents in the
structure.
 • Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
Stripper Column: The Stripper column due to its diameter is being evaluated for both shop fabrication
and field erection execution strategy. A final decision on stripper column fabrication strategy will need to
be made once logistics study is conducted.

 71

 • Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING

5.8.2.4 Construction Access
Logistics Study: Carbon Capture necessitates deployment of large equipment along with large pre-
assembled units to drive a cost-effective solution. The logistics study will eventually be needed to determine
the limitations for truckable modules and pre-assembles units (PAUs). There is no waterway access for
barge shipment of units. Logistics study should be from point of origin to site (Logistics study establishes
shipping envelope and also determines best method for shipment of heavy haul items).
Based upon discussions with some local fabrication shops in Texas, the equipment selected (as described
above in the GENERAL section) should be truckable to the New Braunfels site. However, this needs to be
validated by the logistics study.
Overhead Power Lines: Overhead high voltage 138kv power lines, bisecting the plant East-West, must be
managed. Preliminary discussions with the local utility suggest raising the HV power lines and power and
communication lines on Wald Road can be handled to accommodate transportation of equipment to site.
Rail Shipments: Cemex New Braunfels site has existing rail for feed and product loadout. Cemex
mentioned this rail may have limitations regarding size and especially width of cargo. This aspect of rail
limitations should be addressed in the logistics study to confirm load/sizing limitations, if any.
Access to Potential Laydown Area on Existing Cemex Plant: The East area of the Cemex property is a
potential laydown area, however, access to the area is sometimes blocked due to loading and staging of rail
cars. Develop strategic transport plans for trucks to enter site during off-peak (if possible) and consider all
alternate routes.
South Plot Access: The plot plan area South of Wald Road can get saturated and can cause vehicles / trucks
to get stuck. Consider all routes and have a fallback plan to remove vehicles from mud if necessary.

5.8.2.5 Laydown Areas
Constraints: The plot area as currently envisioned does not provide what may be considered the “ideal”
laydown space (relative to a “factor” of total plot area). Considering the large area of the air coolers and
utilizing some of the corners of adjacent facility designated space; the strategy of ‘just-in-time’ deliveries
was considered one key approach to maintain maximum laydown areas during peak construction activities.
The following equipment was identified as candidates for ‘just-in-time’ delivery.
 ▪ Air coolers
 ▪ Flue Gas duct sections
 ▪ Tray and packing installation and storage
Temporary Construction Facilities: Laydown areas shall also consider temporary construction facilities,
which could be up to 2.0 times the total build area, depending on the project. This is to allow for the
following temporary facilities need to support construction:
 ▪ CM Office, Supervisor's offices, Safety facility, Quality office, Permit office, Warehouse
 (including Warehousing of temperature control sensitive equipment), Welfare tents (Break tents),
 Area for stockpiling re-usable and non-reusable soil

 72

5.8.2.6 Sequencing of Construction
Lifting Plans: The development of lifting plans/procedures should be completed early. Due to the
constraints mentioned above, this will be critical to orchestrate crane usage, costs, and safety.
Sequencing of Equipment: Development of proper sequencing of key equipment deliveries and
installation such as Absorber Column, DCC, Boilers, Air coolers. The optimum path of construction and
schedule will need to reflect a staging of key erection and lift and setting plans for the critical pieces of
equipment identified earlier.
Engage an EPC for final path of construction and develop appropriate Advanced Work Packages (AWP).
The following can be packaged by the project EP team:
 EWP – Engineering work package
 PWP – Procurement work package
 CWP – Construction work package
 IWP – Installation work package (The IWP process is typically a function of the construction
 execution contractor)
Tie-in Sequencing of Cement Pyro Process: Develop the sequence for shutdown / cooldown of the cement
pyro process system for tie-in of CC Plant equipment. Tie-ins must be planned during schedule outages.
Ensure PHA review addresses tie-in logic and cut over in design.
Consider a complete field survey of each tie-in point, irrespective of discipline. Then provide a tie-in
package for each tie-in which can include a location drawing, P&ID, data sheets, photographs and
description and purpose of the tie-in.
Tie-in Sequencing of Electric Power Sources: Investigate electric power sources and eventual tie-ins. In
support of commissioning activities, identify power sources and early tie-in strategy if needed. It is
recommended to consider whether alternative power sources are needed.
Construction Sequencing Path: The path should set up at a minimum the following objectives:
 ▪ A construction execution schedule
 ▪ High level Construction Organization
 ▪ HSSE Strategy
 ▪ QA Strategy
 ▪ QC Strategy
 ▪ Path of construction (Plot plan, CWA)
 ▪ HHR and HHL Plans
 ▪ TCF Layout and work sequence
 ▪ HSSE
 ▪ Logistics (project freight)
 ▪ Logistics strategy (Local to plant traffic/workers)
 ▪ HHL/HHL Strategy

The complete Constructability Review Summary is provided in Appendix G.1.

6 Project Schedule

6.1 Schedule Overview
As part of the Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) phase, KBR developed a Level 2 EPC schedule to
provide a high-level view of engineering, procurement, construction, and commissioning activities. This
schedule outlines key project phases, interfaces, and milestones, and supports alignment between design
and execution planning. It serves as a strategic tool for scope definition, constructability planning, and
development of the AACE Class 3 cost estimate.
While not fully exhaustive, the schedule is based on available FEED deliverables, current project definition,
and multi-discipline input. It offers mid-level detail suitable for constructability reviews, risk identification,
and initial execution strategy. This Level 2 schedule will be further developed into a detailed Level 3
schedule post-award to support project controls, progress tracking, and execution planning.
The project is assumed to commence with contract signing and a kickoff meeting (Month 0), followed by
mobilization of the project team within 10 working days with an estimated duration of 24 months.
Construction is planned to begin 8 months after detailed engineering starts and will run for 24 months,
overlapping with design by approximately 16 months. A 3-month commissioning phase will follow,
resulting in a total project duration of approximately 36 months. The Level 1 schedule is shown in Figure
24.

Figure 24: Level 1 Schedule for the RTI NAS CO2 Capture Project

planning, scheduling, and control. The WBS below reflects the current FEED phase understanding and is
developed to Level 2. It will be further refined during the EPC phase.

6.4 Engineering Schedule Assumptions
The engineering schedule and associated milestones have been developed based on the following key
assumptions, reflecting the current FEED deliverables and typical project execution strategy:
 • The project includes structured 30%, 60%, and 90% model development stages, all of which are
 considered critical milestones in the schedule.
 • Each model review is planned as a five-day joint review involving the client and the contractor.
 • Piping, Civil, Electrical, and Instrumentation teams will actively participate in all model reviews
 to ensure cross-discipline coordination.
 • Material Take-Offs (MTOs) will be extracted in alignment with model development stages:
 o Preliminary MTOs will be generated upon completion of the 30% model.
 o Final MTOs will be issued following the 90% model completion.
 • The schedule reflects separate MTO activities and procurement cycles for Civil, Piping, Electrical,
 and Instrumentation disciplines.
 • The HAZOP review is planned as a five-day workshop, commencing after the issuance of P&IDs
 for HAZOP. P&IDs will be updated post-HAZOP before progressing to detailed design.
 • Critical vendor data, including general arrangement drawings, equipment dimensions, and essential
 technical information, is assumed to be available in time to support the 60% model development.
 • Upon approval of critical vendor documents, equipment manufacturing activities are expected to
 commence.
These assumptions are aligned with the Level 2 EPC schedule and will be further refined during the
development of the detailed Level 3 schedule in the EPC phase.
Refer to Appendix H.1 for the detailed EPC schedule.

6.6 Construction Assumptions
This section outlines key assumptions used in developing the construction phase of the project schedule.
Construction activities are broken down into distinct phases and are expected to commence approximately
8 months after contract award, contingent on availability of sufficient IFC deliverables following the
30% model review.
Construction Work Breakdown Structure (WBS)
Construction activities are organized into the following major categories:
 • Early Works
 • Underground Works
 • Above Ground Works
 • Flue Gas Run
 • Commissioning and Start-Up
Early Works
Early works include all initial site activities necessary to prepare for main construction. These are
assumed to begin 8 months post-contract award and include:
 • Contractor mobilization
 • Site clearance (as required)
 • Installation of temporary site facilities
 • Site survey, expansive soil removal, filling, and compaction
Early IFC drawings are assumed to be available following the 30% model review, enabling start of these
activities.
Underground Works
Following completion of underground works, above-ground activities begin and continue through
mechanical completion. Underground works refer to all construction below grade, including but not limited
to:
 • Excavation
 • Installation of underground tanks
 • Firewater ring mains and collection heads
 • Backfilling and compaction

 78

Above Ground Works
Above ground works encompass the bulk of civil, structural, mechanical, piping, electrical, and
instrumentation activities, summarized as follows:
Foundations and Civil Works
 • Foundations for pipe racks, main equipment, and pre-engineered buildings
 • Paving and road construction (executed in parallel with commissioning phase)
Structural Steel
 • Primary steel: Main structural supports for major equipment
 • Secondary steel: Ancillary steel structures and supports
Equipment Erection
 • Direct erection of major equipment upon delivery to site
 • Absorber and Direct Contact Cooler erection (~10 months duration)
 • Air Coolers to be erected in batches, beginning upon first delivery
Piping Works
 • Piping prefabrication
 • Pipe and flange erection
 • Hydrotesting
Electrical and Instrumentation (E&I) Works
 • Substation erection and power cable installation
 • DCS equipment erection
 • Cable tray installation, cable pulling, glanding, and termination
 • Instrumentation erection
Finish Works
 • Insulation of instruments, equipment, and pipework
 • Final painting of pipework, equipment, and pipe racks

Flue Gas Run
This is a dedicated scope section for ductwork connecting to the existing cement plant. Activities include:
 • Excavation and foundation for duct supports
 • Backfilling and compaction
 • Erection of support racks
 • Installation of associated ductwork

6.7 Commissioning and Start-Up
Commissioning is the process of verifying that all systems and components are designed, installed, tested,
and operated in accordance with the owner’s requirements and the design intent. It is a critical phase prior
to start-up, ensuring that the facility is safe, functional, and ready to process raw materials.
Commissioning activities will commence following the completion of all mechanical, electrical, and
instrumentation erection. This phase includes:

 79

 • Equipment site testing (no-load and load testing)
 • Instrument loop checks
 • Verification of control logic and interlocks
 • System-level functional testing
A total duration of 3 months is allocated for commissioning and start-up in the project schedule.

6.8 Schedule Risks and Constraints
A formal schedule risk analysis has not yet been performed; however, key constraints have been identified
based on current FEED-level information. These include timely completion of model reviews, availability
of critical vendor data to support design progression, and the sequencing of engineering deliverables to
enable early procurement and construction activities. These factors will be further evaluated and integrated
into the detailed Level 3 EPC schedule post-award, where quantitative schedule risk analysis (QSRA) may
also be performed.

6.9 Construction Execution Plan

A standalone Construction Execution Plan (CEP) has been prepared as part of the FEED deliverables and
is provided in Appendix H.2. This document outlines the planned construction strategy, key execution
milestones, construction labor plan, site logistics, temporary facilities, and quality, safety, and
environmental controls.

The CEP supports the development of the Level 2 EPC schedule by defining construction sequencing, key
interfaces, and execution constraints. It also includes input into schedule durations for early works,
underground and above-ground construction, and commissioning readiness.

This plan will be further refined during the EPC phase and aligned with the detailed Level 3 schedule and
contractor execution plans.

7 Project Cost Estimate

7.1 Introduction
The estimate covers the RTI NAS Carbon Capture Plant intended for integration with the Balcones cement
plant for 95% CO2 capture. The estimate has been prepared in alignment with AACE International’s
Recommended Practices (18R-97) as a Class 3 estimate, targeting a level of accuracy of +15%/-15%. This
estimate was developed during FEED phase and employs the Aspen Capital Cost Estimator (ACCE V14.2).
The total project cost reflects U.S. dollars with a fourth-quarter 2024 cost basis and includes escalation
factors leading to a projected start date of Q1 2026. Indirect, owner-specific, and construction expenditures
were carefully accounted for in this evaluation. Key exclusions and assumptions are detailed in the full
Basis of Estimate (BOE) included as an appendix.
This report summarizes the estimate's purpose, methodology, cost breakdown, and assumptions, enabling
stakeholders to assess the project’s financial viability and risk exposure.
The estimate reflects a stick-built project execution model with a non-modular approach and assumes
construction will be performed under an Engineering, Procurement, and Construction Management
(EPCM) model.

7.2 Basis of Estimate (Summary)
The full Basis of Estimate document is provided in the Appendix I.1. Key elements of the BOE are
summarized below:

7.2.1 Estimation Tools and Methodology:
Estimates were generated using the Aspen Capital Cost Estimator (ACCE V14.2) with data inputs sourced
from engineering deliverables (e.g., MTOs, equipment lists, P&IDs), vendor quotes, and historical EPC
data. The ACCE tool provided field labor hours and material estimates adjusted for regional cost conditions
near San Antonio, Texas.

7.2.2 Currency and Units of Measure:
The estimate is presented in U.S. Dollars, calculated using Imperial units of measurement.

7.2.3 Project Work Breakdown Structure (WBS):
Costs were organized by system and discipline, as follows:
Unit 01: CO2 Capture System
Unit 02: CO2 Compression, Dehydration, and Polishing
Unit 03: Utility Systems which comprises of:
 ▪ Unit 11 – Raw, Potable, and Demineralized Water System
 ▪ Unit 12 – Boiler & Steam System
 ▪ Unit 14 – Solvent & Chemical Storage Utilities
 ▪ Unit 15 – Cooling Water System
 ▪ Unit 16 – Air / Nitrogen System
 ▪ Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
 ▪ Unit 20 – Stack

 81

 ▪ Unit 22 – Utility Headers
 ▪ Unit 23 – Power Generation

7.2.4 Construction Execution Basis:
The project is planned as a stick-built construction using a single mobilization strategy. Construction
activities will follow a five-day workweek, comprising 40 hours of regular time and 10 hours of overtime
paid at a 50% premium. Labor costs are based on local labor surveys, which were used to determine an
"All-In Wage Rate" (AIWR) of $105.80 per hour. This rate includes labor wages, fringe benefits, equipment
usage, temporary facilities, and contractor overhead.

7.2.5 Cost Basis and Escalation:
The cost baseline reflects Q4 2024 values. Forward escalation rates of up to 7.5% were applied to labor,
materials, and equipment to reflect projected costs at midpoint construction (Q4 2027).

7.2.6 Key Assumptions and Constraints
The project assumes adequate space within the site; however, workspace constraints (e.g., working around
large absorbers and regenerators) were identified and addressed with productivity adjustments. External
risks, such as global pandemics or extreme weather events, were not included as they are considered force
majeure events.

7.2.7 Labor Productivity Factors:
Modest inefficiency adjustments were applied to reflect the labor market, skill availability, and site-specific
conditions (e.g., rigging requirements, on-site fabrication). Overall labor efficiency was determined to be
70% compared to a baseline project.
Refer to the BOE appendix for additional details regarding excluded costs, engineering deliverables,
execution strategies, and owner-provided estimates.

were derived from discipline-level material take-offs (MTOs), preliminary design documents, and vendor
data available at the time of the estimate. Key materials include structural steel, piping (aboveground and
underground), electrical cable, instrumentation devices, concrete, and other civil and mechanical
components. Quantities were validated through available 3D model progress, line lists, and equipment
datasheets, ensuring alignment with the current level of engineering definition. These material quantities
form the basis for calculating bulk material costs and associated installation labor, and they directly inform
the development of the Total Installed Cost (TIC).

7.4 Capital Cost Estimates
The total estimated project costs for the Carbon Capture Plant have been calculated based on material
quantities, labor hours, wage rates, vendor quotes, and indirect cost allowances. The estimate includes Total
Direct Costs (TDC), indirect field costs, owner's indirect costs, escalation, and contingency.
The direct field costs encompass labor hours, average wage rates, labor costs, material costs, and their
respective contributions as percentages of the Total Direct Cost (TDC). In addition to these direct costs, the
estimate includes indirect field costs, owner’s indirect costs, and engineering/home office services. To
arrive at the Total Installed Cost (TIC), the estimate was further adjusted for escalation and contingency. A
summary of the final project costs, inclusive of these adjustments, is provided below.
The CAPEX cost estimates were developed by KBR based on detailed technical documentation, including
datasheets, requisition packages, specifications, and other relevant materials, to support the procurement of
vendor budgetary quotes. These quotes were evaluated against the associated design specifications to
determine scope inclusions and exclusions. For any excluded items, appropriate allowances were applied
to complete the estimate. Vendor quotes were obtained for all critical mechanical equipment, representing
94% of the total mechanical equipment cost. Additional quotes were also gathered for most electrical and
instrumentation equipment.
Generally, the lowest technically acceptable vendor quotes were selected for inclusion. However, in cases
where there was uncertainty regarding the accuracy or completeness of quotes, average pricing was used
as a conservative approach. Contractor quotations were also included for various infrastructure components
such as the power overhead feeder, gatehouse, warehouse, ECR building, control and change room building,
maintenance building, laboratory, and compressor sheds. The combined value of vendor budgetary quotes
and contractor estimates makes up approximately 66% of the total project equipment and material costs.
The remaining bulk materials and equipment costs were estimated using the ACCE (Association for the
Cost Engineering) methodology, with adjustments based on KBR’s historical cost data. Capital spares were
also incorporated into the estimate in accordance with KBR’s projections.
The project area south of Wald Road and the length of the low duct supports area will need to be over-
excavated by 9’ since the soil (expansive soils) is not suitable for structural backfill, as per the Cemex new
Geotechnical Report. This excavated soil will need to be disposed of at an approved landfill off-site.

 84

Imported non-expansive soil fill will need to be brought into the site and compacted to replace the over-
excavated expansive soil. The costs for the imported fill, soil transportation, and soil disposal cost were
estimated by KBR based on previous projects in the area.
The breakup of the total direct costs for major equipment systems is provided in Table 19
