7.5 Operating Cost Estimates
Operating cost projections were developed based on data generated during the FEED phase. These
projections incorporate detailed estimates for consumables, waste treatment, and utility consumption,

 88

including natural gas for steam generation and electricity for the operation of the CO₂ capture and
compression systems. To account for anticipated future conditions, the costs of natural gas and electricity
were escalated using internally forecasted rates provided by Cemex. Labor costs were determined by
identifying the necessary operations and maintenance staffing requirements and applying prevailing wage
rates corresponding to each role. This approach enabled the estimation of both fixed and variable labor
costs, reflecting local labor market conditions and the technical demands of operating the facility.
Total annual variable operating costs include all consumables necessary for the operation of the facility,
such as NAS solvent makeup, water and wastewater treatment chemicals, caustic soda, sulfuric acid,
activated carbon, de-oxo catalyst, and triethylene glycol (TEG). Cost estimates for the NAS solvent,
activated carbon, and de-oxo catalyst were developed based on vendor quotations. For other consumables—
including sulfuric acid, caustic soda, and TEG—unit prices were sourced from the U.S. Department of
Energy’s baseline study on retrofit CO₂ capture processes at cement plants [1]. The variable operating costs
also include the disposal of waste materials, including spent TEG, used adsorbents, and spent catalysts.
The total annual variable operating costs were estimated at $41.7 million. In addition, fuel and purchased
power costs were estimated at $61.6 million annually. Fixed operating costs include annual operations and
maintenance (O&M) labor, administrative and support staffing, as well as costs associated with
maintenance materials, property taxes, and insurance. The total annual fixed operating costs were estimated
at $11.4 million. Total annualized operating costs were estimated to be $108.8 million.

7.6 Cost of Capture
The cost of CO₂ capture was derived using the CAPEX estimates from the AACE Class 3 cost assessment
conducted during the FEED study performed by KBR. The OPEX cost estimates were developed based on
the FEED study deliverables, which included detailed lists of consumables, utility requirements, and waste
management needs.
Plant availability was assumed to be 90%. The CO₂ capture plant was designed with a capture capacity of
2.4 million metric tonnes per year, based on the nominal production capacity of the cement kilns and
expected variations in process flow rates. The average operating capacity of the plant is assumed to be 86%,
which, when accounting for the 90% availability, results in an overall capacity factor of 74%.
Total Owner’s costs encompass pre-production expenses, including six months of labor, one month of
maintenance materials, and start-up and commissioning costs. Additionally, these costs cover a 60-day
supply of non-fuel consumables and the expenses associated with the electrical substation required to
distribute power from the grid to the capture plant, as provided by New Braunfels Utility company. The
Owner’s costs also include estimates for permitting, topographical, soil, and water studies, as well as legal
fees and financing costs. These Total Owner’s costs were added to the Total Plant Cost (TPC) to derive the
Total Overnight Cost.
Overall cost of capture was estimated to be $101.6/t-CO2. This does not include the cost for transportation
and sequestration.
Inclusion of the amine recovery from acid wash waste stream technology reduces the cost of capture to
$93.0/t-CO2.

 89

8 Business Case Analysis
A comprehensive pro forma financial analysis of the CO₂ capture project was conducted by Cemex to
evaluate the project’s economic viability and long-term financial performance. This analysis incorporates
a detailed AACE Class 3 cost estimate, developed by KBR during the Front-End Engineering Design
(FEED) phase. The Class 3 estimate provides a robust foundation for evaluating capital expenditures, with
an expected accuracy range of +/- 15%, making it suitable for preliminary investment and funding
decisions.
Operating cost projections were developed using data from the FEED phase, including detailed estimates
for consumables, waste treatment, and utility consumption. This includes natural gas requirements for steam
generation and electricity usage for the operation of the CO₂ capture and compression systems. To reflect
future operating conditions, natural gas and electricity costs were escalated based on internal Cemex
forecasted rates. Labor costs were estimated by identifying the required operational and maintenance
staffing levels and applying prevailing wage rates for each role. This methodology allowed for the
development of both fixed and variable labor cost projections that reflect local labor market conditions and
the technical complexity of operating the facility.
CO₂ transportation costs were estimated using a recent internal quote from a comparable pipeline
infrastructure project. This baseline was adjusted to reflect the specific conditions of the Balcones CO₂
capture project, including the proximity of the proposed sequestration site. For the purposes of this analysis,
a conservative assumption of 20 miles of pipeline was used to estimate associated transportation costs.
CO₂ storage costs were derived from SLB’s internal estimates based on analogous storage projects. These
estimates include the cost of CO₂ injection and the installation and operation of measurement, monitoring,
and verification (MMV) systems in accordance with regulatory and environmental compliance
requirements. However, the storage cost estimate excludes financing costs, long-term liability provisions,
and pore space fees, which are expected to vary based on final site selection and future regulatory
frameworks.
Together, the transportation and storage cost estimates inform the broader calculation of the levelized cost
of CO₂ capture and storage (CCS), supporting a comprehensive financial evaluation of the carbon
management value chain for the Balcones project.
The analysis incorporates the benefit of federal 45Q tax credits, which offer a per-metric-ton incentive for
captured and permanently sequestered CO₂. Under the most recent guidance provided by the Inflation
Reduction Act, the value of the 45Q credit has been increased to $85 per metric ton for CO₂ stored through
secure geological sequestration, which is the basis used in this analysis.
To complete the analysis, Cemex incorporated company-specific financial assumptions related to financing
structure, cost of capital, and tax considerations to model project cash flows over its operational lifespan.
These inputs were used to determine the levelized cost of capture, as well as key financial metrics, including
the internal rate of return (IRR), net present value (NPV), and payback period.
Results from the pro forma are shown in Table 23 below. Key assumptions that are not proprietary and
business confidential are listed below:

• Inflation assumed (from IMF’s World Economic Outlook April 2025) [2]:

Results from the BCA indicates a negative value for the project net present value (NPV); therefore, further
incentives to make the project feasible will be needed in the future. Additional incentives could be achieved
through a combination of (1) further DOE/similar grants to fund carbon capture plant construction and
reduce exposure to high CAPEX, and (2) higher tax incentives to mitigate project OPEX and/or other
endeavors outside of taxes. The BCA indicates that for the project to break even, given the current 12-year
45Q period, the credit value would need to be approximately $175/MT sequestered compared to the current
$85/MT. Levelized revenues of 45Q credits do not reflect their full value due to intermediation costs and
the fact that the project is evaluated over 25 years while the tax credit is only valid for 12 years. If the 45Q
period were extended to 24 years, the required credit value would be around $120/MT for the project to
break even.

there is no green premium for decarbonized cement in the United States.

9 Value Engineering Opportunities
As part of the FEED phase, the project team performed a targeted value engineering (VE) review to identify
potential cost-saving opportunities without compromising technical integrity or operational requirements.
Two specific VE cases were identified and evaluated during this phase. The assessment focused exclusively
on potential capital cost reduction, with no consideration given to schedule or lifecycle impacts. The
following section outlines the VE opportunities reviewed, along with the rationale and estimated cost
implications.

9.1 Co-generation of Heat and Power
During the FEED phase, one of the key value engineering opportunities evaluated was the replacement of
the existing natural gas boiler—designed to supply low-pressure steam (LPS) for solvent regeneration in
the CO₂ capture plant—with an integrated co-generation system. This alternative aimed to improve overall
energy efficiency by generating both steam and electricity onsite.
Three configurations were assessed:
 • Option A: Installation of a larger natural gas boiler producing medium-pressure steam (MPS) at
 290 psig. The MPS would be expanded through a steam turbine to generate electricity, with the
 exhaust LPS utilized for solvent regeneration. This configuration yields approximately 36 MW of
 electricity, meeting 57% of the CO₂ capture and compression (CC) system's power demand.
 • Option B: Installation of a boiler producing high-pressure steam (HPS) at 620 psig. Similar to
 Option A, the HPS would be routed through a turbine, generating approximately 52 MW of
 electricity, covering 82% of the CC power demand.
 • Option C: Similar to Option B, this configuration also utilizes HPS but incorporates a larger BT63
 turbine instead of the BT50. The higher efficiency of the BT63 turbine increases power generation
 to 56.5 MW, covering approximately 90% of the CC facility’s electrical load, with only 10% of
 power required from the grid.
To evaluate the technical and economic feasibility of each configuration, capital cost estimates were
developed based on vendor quotations for both the upgraded boiler systems and the steam turbines.
Operating costs, including increased natural gas consumption, were also estimated. Net power generation
figures were calculated based on the steam requirements of the CO₂ capture process.
A summary of the capital and operating cost estimates for the co-generation options is provided in Table
24.

Among the options considered, Option C appears to offer the most favorable balance between capital
investment, energy efficiency, and operating cost reduction. Its ability to meet 90% of the CCS plant's
power demand internally presents a significant opportunity to minimize reliance on grid electricity and
improve overall project economics. However, due to its higher complexity and equipment scale, Option C
will require further technical and economic validation including a more refined assessment of integration
impacts, space requirements, and operational flexibility.

9.2 Increased flue gas CO2 concentration
An air leakage assessment conducted at the plant identified the raw mill circuit as a significant source of
false air ingress, particularly during raw mill operation. Air enters the system, leading to dilution of the flue
gas exiting the pre-heater tower. This false air reduces the CO₂ concentration in the flue gas, which in turn
negatively affects the efficiency and cost-effectiveness of downstream CO₂ capture.
Measurements taken during the assessment indicated a CO₂ concentration of approximately 12% (wet basis)
when the raw mill was in operation. Based on the leakage points identified, it is estimated that eliminating

or significantly reducing false air ingress could increase the CO₂ concentration to approximately 24% (wet
basis). Even with a conservative assumption of 3–5% residual air leakage, the CO₂ concentration is expected
to improve to 22.6–23.0%, representing a significant enhancement over current conditions. Given the
ongoing requirement to dry raw materials, and the technical challenges associated with recovering heat
from dust-laden flue gas via alternative means, continued use of pre-heater flue gas for raw material drying
remains the most practical and thermally efficient approach—particularly once air leakage is minimized.
This presents a clear value engineering opportunity for the CO₂ capture project. By addressing air ingress
through targeted maintenance actions, the plant can achieve a substantial increase in flue gas CO₂
concentration with minimal capital investment.
Higher CO₂ partial pressure in the flue gas will enhance capture system performance by improving solvent
absorption kinetics and reducing the energy demand for solvent regeneration. In addition, the associated
reduction in flue gas volumetric flow allows for downsizing key components, including absorber and DCC
(Direct Contact Cooler) columns, flue gas ducts, and blower. These changes are expected to result in capital
cost savings across the capture system, although the magnitude of these savings will need to be quantified
through detailed design and cost estimation. Collectively, these improvements contribute to a lower overall
cost per tonne of CO₂ captured, reinforcing the importance of minimizing air leakage in the raw mill circuit
as part of the broader decarbonization strategy.

9.3 Amine recovery from acid wash waste
The CO₂ capture plant design proposes the use of an acid wash system for emissions control, utilizing RTI’s
Non-Aqueous Solvent (NAS). In its current configuration, the acid wash removes any residual amine from
the treated gas by reacting the amine with the acid to form an amine-sulfate salt. This salt is typically treated
as waste, and the lost amine must be replenished through the addition of makeup amine. However, this
approach introduces operational inefficiencies in terms of chemical costs and waste handling.
A value engineering opportunity exists to recover the amine from the acid wash waste, leveraging the
hydrophobic nature of the NAS solvent amine. Experimental work conducted by RTI has demonstrated that
this amine can be successfully separated from the acid wash waste stream with a recovery rate of at least
95%. The process entails neutralizing the amine-sulfate salt to regenerate the parent amine, which then
separates out as an organic phase due to its hydrophobic properties. The recovered organic amine can be
recycled into the NAS solvent loop, creating a closed-loop process. By reducing the loss of amine and
limiting the need for fresh chemical makeup, this recovery process offers significant cost savings.
Experimental testing has demonstrated the technical feasibility and maturity of the amine recovery from
acid wash waste stream technology, thereby eliminating significant implementation risks. The technology
is planned for inclusion as part of the standard design package for subsequent projects. However, it was not
incorporated into the current FEED design, as its development progressed in parallel and reached
completion after the FEED design freeze. The cost and performance impacts associated with its
implementation have been evaluated and incorporated into the Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) to inform
future design and deployment decisions.

10 Environmental Health and Safety (EH&S)
This section of the FEED report presents prominent Environment, Health, and Safety (EH&S)
considerations associated with the proposed NAS CO2 capture technology at the Balcones site. It introduces
targeted evaluations of potential emissions and waste streams, outlines the toxicological profiles of involved
substances, and discusses relevant chemical hazard properties. Additionally, this section encompasses
regulatory compliance aspects, an engineering assessment of hazardous materials, and comprehensive
guidelines for safe handling and storage. Each of these topics is elaborated upon in the subsequent
subsections to ensure a thorough understanding of the EH&S implications, facilitating safe, compliant, and
environmentally responsible project execution.

10.1 Potential Ancillary Emissions and Waste Streams
The FEED study identifies the addition of a new emissions stack (A-2001) at the Balcones site as part of
the CO₂ capture process. This stack, situated at the top of the acid wash section of the absorber, will serve
as the primary discharge point for treated flue gas, as well as for relief, vent, and off-gas streams originating
from various units across the capture plant. Emissions sources that contribute to this stack include gas reliefs
from pressure safety valves (PSVs), vents from equipment such as the TEG package (A-0221) and
Regenerator Column (C-0151), and the outbreathing vent from the Solvent Sump (V-1401). Although the
A-2001 stack is physically separate, its emissions will ultimately merge into the absorber stack. Additional
emissions will derive from direct atmospheric vents and fugitive releases across different units within the
NAS CO₂ capture plant. Net change in emissions data covering minor species such as PM, THC, SO₂, CO,
NO , and HCl, is summarized in Table 25. Data for CO2 is shown as % net reduction

10.2 Toxicological Effects of NAS Components
The NAS amine constitutes the primary focus for evaluating toxicological effects related to EH&S. The
formulation comprises an amine species blended with a hydrophobic organic diluent. While comprehensive
toxicity studies on NAS are currently unavailable, relevant data from past projects (DE-FE0013865, DE-
FE0026466, DE-FE0031660) and existing literature have been reviewed to evaluate potential health and
environmental impacts. Extensive ecological and toxicological studies are essential to establish exposure
limits, acceptable contamination levels in waterways, and biological toxicity for large-scale commercial
deployment.
The amine is commercially available and categorized as a combustible and corrosive liquid that can cause
skin and eye irritation. As indicated by its Hazardous Material Identification System ratings, it poses low
toxicity risks and is not classified under significant hazardous chemical lists (e.g., TSCA, SARA). Despite
its relatively benign nature, it is critical to mitigate the risks associated with its release into the environment,
whether through emissions or wastewater. Current releases are estimated at about 30 ppm; however,
ongoing development of emission control technologies (ECT) aims to reduce this figure to less than 1 ppm.
The organic diluent, akin to the amine, is commercially produced, carrying no persistent or bioaccumulative
risks. It is deemed harmful to aquatic life, with a Derived No Effect Level (DNEL) defined for workers.
The diluent exhibits low volatility and a high biodegradability rate (99%), reflecting minimal environmental
impacts. In cases where wastewater is released, it may contain trace amounts of the diluent, necessitating
diligent handling as hazardous waste.
The assessment indicates that NAS solvent components possess low toxicity. Nevertheless, proactive
monitoring and control measures will be imperative to minimize any environmental and health risks
associated with their use in extensive applications. Further investigations and adherence to local regulations
are vital for the safe implementation of NAS technology.

10.3 Physical Properties of NAS
NAS is a formulation of a hydrophobic amine in an organic diluent. NAS is a liquid characterized by
relatively high density and low volatility, thus rendering it suitable for industrial applications. NAS has no
measurable flash point. The NAS technology also employs an acid wash process for emissions control,
effectively minimizing potential environmental and safety risks.
Corrosion coupon evaluations performed during the Technology Centre Mongstad (TCM) engineering
demonstration confirmed the low-corrosivity nature of the NAS solvent [3]. This finding indicates a reduced
potential for material degradation and supports consideration of lower-grade carbon steel for certain process
equipment, subject to detailed design verification. Complementary testing at the National Carbon Capture
Center’s Slipstream Testing Unit (NCCC-SSTU) in 2018 further corroborated these results, showing metal
ion concentrations substantially lower than those observed during previous MEA baseline studies. These
results suggest that the NAS solvent poses minimal corrosion risk under expected operating conditions,
contributing positively to overall plant safety and maintainability.

 96

Analysis of various metal concentrations, including barium (Ba), selenium (Se), arsenic (As), cadmium
(Cd), lead (Pb), and silver (Ag), in NAS solvent samples confirm compliance with U.S. Environmental
Protection Agency’s Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) limits, highlighting safe handling
and minimal environmental impact.
The favorable physical properties and low corrosivity profile of the NAS solvent substantiate its application
in industrial settings, potentially supporting the use of lower-grade materials while ensuring adherence to
regulatory requirements for environmental safety.

10.4 Compliance and Regulatory Implications
The NAS technology for acid gas removal shares similarities with established natural gas processing
techniques that utilize amine solutions to effectively remove H2S and CO2. Numerous successful
installations have been implemented across the U.S. and Canada, demonstrating safe operations in
accordance with applicable regulations.
The NAS process is specifically designed to capture pollutants that would otherwise be emitted in stack
emissions from power plants, producing negligible environmental impact. Compliance with the Clean Air
Act and National Standards for Performance (NSPS) concerning the removal of specified amounts of SOx,
NOx, and particulates remains intact, as these emissions are managed upstream of the NAS system. The
implementation of emission control technologies (ECT) is anticipated to further reduce amine and
degradation product emissions while minimizing solvent losses over time. Nonetheless, contaminants in
the CO2 product, including amines, must be addressed through condensation and dehydration processes to
meet CO2 pipeline standards, thereby mitigating risks related to bacterial growth within the pipeline and
reservoir.
Under the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA), NAS
components do not appear on the hazardous substance list. Some glycol ethers, however, are generically
listed without specific Reportable Quantities. The components used in NAS's diluent are noted in the EPA’s
Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) inventory.
None of the NAS chemicals are classified as Hazardous Air Pollutants (HAP) under the Clean Air Act;
however, glycol ethers may be subject to broader regulations. The NAS chemical components do not fall
under air toxics regulated by the NSPS program or accidental release prevention measures specified in
Section 112(r) of the Clean Air Act.
In terms of reporting obligations under the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act (SARA) Title
III, Sections 311-312 require facilities handling hazardous chemicals to maintain a Tiered chemical
inventory if quantities exceed specified thresholds. While none of the NAS components are present on the
Section 313 chemical list, Diethylene Glycol Dibutyl Ether (CAS 112-73-2) falls within the N230 category
code for certain glycol ethers, potentially triggering additional reporting requirements.
The NAS technology offers a low environmental impact profile and complies with various regulatory
requirements, positioning it favorably for industrial applications. Adherence to existing laws and
regulations ensures safe operations while addressing any potential environmental concerns related to
chemical emissions.

10.5 Engineering Analysis of Identified Hazardous Materials

 97

To minimize emissions of NAS amine in streams intended for environmental discharge, the implementation
of Emission Control Technologies (ECT), such as acid wash systems, is essential. Testing conducted at the
Technology Centre Mongstad (TCM) has yielded promising results, demonstrating that overall amine
emissions from the CO2 capture plant can be maintained below 1 ppm.
Environmental releases resulting from leaks, piping failures, spills, and drips are efficiently managed
through the integration of appropriate containment infrastructure. All collected drips will either be recycled
or disposed of in compliance with regulatory standards. Additionally, vessels and storage containers will
be designed to adhere to applicable codes and utilize compatible materials to effectively mitigate corrosion
and prevent leakage.
To ensure safe handling during solvent offloading from drums or containers, engineering controls will be
implemented to prevent mismatched connections to transfer pumps and the use of incompatible elastomers
or hoses. Importantly, no direct access to the plant sewer from the process skid will be allowed. Personnel
will utilize appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) to avoid contact with the solvent during routine
and maintenance procedures.

10.6 Precautions for Safe Handling and Storage Conditions
 • To ensure the safe handling of NAS, the following precautions must be observed:
 • Avoid contact with skin, eyes, and clothing; avoid inhalation of mists/vapors.
 • Implement local exhaust ventilation during use.
 • Wear chemical-resistant gloves and protective clothing.
 • Ensure eye wash fountains and safety showers are readily accessible.
A solvent-side slipstream reclaim process is implemented to manage potential nitrosamine formation during
CO2 capture. An activated carbon bed process will be utilized for nitrosamine removal during pilot-scale
operations.
The NAS solvent is not highly volatile, and vaporization from stored solvents is expected to be minimal
with proper storage practices, resulting in negligible exposure levels. The primary risk of exposure may
occur during the offloading of the solvent from shipping containers and during certain maintenance tasks,
such as the replacement of cartridges or filters. Job Safety Analyses (JSAs) will be conducted for these
procedures, and appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE), including hazard suits, gloves, face
shields, and breathing apparatus, will be worn, as necessary.
Waste generated from NAS, both solid and liquid, will be disposed of at approved hazardous waste disposal
facilities, with no onsite waste disposal or treatment permitted.

10.6.1 Personal Protective Equipment (PPE)
 • Eye/Face Protection: Use chemical goggles or a face shield as required.
 • Skin Protection: Wear protective clothing that is chemically resistant, selecting specific items (face
 shield, boots, apron, full-body suit) based on task requirements.
 • Hand Protection: Employ chemical-resistant gloves (e.g., nitrile, chloroprene, PVC), ensuring
 selection considers other chemicals handled, physical requirements, and glove material
 characteristics.

 98

 • Respiratory Protection: Use respiratory protection if ventilation is inadequate. In emergencies or
 high exposure situations, a NIOSH-certified full facepiece SCBA or supplied-air respirator is
 advised.
 • Ingestion Precautions: Avoid ingestion to prevent gastric disturbances. Eating, drinking, and
 tobacco use should not occur in the work area, and handwashing prior to these activities is essential.

10.6.2 Engineering Controls
Ventilation: Local exhaust ventilation or similar engineering controls must be utilized to maintain airborne
levels of NAS below exposure limits. General ventilation is typically sufficient for most operations.

10.6.3 Nitrosamine Exposure Management
Potential exposure to nitrosamines can occur through incidental ingestion, dermal exposure, and inhalation;
inhalation is considered the most plausible route. The use of PPE significantly minimizes both dermal
exposure and incidental ingestion. To prevent the incidental inhalation, engineering control such as (i)
Negative-Pressure Zones to maintain negative pressure in solvent-handling rooms, pilot units, or high-
hazard areas to prevent vapors drifting into hallways or adjacent labs, (ii) Enhanced General Ventilation to
increase air volumetric displacement per hour (ACH ≥ 8–12) in spaces where nitrosamines are expected to
be present or accumulate.
Monitoring of air emissions in industrial settings has revealed varying concentrations of nitrosamines;
extensive studies have indicated that no nitrosamines were detected during baseline air quality monitoring
in operations similar to NAS. Notably, emissions from CO2 capture pilot testing exhibited average
nitrosamine derivatives of the NAS amine at 25 ppb in treated flue gas.
The precautions and protective measures outlined above are essential for the safe handling of NAS during
industrial applications. With effective engineering controls and the appropriate use of personal protective
measures, exposure risks can be minimized, ensuring compliance with relevant environmental health and
safety standards.
For further detailed information, please refer to the full report in the Appendix J.

 99

11 Lifecycle Analysis

11.1 Goal and Scope
The study presents an LCA for the proposed carbon capture system (CCS) at the Balcones Cement Plant in
New Braunfels, Texas. The proposed product system is based on system/subsystem models and prototypes
demonstrated in a relevant environment, with the project currently at TRL 6. It would capture and
geologically sequester 95% of CO2 directly emitted from the plant as well as additional CO2 produced by
the CCS system. This LCA incorporates CO2 saline aquifer transport and storage, combustion emissions at
the facility, process operations and chemical inputs to the facility, and construction impacts for required
materials and equipment.
The goal is to compare and understand the differences between the NETL Tool for Reduction and
Assessment of Chemicals and Other Environmental Impacts (TRACI) 2.1 environmental impacts, with a
particular focus on global warming impacts, from the Cemex Balcones cement plant with and without RTI’s
proposed CCS system for the U.S. DOE Carbon Utilization Program and as a public record per the funding
opportunity announcement requirement. The scope includes the proposed product system of the Cemex
Balcones Cement Plant with carbon capture and storage, with the functional unit being a metric tonne (MT)
of Type IL and Type I/II cement produced. The system boundary is cradle-to-gate, stopping at the gate of
a MT of cement produced. Data representativeness assumes the CCS facility has a lifetime of 25 years, with
global warming potential assessed on a 100-year basis. Electric power requirements will be modeled from
the Texas Reliability Entity (TRE). No credit or allocation will be applied for co-products, and recycling
and recycled content will follow the cut-off rule. See Figure 27 and Figure 28 for a simplified system
boundary with reference flows between key processes or life cycle stages.

 100

Figure 27: Process flow diagram for the proposed product system

 Figure 28: Process flow diagram for the comparison product system

11.2 Lifecycle Inventory Analysis
This project was modeled using OpenLCA software and uses the EcoInvent 3.10 Cutoff System Processes
and NETL databases as LCIs. Further, this study uses the TRACI 2.1 impact assessment method combined
with the latest global warming potential (GWP) factors included in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate
Change (IPCC) Fifth Assessment Report (AR5) report.

11.3 Lifecycle Interpretation

 101

The life cycle results for the AR5, 100-year GWP are presented in Figure 29 and Figure 30 for both Types
IL and I/II cement products. These results show the proposed product system (i.e., with CCS) with the
comparison product system (i.e., without CCS) for each type of cement. The comparison product system is
shown in the first column and is divided into direct and indirect CO2 emissions. The direct CO2 emissions
are shown in orange and the indirect CO2 emissions are shown in blue. CO2 emissions that were added with
CCS to the proposed product system are shown in gray and CO2 emissions that are captured with CCS are
shown in green. The proposed product system is shown in the last column and is divided into direct and
indirect CO2 emissions. The direct CO2 emissions are shown in orange and the indirect CO2 emissions are
shown in light blue. The proposed product system shows a 66.1% decrease in GHG emissions for the Type
IL cement and a 66.5% decrease in GHG emissions for the Type I/II cement.

 Figure 29:GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT
 cement basis for Type IL cement
An estimated 668.13 kg CO2/MT-cement produced and 725.58 kg CO2/MT-cement produced are emitted
as direct CO2 emissions for Type IL and Type I/II cements, respectively, without the FEED CCS system.
Per Cemex’s environmental product declaration, the overall CO2 emissions from production of cement at
its Balcones plant is 756 kg CO2eq/MT of Type IL cement and 816 kg CO2eq/MT of Type I/II cement when
including supporting processes. Employing the FEED carbon capture process is estimated to save 499.98
kg CO2e/MT Type IL cement produced and 542.92 kg CO2e/MT Type I/II cement produced, limiting direct
CO2 emissions to 41.00 kg CO2e/MT cement and 44.59 kg CO2/MT cement at the cement plant for Type
IL and Type I/II cements, respectively. This represents a 66.1% (Type IL) and 66.5% (Type I/II) reduction
in overall GHG emissions for Portland cement production.

 cement basis for Type I/II cement
As the electrical grid and transportation systems are decarbonized in the future, further CO 2eq emissions
could be realized as the embodied carbon within the processes used in this model would decrease.
Given the large reductions of approximately two thirds in the Balcones cement plant’s carbon footprint for
both Types IL and I/II cement from the proposed CCS system, the PIs recommend further project evaluation
of the Proposed Product System.

specifications to see what is included and excluded. Any items excluded from the quote were estimated
with allowances. Equipment quotes were obtained on all critical mechanical equipment, which make up
94% of the total mechanical equipment costs in the estimate. In addition, quotes were obtained on most
electrical equipment and instruments. In general, the lowest technically viable quotes were considered for
the estimate, but averages were used in a few cases due to uncertainty in the accuracy of the quote and items
included by the vendor. Contractor quotes were also included for Power Overhead Feeder, Gatehouse,
Warehouse, Engineering & Control Room Building, Control Room & Change Room Building,
Maintenance Building, Lab, and Compressor Sheds. The combined value of the vendor budgetary quotes
and contractor estimates make up 66% of the total project equipment & material costs. The remaining bulk
materials and equipment were estimated by ACCE indexed based on experience.

 104

A Cost Risk Analysis was performed by KBR for the project through a Monte Carlo simulation The analysis
included conversations with the engineering disciplines and other KBR departments involved in the
development of the estimate to assign proper expected variability ranges to quantities and costs. After
discussions with Cemex, the P70 value of the results, corresponding to 6.9%, was included as the project
contingency cost in the estimate summary. A process contingency of 17% was used for CO 2 capture
CAPEX costs.
Owner’s cost was then added to TPC to produce the total overnight cost (TOC) for the system. Owner’s
costs as defined in the DOE Baseline Study [1]

12.1.3 Operation and Maintenance Costs
Operating labor cost was determined based on the number of operators required to work in the plant. Other
assumptions used are the same as the DOE-NETL 2023/3856 Baseline report [1].
The cost of consumables, including fuel, was determined based on the individual rates of consumption, the
unit cost of each specific consumable commodity, and the plant annual operating hours. Waste quantities
and disposal costs were evaluated similarly to the consumables.
The unit costs for major consumables and waste disposal was selected from the DOE/NETL -2023/3856
Baseline Report, Analysis of Carbon Capture Retrofits for Cement Plants [1] or vendor quotes. Natural gas
fuel is purchased at $4.61/MMBtu, and power is purchased at a price of $67.3/MWh and are consistent with
the values used in the DOE/NETL 2023/3856 Baseline Report.

12.1.4 Cost of Capture
The primary metric used to evaluate overall financial performance is the cost of capture (COC) for the
retrofit CO2 capture plant. All costs were expressed in the “first-year-of-construction” year dollars, and the
resulting COC was also expressed in “first-year-of-construction” year dollars.
The same financial modeling methodology was used for this study as per the 2023/3856 Baseline Report
[1] and guidelines in the QGESS Cost Estimation Methodology for NETL Assessments of Power Plant
Performance [2] document. This is a simplified method that is a function of the plant TPC, capital charge
factor (CCF), fixed and variable operating costs, CF, as shown in the equation below:

Where:
 TOC Total Overnight Cost of equipment added for CO2 capture
 CCF Capital Charge Factor
 FOM Annual fixed O&M costs
 VOM Annual variable O&M costs
 PF Purchased Fuel
 PP Purchased Power

The CCF used in evaluating the COC was 8.84%, and developed by NETL’s Energy Markets Analysis
Team in December 2022 based on market data respective to the cement sector, as outlined in the DOE-
NETL 2023/3856 Baseline report [1].

 105

Three different cases are evaluated. Case A is the base case with the capture plant as currently designed
with hybrid cooling and heat-integrated CO2 compressors. Case B is a hypothetical case where the
constraint for the limited water supply was removed and the plant and CO2 compressors are water cooled
and the plant is sited in proximity to the cement plant. Case C is a comparative case that evaluates the
impact of co-generation of steam and electricity. A comparative “Cemex” case is also presented which uses
the Cemex financial parameters (except for capital charge factor), Cemex’s estimate for the owner’s cost,
labor estimates based on local wages, and utility prices.
The cost of capture, excluding T&S, using Cemex parameters was estimated to be $101.6/t-CO2. Using
DOE parameters and assumptions results in the cost of capture of $112.9/t-CO2. Relaxing the constraint of
limited water availability reduces the cost of capture to $98.3/t-CO2. Also, using a cogeneration plant for
steam and electricity reduces the cost of capture for the base case from $112.9/t-CO2 to $103.9/t-CO2.
Results from the TEA are summarized in Table 2.

17 Conclusions/Recommendations
RTI International, with Cemex Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., participated in a joint effort to perform a Front-
End Engineering Design (FEED) study for carbon dioxide (CO2) capture from cement flue gas utilizing the
Non-Aqueous Solvent (NAS) CO2 capture process developed by RTI. The FEED study evaluated the
integration of RTI’s CO2 capture system at an existing cement plant, the Cemex Balcones cement plant at
New Braunfels, Texas. The FEED was used to develop an Association for the Advancement of Cost
Engineering (AACE) Class 3 cost estimate for a nominal 2.4 million tonnes per annum (MTPA) CO₂
capture system integrated with Cemex’s Balcones cement plant in New Braunfels, TX.
Major findings from the FEED study are presented below:
 • The estimated total capital expenditure (CAPEX) for the NAS CO₂ capture plant integrated with
 Cemex’s Balcones cement plant is $849 million. This includes $558 million in direct costs, $54
 million in indirect costs, $51million in engineering costs, $24 million in Owner’s costs, and $104
 million in contingency.
 • The design rate of CO2 capture is 2.4 MTPA (6,575 tonnes/day) at 100% capacity factor. Current
 CO2 capture plant capacity factor is 74%, with a CO2 capture rate of 1.766 MTPA (4,839
 tonnes/day).
 • The current design employs a hybrid air and water cooling system, with 84% of the cooling load
 handled by air cooling. This configuration significantly affects both capital cost and electricity
 consumption due to the extensive use of air coolers and the heat-integrated CO₂ compression train.
 The use of alternative cooling systems to compensate for limited water availability, however, has
 an impact on the overall cost of CO₂ capture, as detailed in the cost analysis section of this report.
 • To further minimize water consumption, the design incorporates a steam-integrated CO₂
 compression system, in which boiler feed water is used to generate steam instead of relying on
 water-cooled interstage compressors. This configuration eliminates the need for cooling tower
 makeup water but results in higher interstage temperatures and a corresponding 33% increase in
 compression power demand.
 • The inclusion of air-cooled heat exchangers, which require approximately four acres of space,
 necessitated relocating the capture facility from its originally identified location closer to the main
 plant. The proposed site is a 15-acre greenfield plot south of the New Braunfels facility, situated
 across the road from the cement plant. Flue gas ducting will be routed over existing rail spurs and
 the roadway to connect the kiln flue-gas streams to the capture plant.
 • The total direct costs are dominated by the air cooling system, accounting for 27.3% of the direct
 costs. The absorber column, which is traditionally the largest cost item, now accounts for 9.3% of
 the total cost. CO2 conditioning and compression accounts for ~5% of the total cost.
 • The cost of CO₂ capture could be substantially lower at sites where cooling water is readily
 available.
 • The total annual variable operating costs were estimated at $35.8 million. In addition, fuel and
 purchased power costs were estimated at $61.6 million annually. Total annualized operating costs
 were estimated to be $109 million, resulting in a breakeven CO₂ capture cost of $101.6 per tonne
 of CO₂.

 113

 • The RTI NAS CO₂ capture plant requires 46 MW of electrical power and approximately 610
 MMBtu/hr of natural gas for steam generation. The energy savings achieved through the NAS
 process’s higher CO₂ regeneration pressure are partially offset by the use of a heat-integrated CO₂
 compression train, implemented due to limited cooling water availability.
 • The current design employs an acid wash system to control emissions by converting residual
 amines to amine-sulfate salts, resulting in waste generation and higher amine makeup costs. RTI
 International has since developed an amine recovery process that regenerates and recycles ≥95%
 of the parent amine. Incorporation of amine recovery is expected to reduce CO₂ capture costs
 (excluding transport and storage) to $93.0 per tonne.
 • The FEED study did not identify any major project risks. The solvent reclamation process, which
 remains under development, was not included in the current design. Integration of the solvent
 reclamation system is planned for the detailed design phase.
The FEED study identified several opportunities for cost reduction and performance improvement that
could further lower the overall cost of CO₂ capture. The following actions are recommended for the next
project phase:
 • Amine Recovery: Develop the detailed design and cost estimate for integrating the amine recovery
 process from the acid wash system to reduce chemical consumption and operating costs.
 • Cogeneration (CHP) Integration: Investigate the potential for co-generation of steam and power
 to reduce electricity demand from the grid and improve overall energy efficiency.
 • Increased CO2 concentration in the flue gas: Assess options minimizing false air ingress to
 reduce flue gas flowrate, increase CO₂ concentration, and improve capture plant economics.
 • Solvent Reclamation: Complete development of the solvent reclamation system and incorporate
 it into the plant design to support long-term operational reliability and cost efficiency.

 114

1.0 GENERAL INFORMATION

  1.1 INTRODUCTION
 1.1.1 OVERVIEW
 The Project Design Basis is intended to supply the basic information and Owner requested
 items which are required in the preparation of a Front End Engineering Design (FEED)
 package that will support the development of a +/- 15% estimate. This document contains
 information on project execution, economics, utility characteristics, environmental
 regulations and requirements, and equipment design details that require input from the
 Owner to ensure that the facilities as designed will meet project requirements. In some
 instances, actual values have not yet been determined but are required for design. These
 values will be shown in red to represent an early phase engineering assumption and must
 be updated in the next phase of engineering.

 1.1.2 NAME AND LOCATION
 Client: RTI, INTERNATIONAL
 Project Name: DE-FE0032220 CARBON CAPTURE PLANT FEED
 STUDY FOR CEMENT MANUFACTURING
 Project Location: BALCONES PLANT, NEW BRAUNFELS, TEXAS
 Logo(s):

 1.1.3 BACKGROUND
 Research Triangle Institute International is developing a non-aqueous Solvent (NAS) CO2
 capture process as an advanced, next generation post combustion capture technology for
 CO2 removal from power generation and industrial flue gas streams. The core of the NAS
 process has been the development of a family of CO2 selective NASs that have the potential
 to substantially reduce thermal regeneration demand associated with solvent regeneration
 as compared to typically used solvents.
 RTI has received funding for a carbon capture study from an FOA (Funding Opportunity
 Announcement) from the DOE (Department of Energy) FECM (Office of Fossil Energy and
 Carbon Management). The FOA is for carbon capture R&D for natural gas and industrial
 point sources and front-end engineering design studies for carbon capture systems at
 industrial facilities and natural gas plants. The OCCCM’s (Office of Clean Coal and Carbon
 Management) mission is to drive R&D and adoption of advanced, affordable, and reliable
 energy systems and products from fossil resources and carbon-neutral or set-negative

 122
 Page 5 of 31

              KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-PM-G-PDB-001                              Rev.: B

 emissions by “advancing technologies to cost-effectively capture carbon and securely store
 carbon across the economy”.
 As with other CO2 capture systems, RTI’s NAS CO2 capture process utilizes an absorber
 tower, contacting cooled flue gas with solvent to capture CO2. The RTI process absorber
 differs in design in that it includes the need for interstage coolers. Once through the
 absorber, flue gas is washed to remove amine and may be further treated (and/or treated
 in advance of the absorber) to remove other undesirable emissions (particulates, sulfur,
 NOx, etc.). CO2-rich solvent from the absorber tower has the CO2 stripped in a regenerator
 and the CO2-lean solvent recirculates to the absorber. Stripped CO2 is treated and
 compressed as needed for export.
 RTI’s approved study is the evaluation of the industrial scale application of their CO2 capture
 technology to an existing concrete plant, the CEMEX Balcones Cement plant in New
 Braunfels, Texas. The study’s basis is the installation of the RTI CO2 capture system on the
 exhaust gas from the two cement kilns which have CO2 emissions of 1.6MM metric tonnes
 per year. In addition, as the steam heat required for the CO2 plant will require the
 installation of a natural gas boiler system, its flue gas CO2 will also be captured. Total CO2
 capture is expected to be 95% or greater of that produced. Water availability is limited so
 process cooling will have to be optimized. CO2 products will be compressed to meet end-
 use specifications. CO2 product end use (sequestration, EOR, etc.) will be determined in
 future phases.

1.1.4 SUMMARY OF SCOPE
 KBR will support RTI with a FEED study, beginning in May 2023 and to be complete by
 December 2024. Per the DOE FOA requirements, KBR will develop a Basic Design Package
 of the proposed CO2 capture plant including integration points with the cement plant,
 process flow diagrams, heat and material balances, sized equipment lists, required utilities
 descriptions, etc. Finished deliverables include the typical list for FEED, including P&IDs,
 equipment layouts, 3D model, material take-off lists, etc., and a class 3 capital estimate (+/-
 15%). CEMEX will provide the required site information. KBR’s battery limits include the
 carbon capture plant’s CO2 supply piping from the three inputs (two kilns and boiler). As
 part of the DOE FOA application requirements, RTI has a number of other related reports
 to submit including technoeconomic, business case, lifecycle, and EH&S and Risk analyses.

 kilns from the existing cement plant + approx. 15% CO2
 capture for the new NG boiler)

9.0 DESIGN PHILOSOPHIES

 9.1 DESIGN PRESSURE AND DESIGN TEMPERATURE (DP/DT) PHILOSOPHY
 9.1.1 DESIGN PRESSURE OF EQUIPMENT
 Equipment will have a design pressure that is 1.1 times greater (or a minimum of 50 psig
 greater) than the maximum pressure provided in the heat and material balance.

 9.1.2 DESIGN TEMPERATURE OF EQUIPMENT
 Design temperature is calculated at design pressure (pdes) conditions, which is the highest
 pressure at which mechanical strength can be guaranteed. In the absence of any other
 specific criteria, design temperature equals to the maximum operating temperature added
 with 50 °F (T design = T oper. max. + 50 °F). All equipment and piping which are to be

 143
                                                                                     Page 26 of 31

                    KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-PM-G-PDB-001                             Rev.: B

 steamed out shall additionally be designed for steam out temperature of 267 °F calculated
 at corresponding saturated steam pressure of 39.7 psia(abs).

 9.2 SPARING PHILOSOPHY
       9.2.1 PUMPS
 Unless otherwise agreed, all pumps with immediate influence on the process must have a
 spare. Sparing of pumps with delayed influence on the process will be agreed on a case-by-
 case basis.

 9.2.2 COMPRESSORS
 Owing to the high reliability of centrifugal compressors, a spare will typically not be
 specified. Full spare capacity will be specified for reciprocating compressors, i.e. typical
 sparing philosophy is 2 × 100. CO2 compressors are considered 1 X 100% for this phase.

 9.3 ISOLATION PHILOSOPHY
 9.3.1 ISOLATION
           Refer to document DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001.

1. INTRODUCTION

 PROJECT OVERVIEW
 RTI International, with CEMEX Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., are participating in a joint effort to
 perform a Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) study for carbon dioxide (CO2) capture from
 cement flue gas. The FEED study will evaluate the integration of a CO2 capture system at
 the Balcones Cement Plant at New Braunfels, Texas.
 The CO2 capture plant is designed to remove at least 95% of CO2 from the flue-gas from the
 Balcones cement plant kilns K1 and K2, and the associated natural gas boiler for steam
 generation. The captured CO2 has a purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, polished to meet
 pipeline specifications, and compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara) to be made ready for
 sequestration. The capture plant (ISBL) consists of an integrated direct contact cooler (DCC)
 and SO2 polisher, a flue gas blower, an absorption column with intercoolers, a regeneration
 column, associated pumps, storage and receiver vessels, and a CO2 compression,
 dehydration, and polishing train.
 SUMMARY OF UNITS
 UNIT 01 – CO2 CAPTURE
 This unit contains the Direct Contact Cooler, the Absorber with integrated water wash and
 acid wash sections, the Lean/Rich Solvent Exchange, and the Regenerator. The Direct
 Contact Cooler cools the incoming flue gas while also reducing the SO2 concentration from
 ~2-5 ppmv in the incoming flue gas down to <1 ppmv using an aqueous solution of sodium
 hydroxide (NaOH) to neutralize the SO2. The CO2 Absorber is a packed column containing
 structured packing. After absorption, the treated flue gas enters two water wash sections
 followed by an acid wash section, where the amine concentration is reduced to less than 1
 ppm. The Regeneration Column strips the CO2 from the solvent so the solvent can be
 recycled back into the Absorber. The captured CO2 from absorption and regeneration is
 then sent to the compression, polishing, and dehydration unit.
 UNIT 02 – CO2 COMPRESSION, DEHYDRATION, AND POLISHING
 The CO2 product gas from the Regenerator is sent to the CO2 compression unit. The CO2
 stream is compressed to 2212 psia (152.5 bara) and conditioned to meet pipeline
 specifications for transport.
 UTILITY SYSTEMS
 These units contain all the physical equipment (requiring plot space) used in generating and
 handling utility streams. These units include:
  • Unit 11 – Raw, Potable, and Demineralized Water Systems
  • Unit 12 – Boiler and Steam Systems
  • Unit 14 – Solvent and Chemical Storage
  • Unit 15 – Cooling Water System

 153
 Page 4 of 12

                 KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                         Rev.: E

• Unit 16 – Air/Nitrogen Systems
• Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
• Unit 20 – Stack
• Unit 22 – Utility Headers
 UNIT 11 – RAW WATER, POTABLE WATER AND DEMINERALIZED WATER
 TREATMENT
 This unit takes in process water and potable water and produces and distributes
 demineralized water, service water, and fire water.
 UNIT 12 – BOILER AND STEAM SYSTEM
 The Boiler and Steam System removes dissolved oxygen from the demineralized water
 to produce and supply BFW to the BFW header. The unit also generates and distributes
 LP steam.
 UNIT 14 – SOLVENT AND CHEMICAL STORAGE
 This unit holds and distributes H2SO4, NAS, and NaOH solution for use within the facility.
 UNIT 15 – COOLING WATER SYSTEM
 The Cooling Water System consists of a hybrid cooling tower, chemical treatment
 package, and pumps to supply cooling water to users.
 UNIT 16 – AIR AND NITROGEN SYSTEMS
 The Air and Inert Systems handle compression, drying, and storage of the facility’s
 supply of plant air, instrument air, and nitrogen.
 UNIT 18 – WASTEWATER TREATMENT PLANT
 This unit collects and treats wastewater effluent streams from throughout the facility.
 It also prepares process water from the DCC and process pond for use as service water.
 UNIT 20 – STACK
 The stack handles treated flue gas and the relief loads from the facility.
 UNIT 22 – UTILITY HEADERS
 This unit contains all interconnecting utility header piping that links the utility
 equipment with each unit that requires that utility.

3. UNIT 01 – CO2 CAPTURE SYSTEM

 DIRECT CONTACT COOLER
 The flue gas stream from Kiln 1, Kiln 2, and the Natural Gas Boiler enters the Feed Direct
 Contact Cooler, or DCC (V-0111), where it is cooled and SO2 concentration is reduced.
 Recirculated and cooled water from the DCC bottom enters the top of the DCC to cool the
 gas. The Feed Scrubber Wash Circulation Pump (P-0111 A/B/C) discharges the excess
 condensed water, and most of the remaining water is recirculated to the top of the bed
 while a small fraction of the stream splits off to be sent to the Wastewater Treatment Plant
 (WWTP). The DCC reduces the acid gas components in the flue gas, thus allowing the use
 of more cost-effective materials of construction in the blower. When the process water is
 recycled, water must go through the DCC Circulation Filter (F-0111), the Feed Scrubber
 Wash Air Cooler (E-0112), and the Feed Scrubber Wash Cooler (E-0111) before it returns to
 the top of the DCC. Cooled flue gas exits through the top of the DCC to the Flue Gas Blower
 (K-0111 A/B) and then advances to the Absorber Column (C-0121).
 ABSORBER COLUMN
 Cooled flue gas from the Blower enters the bottom of the Absorber and flows upward,
 counter-current to the CO2 lean solvent that is fed into the top of the absorber section from
 the Lean Solvent Air Cooler (E-0142) to remove CO2 from the gas stream. Gases exiting the
 top of the Absorber Column are vented to safe location via a stack. Provisions for a
 continuous emission monitoring system (CEMS) are included in the design.
 The solvent is collected and sent to the Absorber Interstage Surge Tanks (T-0121, T-0122),
 from which the Absorber Interstage Circulation Pumps (P-0121 A/B/C, P-0122 A/B/C) send
 the NAS solvent stream through an air cooler to remove the heat of absorption from the
 CO2. The Absorber Interstage Air Coolers (E-0121, E-0122) cool the solvent before it
 continues back into the column above the next stage. At the bottom of the column, the
 now CO2 rich solvent is discharged by the Rich Solvent Pump (P-0124 A/B/C) to both the
 Lean/Rich Solvent Heat Exchanger (E-0141) and to the Regenerator Column.
 ABSORBER WASH SECTION
 After the packed absorber stages, the gas reaches the wash section integrated into the top
 of the Absorber Column. This section is where trace amounts of amines are removed. The
 water wash section removes the bulk of the amines, and the condensed water, along with
 the amines, is returned to the top of the Absorber.
 One washing stage brings the gas in contact with wash water from the Activated Carbon
 Beds that has been scrubbed of amine. Wash water passes through this stage, then collects
 and enters the Absorber Overhead Wash Water Surge Drum. The water is pumped out of
 the drum by Absorber Overhead Wash Water Pump (P-0132 A/B/C) into the Absorber
 Overhead Wash Water Cooler (E-0132 In Wash Water Cooler, the wash water transfers heat

 156
 Page 7 of 12

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                     Rev.: E

 to a stream of cooling water before it enters the Activated Carbon Beds (V-0134). The beds
 are lined up so that once one bed is loaded with amine, the spent bed can be taken off-line
 while the others can continue the flow of wash water back to the Absorber.
 The final stage of the column is the acid washing stage. A stream of wash water with
 sulfuric acid enters the top of the Absorber Column above the packing. The acid stream
 leaves the column after passing through the packing and enters the Absorber Overhead
 Acid Wash Surge Drum (V-0133) where sulfuric acid is added from Unit 14 Sulfuric Acid
 Storage. The treated flue gas that leaves the top of the Absorber Column is sent to the
 Stack.
LEAN/RICH SOLVENT EXCHANGE
  CO2 lean solvent from the Regenerator Column (C-0151) enters the Lean Solvent Surge Tank
  (T-0141) along with a makeup stream of NAS. CO2 lean solvent coming into the Surge Tank
  first passes through the Lean/Rich Solvent Heat Exchanger (E-0141), in which heat is
  transferred between the CO2 lean solvent and the CO2 rich solvent from the Rich Solvent
  Pump (P-0124 A/B/C). The Surge Tank effluent discharged by the Lean Solvent Pump (P-
  0141 A/B/C) enters the Lean Solvent Air Cooler (E-0142). The CO2 lean solvent then
  proceeds to the Absorber Column, with part of the flow passing through the Lean Solvent
  Particulate Filter (F-0141) and the Lean Solvent Carbon Filter (F-0142) during normal
  operation.
REGENERATOR
 A slipstream of the CO2 rich solvent from the Absorber Column enters the Regeneration
 Column (C-0151) and is distributed onto the upper packing stage along with reflux from the
 top of the column. The remainder of the CO2 rich solvent passes through the Lean/Rich Heat
 Exchanger (E-0141) and enters the Regeneration Column at the top of the lower packing
 stage. A portion of the lean solvent from the bottom of the Regeneration Column is sent
 back to the Lean Solvent Surge Tank (T-0141) via the Lean/Rich Solvent Heat Exchanger.
 The remaining Regenerator Column bottoms is sent through the Regenerator Reboiler (E-
 0152 A/B/C/D), which uses a flow of LP steam to provide energy for regeneration. The
 reboilers draw feed from the chimney tray of the Regenerator Column. The vapor from the
 reboilers combines and is sent back to the column. The gas that reaches the top of the
 column first enters the Regenerator Overhead Condenser, after which it enters the
 Regenerator Overhead Condensate Drum (V-0151). From the Condensate Drum, pressure-
 controlled flow of captured CO2 is sent to the Unit 02 CO2 Compressor Package, and liquid
 reflux is recycled back to the Regenerator Column through the Regenerator Reflux Pump
 (P-0151 A/B). The vent line downstream of the pressure-control valve sends the captured
 CO2 to the stack if there is any upset in the downstream unit (compressor package).

 157
 Page 8 of 12

                    KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                      Rev.: E

4. UNIT 02 – CO2 COMPRESSION, DEHYDRATION, AND POLISHING

 The CO2 Compressor Package (K-0211) supplied by vendor compresses captured CO2 from the Unit
 01 Regenerator Column followed by polishing and TEG dehydration between the stages to meet
 the CO2 product specifications. This package is comprised of a five-stage integrally geared
 compressor, heat exchangers, and suction/knock-out drums. The compressor is able to operate at
 relatively high temperatures, allowing the intercoolers to utilize a stream of BFW to recover heat
 from process gas and then send a return stream of LP steam to the utility header. O2 polishing and
 TEG dehydration and steps occur as intermediate steps between stages 3 and 4 of the Compressor
 Package.
 The O2 Polishing Reactor (V-0221) is fed with CO2 directly from stage 3 of the CO2 Compressor
 Package and contains catalyst that reactors with the feed CO2 gas and hydrogen to reduce O2
 concentration down to < 10 ppmv to meet the specifications for pipeline transport. The hydrogen
 supplied to the reactor is generated by electrolysis reaction, which is done in the vendor supplied
 Electrolyzer Package (A-0232), which utilizes demineralized water from OSBL.
 The vendor-supplied TEG Dehydration Package (A-0221) is fed with CO2 from the Polishing Reactor.
 A TEG makeup stream feeds into the package, and a TEG waste stream exits to the utility header.
 The polished, dehydrated CO2 stream then re-enters into stage 4 of the Compressor Package to go
 through further cooling and compression and then is sent out through the pipeline.

5. UNIT 11 – RAW WATER SYSTEM

 The Water Treatment Unit contains the following main sections:
 • Raw Water System
 • Demineralized Water System
 • Potable Water System
 RAW WATER SYSTEM
 Treated wastewater from the Wastewater Treatment Package (A-1801) is sent to the
 Service Water Tank (T-1101) along with an additional supply raw well water from the pond
 via Process Water Pump (P-1801 A/B) as needed. From the tank, water is pumped by the
 Service Water Pump (P-1101 A/B) into the service water header, to the Unit 15 Cooling
 Water Package as makeup, and to the Demineralized Water Package. The Fire Water Pump
 (P-1102 A/B) supplies water from the Service Water Tank to the fire water header in case
 of an emergency. During normal operation, pressure in the fire water lines is maintained by
 the Fire Water Jockey Pump (P-1103 A/B), which also draws from the Service Water Tank.
 Both Fire Water and Jockey Pumps will be in compliance with NFPA 20. One Fire Water
 Pump will be diesel driven; the other Fire Water Pump will be motor driven. The tank outlet
 nozzle to the Service Water Pump is located at a sufficient height to ensure adequate fire
 water supply.

 158
 Page 9 of 12

                  KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                      Rev.: E

 DEMINERALIZED WATER SYSTEM
 The Demineralized Water Package (A-1101) removes inorganic salts from service water to
 reduce the likelihood of corrosion in the Deaerator (V-1201). Neutralization waste and
 demineralizer backwash from this package are intermittently sent to Unit 18 Waste Water
 Treatment. The demineralized water continues to the Demineralized Water Tank (T-1102),
 where it is further pumped to the Unit 12 Deaerator and the Unit 02 Electrolyzer Package
 by the Demineralized Water Pump (P-1104 A/B). If needed, demineralized water may also
 be sent to Unit 01 Direct Contact Cooler. The Demineralized Water Tank utilizes a pressure
 control valve to moderate nitrogen blanketing from the nitrogen header. A flow-controlled
 slip stream of demineralized water is intermittently recycled back to the Demineralized
 Water Package to be used as backwash/neutralization water.
 POTABLE WATER SYSTEM
 Potable water from the offsite municipality is stored in the Potable Water Tank (T-1103)
 and then pumped by the Potable Water Pump (P-1105 A/B) to the potable water header
 for distribution to all users.

6. UNIT 12 – BOILER AND STEAM SYSTEM

 BOILER SYSTEM
 A stream of demineralized water from the Unit 11 Demineralized Water Tank flows into the
 Deaerator (V-1201) after receiving a chemical injection for oxygen scavenging as a corrosion
 preventative from the BFW Chemical Package (A-1202). Steam condensate and a pressure-
 controlled stream of LP steam is introduced to the Deaerator from the LP steam header to
 strip the unwanted dissolved gases from the feed water. The unwanted gases are vented
 to the atmosphere at a safe location. The BFW is pumped out of the Deaerator by the BFW
 Pump (P-1201 A/B), where it is then sent to BFW users and directly to the Natural Gas Boiler
 Package (A-1203). The BFW Chemical Package (A-1201) is injected into this stream to
 control pH and scaling.
 Blowdown from Unit 12 Natural Gas Boiler is sent to the Blowdown Drum (V-1202), from
 which flashed LP steam is sent to the LP steam header. Blowdown liquids from the bottoms
 are removed and sent to Unit 18 Primary Wastewater Treatment.
 Steam condensate from the LP condensate header is sent to the Condensate Flash Drum
 (V-1203). Flashed gases are vented to atmosphere, and steam condensate collected at the
 bottom is sent to the Deaerator through the Condensate Pump (P-1203 A/B).
 LP STEAM SYSTEM
 For the production of LP steam, BFW from the Deaerator and a stream of natural gas are
 fed to the Natural Gas Boiler Package. The LP steam obtained from boiler enters the LP

 159
                                                                                     Page 10 of 12

                    KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                       Rev.: E

 steam header. The blowdown from the Boiler Package is sent to the Unit 12 Blowdown
 Drum, and flue gas is sent to the Unit 01 Feed Direct Contact Cooler.

7. UNIT 14 – SOLVENT AND CHEMICAL STORAGE UTILITIES

 SOLVENT STORAGE SYSTEM
 AMINE FILL IS SUPPLIED BY TRUCK DELIVERY THROUGH AN UNLOADING CONNECTION INTO
 THE SOLVENT STORAGE TANK (T-1401). AMINE SOLVENT IS DISCHARGED FROM THE TANK
 BY THE SOLVENT TRANSFER PUMP (P-1401 A/B) THROUGH THE SOLVENT SUMP FILTER (F-
 1401) AND THEN CARRIES ON TO THE UNIT 01 LEAN SOLVENT SURGE TANK. CAUSTIC
 STORAGE SYSTEM
 The caustic solution is delivered by truck and discharged to the Caustic Storage Tank (T-
 1402) through an unloading connection. The solution is then sent to the Feed Direct Contact
 Cooler to neutralize SO2 by the Caustic Transfer Pump (P-1403 A/B).
 SULFURIC ACID STORAGE SYSTEM
 Sulfuric acid is supplied by a truck unloading connection to the Sulfuric Acid Storage Tank
 (T-1403). Sulfuric acid is pumped from the tank by the Sulfuric Acid Transfer Pump (P-1404
 A/B) to the acid washing stage of the Unit 01 Absorber Column.

8. UNIT 15 – COOLING WATER SYSTEM

The Cooling Water Package (A-1501) is supplied by vendor. The hybrid cooling tower operates using
makeup water from the Unit 11 Service Water Tank. The cooling water supply is sent to the utility
header, and cooling water blowdown is sent to Unit 18 Waste Water Treatment.

9. UNIT 16 – AIR/NITROGEN SYSTEMS

 AIR COMPRESSOR SYSTEM
 Air is compressed by the Primary Air Compressor Package (K-1601) and Secondary Air
 Compressor Package (K-1602). Both compressors are supplied by vendor and controlled by
 a split header pressure controller. The Air Distribution System is designed to close the plant
 air header control valve and start the Secondary Air Compressor after the instrument air
 header pressure drops below the minimum requirement. The air from the compressor
 packages enters the Air Receiver (V-1601), which also serves as emergency instrument air
 supply to safely shut down the plant in the event of a total loss of compressed air. The
 compressed air then feeds into the plant air header and the Instrument Air Dryer Package
 (A-1602) to reduce the dew point of the compressed air. The dried, compressed air then
 joins the instrument air header.

 160
                                                                                        Page 11 of 12

                      KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PRS-001                      Rev.: E

 NITROGEN GAS SYSTEM
 The inert gas used in the facility is nitrogen. The Nitrogen Generation Skid (A-1601) supplied
 by the vendor intermittently provides nitrogen for high pressure nitrogen users. The high
 pressure nitrogen at 145 psia is let down through a pressure controller valve to a low
 pressure nitrogen at 45 psia.

10. UNIT 18 – WASTE WATER TREATMENT PLANT

The Waste Water Treatment Package (A-1801) provided by the vendor is intended to treat water from
both ISBL and OSBL areas. These streams include blowdown from the Unit 12 Blowdown Drum,
neutralization waste and backwash from the Unit 11 Demineralizer, Cooling Water Blowdown from
the Unit 15 Cooling Water Package, acid waste from the Acid Wash Pump (P-0133 A/B), process water
from Feed Direct Contact Cooler (V-0111), blowdown from the Electrolyzer Package (A-0232),
intermittent aqueous phase stream from the Unit 20 Knock Out Drum, and contact sewer drains from
the facility. Treated water is recycled to the Unit 11 Service Water Tank (T-1101) or discharged to
battery limit.

11. UNIT 20 – STACK

This unit manages the relief system and depressurizing loads from the facility. The stack is not expected
to engage during normal operation. In the event of a relief or depressurization scenario, gases are
routed to the stack through the main relief header.

Relief gases from the relief header enter the KO Drum (V-2001), which protects the Unit 20 stack
system from liquid carryover. Gases from the top of the KO Drum are sent to the Stack (A-2001), which
ties into the treated flue gas stream from the Unit 01 Absorber Column stack. Therefore, the discharge
from the Absorber Column stack is the only emission point. CEMS monitors the effluent from the
Absorber Column stack in Unit 01 and therefore is able to monitor the discharges from both Unit 01
and Unit 20. During plant upset in Unit 02, the captured CO2 is also sent to stack.

The aqueous-phase liquid present in the KO Drum is intermittently discharged to Unit 18 Wastewater
Treatment by the KO Drum Aqueous Phase Pump (P-2002) when the water level in the drum is deemed
sufficiently high by a level control on the drum.

1. NOTES

  1) BFW requirement is based on the steam generation rate in the Natural Gas Boiler Package, CO2
 Compression Package, and 3% blowdown from the boiler.
  2) Cooling Tower recirculation rate is calculated based on the max case exchanger heat duties and
 temperature rise of 12.6 °F.
  3) The Instrument and Plant Air requirements are high-level estimates to be refined during later
 phases of engineering.
  4) Assumed 100 people on site with average usage of 3.9 ft3/person/day. Number of people to be
 updated at client's discretion in a later phase of engineering. Also assumed two safety shower
 and eye wash stations are operating simultaneously, consuming 183.6 ft3/hr of potable water
 each.
  5) Steam requirement for the Natural Gas Boiler Package is calculated based on the total facility
 steam demand minus the steam produced by the CO2 Compressor Package. Steam generator
 pressure is assumed to be 150 psig.
  6) Nitrogen rate requirement is a high-level estimate to be refined during later phases of
 engineering.

 1. GENERAL

 1.1 Scope

 1.1.1 This document (P50-1D) states the standard BR overpressure protection and vent philosophy.
 Overpressure protection is provided in accordance with this document, but is limited to the project scope as
 defined in the project contract and/or subsequent project documentation related to this subject.
 Overpressure protection is also limited to scenarios considered credible incidents identified in industry codes
 and standards. The Owner has the authority and responsibility to identify, document, and communicate to
 BR scenarios that are not to be considered credible for overpressure protection because other protective
 measures, such as administrative procedures or instrumentation, are sufficient to prevent the overpressure
 scenario. The Owner is responsible for ensuring that administrative procedures are followed and protective
 instrumentation is adequately maintained to provide sufficient overpressure protection. BR is not
 responsible for overpressure consequences that occur because these conditions are violated. Such special
 requirements shall be reviewed and approved by the Office of the Chief Engineer.

 1.1.2 The safety of personnel, the protection of equipment from damage due to overpressure, and the
 protection of the environment due to loss of containment are the basis for the designing, sizing, and selection
 of pressure relieving systems.

 1.1.3 All systems and pressure relief devices shall meet applicable codes, industry standards and practices,
 and related Owner job instructions.
 CONFIDENTIALITY NOTICE: This document contains information which is proprietary to BR, Inc. and/or its affiliates ( BR ). This
information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 2 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 1.1.4 The objective is to apply a systematic examination to all modes of operations and engineering
 intentions to protect the mechanical integrity of the equipment and piping systems based on all credible
 incidents. Provisions shall be made to contain or safely relieve any excessive pressures in the system.
 These provisions may include the utilization of an emergency shutdown and depressuring system at the
 discretion of the Owner.

 1.1.5 Statutory Requirements: BR and the Owner shall mutually determine the measures that must be
 taken to comply with all applicable governmental codes, regulations, ordinances, and rules, which shall be
 applied as a first precedence.

 1.1.6 The equipment and piping systems shall be designed, fabricated, tested, and assembled in
 accordance with the job specifications and shall be subject to the Supplier s quality assurance and control
 procedures, including third party inspection.

 1.1.7 The practices outlined in this document shall be followed for all process unit areas, including related
 utilities and offsites, and licensor and non licensor packages.

 1.1.8 Owner specifications or other practices, which represent a clearly hazardous condition to personnel or
 plant safety, shall be brought to the attention of BR Chief Engineer for review and resolution.

 1.1.9 The following defines BR s scope of responsibility for projects that require BR to provide purchasing
 input only. BR will purchase parts to be used for repair of existing relief valves and purchase new relief
 valves as replacement-in-kind for existing relief valves as indicated by our scope of work documents. In
 these specific situations, the BR scope of work will not include the following engineering activities or the
 engineering responsibility for any possible deficiencies in the existing system such as:

 a. Sizing of relief valves or selection of type suitable for application.
 b. Establishing the set pressures.
 c. Define the sizing scenarios.
 d. Overpressure protection.
 e. Checking sizing of existing flare header and resulting back pressures.
 f. Checking existing flare capacity.
 g. Any consideration of the materials being flared and their properties.
 h. Any evaluation or consideration of any needed mitigation measures (such as operating procedures or
 control systems) that may be required to assure the integrity of the systems.

 1.2 KBR Documents, Codes, and Industry Standards

 The design of the pressure relieving systems is based on the following codes, industry standards,
 specifications, and recommended practices. The applicable dates of issue including addenda, modifications,
 or additional requirements for the project are identified in the Project Design Data document.
