  2. CAUSES OF OVERPRESSURE

  2.1 General

  2.1.1 All equipment and piping systems must be protected when the internal or external pressure can
  exceed the design condition of the system due to an emergency, upset condition, operational error,
  instrument malfunction, or fire. Possible scenarios are discussed in API STD 521 and API 14E.

  2.1.2 All equipment items shall be protected from overpressure. This overpressure protection shall be
  provided by a pressure relief device system. A single pressure relief device system may be used to protect a
  group of equipment items (a separate pressure relief device system is not required for each equipment item)
  if there is no means of isolating the equipment from the pressure relief device system (see Paragraph 2.4.4)
  or if isolating the equipment from the pressure relief device system also removes the source of overpressure.
  Per ASME Section VIII Division 1, UG-140, overpressure protection may also be provided by system
  design. The decision to limit the pressure by system design is the responsibility of the user. If the pressure
  is self-limiting (e.g., pump shut-off less than downstream equipment MAWP), UG-140(a) shall be used with
  agreement from the Owner. If the pressure is not self-limiting, (e.g., high integrity instrumentation is used to
  replace or supplement relief devices for overpressure protection), UG-140(b) shall not be used unless
  directed by the Owner.

  2.1.3 For offshore production facilities, per API 14C, the safety system should provide two levels of
  protection. In general, the primary protection shall be a separate, safety designated, high pressure trip that
  will initiate actions to remove the high pressure source. The secondary protection shall be a pressure relief
  device.

  2.1.4 Pressure relieving devices are installed to ensure that a system or any of its components are not
  subjected to pressures, which exceed the allowable pressure accumulation per applicable Code
  requirements (e.g., ASME Boiler and Pressure Vessel Code Section I and Section VIII, ASME B31.1, and
  ASME B31.3).

  2.1.5 Any circumstance which reasonably constitutes an overpressure type hazard under the prevailing
  conditions shall be analyzed and evaluated. In support of this statement, it is assumed that the plant will be
  staffed by trained operators and that administrative procedures will be followed. For example, it is assumed
  that a trained operator may close a normally open valve by mistake, but will not close a valve designated as
  locked open without following the prescribed administrative procedures. Also, it is assumed that no willful
  sabotage occurs and that no one will perform an operation for the purpose of damaging equipment or
  harming the plant.

 260

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 4 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 2.2 Assumptions

 2.2.1 The simultaneous occurrence of two or more conditions which could result in overpressure is not to be
 considered if the causes are unrelated, i.e., if no process, mechanical, or electrical commonality exists
 among the causes.

 2.2.2 Control response and the automatic start up of equipment are not to be considered as a substitute for
 pressure relieving devices for equipment protection. As a general rule, final overpressure protection shall be
 provided by means of a mechanical pressure relieving device.

 2.2.3 Equipment not affected by a utility failure being evaluated is to be considered to remain in normal
 operation.

 2.2.4 Flow rates through the equipment and other conditions during the emergency are to be assumed to
 remain at the normal rates and conditions occurring prior to the upset condition unless the particular
 emergency case under consideration alters the flow rate or conditions. In case of fire, the flow is to be
 assumed to stop and be contained within a defined system.

 2.2.5 The possibility of an operator inadvertently opening or closing any one valve or taking any incorrect
 action in the wrong sequence or at the wrong time must be considered. However, block valves, electric
 switches, and other equipment items that are locked or car sealed in the safe position are not to be
 considered involved in any case of operator error.

 2.3 Principal Causes

    2.3.1 General

 The following describes some common principal causes of overpressure, which shall be analyzed to
 determine the individual relieving flow rates for pressure relieving devices. Also, clarification of the analysis
 method and type of overpressure protection device to be used are provided if applicable. The list is not
 intended to be all inclusive, but is intended to serve as a guide.

 2.3.2 Power Failure (Steam or Electric)

 a.Plant-wide, plant section-wide, and individual equipment failures (i.e., total and partial failures) shall be
 considered. Equipment which is not affected by the failure of concern is to be considered to remain in
 normal operation. Electrical one-line diagrams shall be examined to determine common mode and related
 failures.

 b.A maximum credit of 25 percent cooling duty should be allowed for the effect of natural draft in the event of
 a loss of an air cooler due to power failure unless specifically advised otherwise by the Owner. The amount
 of credit is affected by the type of air cooler design.

 2.3.3 Cooling Water Failure

 a. Total or partial loss of cooling water, depending on pump installation, individual exchangers, and power
 source of drivers shall be considered. Partial credit may be taken if the cooling water circulating system
 consists of two or more pumps in parallel and continuous service with drivers having independent power
 sources. Generally, if only one of two 50% pumps is considered to fail, 50% cooling water flow is
 assumed to continue.

 CONFIDENTIALITY NOTICE: This document contains information which is proprietary to BR, Inc. and/or its affiliates ( BR ). This
information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 5 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 b. The pump(s) with the unaffected driver(s) are to be assumed to continue to operate. Credit shall not be
 taken if the unaffected pump(s) are on standby because standby devices are not considered totally
 reliable. Credit shall not be taken if the unaffected pump(s) can be removed from service for
 maintenance and the remaining operating pumps are dependent on the same power source.

 c. Total cooling water failure must be considered simultaneous with power failure if the common pump
 power source is lost during a power failure. The loss of individual cooling water supply to exchangers,
 compressors, etc., shall be considered.

 2.3.4 Column Reflux or Pump-around Failure

 Loss of column reflux, whether supplied by the overhead circuit, sidestream reflux, and/or by the top tower
 pump-around, shall be considered. Other facilities associated with column operation are to be considered to
 continue to function as designed.

 2.3.5 Open External Fire

 a. It is to be assumed that all fluid flow to the equipment has stopped and at the time of stoppage, the
 equipment contains a normal liquid level. Only the internally wetted portion of the equipment, elevated
 7.6 m (25 ft) or less above the source of the flame, shall be considered in determining the wetted
 surface area.

 b. The source of flame base elevation shall be considered any level on which a substantial spill or pool fire
 can accumulate and be sustained. Portions of equipment higher than 7.6 m (25 ft) above the flame
 base elevation, including that portion of liquid full vessels, shall be excluded. The liquid hold up on
 tower trays and the liquid inventory of any integral reboiler shall be included for determining the wetted
 surface up to 7.6 m (25 ft).

 c. Unless a finite curbed area is established, a fire circle of 21.3 m (70 ft) diameter shall be used to
 determine the amount of equipment affected by a single pool fire. If no source of a pool fire can be
 established or if it can be established that the excess flow caused by the fire is relieved through the
 process without causing an overpressure condition, and this flow passage cannot be shut in during
 normal plant operation or because of fire effects, then no fire relief protection needs to be provided. Jet
 fires are generally not considered except under special circumstances.

 d. If the only time an equipment item can be shut in is during isolation for maintenance purposes, fire
 relief protection is not required if administrative controls ensure that the equipment item is vented and
 drained immediately when removed from service.
 e. Equipment which normally operates dry must be evaluated for the vapor expansion fire case.

 f. Equipment which is located entirely above the 7.6 m (25 ft) elevation limit may still be provided with
 some degree of relief protection from external fire. The impact of fire on such equipment is to be
 considered on a case by case basis.

 g. The extent of the pool fires underneath equipment should be minimized (e.g., by sloping the paving and
 ground away from the equipment, not locating other equipment directly underneath, etc.). If exposure to
 fire from adjacent equipment provides significant relief loads, water sprays are to be considered.
 However, no reduction in relief load can be taken because of the addition of water sprays.

 h. The insulation system for an equipment item is to be considered individually. Credit shall be taken for
 equipment insulation if the requirements of insulation integrity during a fire are met, including the ability
 to withstand fire hose stream impact.

 CONFIDENTIALITY NOTICE: This document contains information which is proprietary to BR, Inc. and/or its affiliates ( BR ). This
information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 6 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  i. Piping and piping components are generally not considered to require separate protection for fire, but
 shall be included if they are part of a group of interconnected equipment items protected by a single
 pressure relieving system.

  2.3.6 Refrigerant Failure

  In addition to a total refrigeration loss, a loss of refrigerant to individual exchangers shall be considered.

  2.3.7 Blocked Outlet

  Normally, equipment and piping should be designed for the maximum possible pressure resulting from a
  single blocked outlet. However, if the accidental closure of a block valve subjects the equipment or piping to
  overpressure, relief protection must be provided. If the closure of a single block valve during normal
  operation causes a significant plant upset, the use of a locked open (LO) or car sealed open (CSO) valve
  may be considered necessary for operational purposes and may provide a relief path that replaces the
  requirement for a relief device. CSO or LO valves should not be specified to replace relief devices, but can
  be considered to provide an open relief path if specified for operational purposes.

  2.3.8 Centrifugal Pump Shut in

  A pressure relief valve is not required to protect the equipment in the discharge system of a centrifugal pump
  from a blocked in case if the equipment design pressure is greater than or equal to the pump shut in
  pressure because the pressure is self-limiting. Per ASME Section VIII Division 1, UG-140(a), unless there
  is an additional scenario that requires a relief device, the Owner must agree that the pressure is limited by
  system design and that the Owner will follow the proper administrative and documentation procedures. If the
  Owner does not agree to follow the requirements of this section of the Code, then a minimum size pressure
  relief valve is required. In addition, the suction lead piping and the pump suction connections should be
  checked for the discharge pressure rating. Normal variations in liquid gravity, maximum suction pressure
  and temperature, and maximum continuous speed shall be considered.

  2.3.9 Instrument Air/Electric Supply Failure

  In the event of a plant wide instrument air or instrument electric power failure, all affected valves shall be
  considered to fail in their specified failure positions. Instrument air and instrument electric power are
  considered to be two unrelated sources of energy. Thus, simultaneous failure shall not be considered a
  viable contingency.

  2.3.10 Control Valve Failure

  a. The failure mode (air to open, close, or last position) on loss of motive power shall be evaluated for each
 valve. Each control valve should have its fail-safe characteristics properly established to minimize the
 hazard to plant operation.

  b. The effect of a mechanical failure of the control valve shall always be considered when evaluating the
 need for overpressure protection of systems associated with the valve.

  c. Control valves with a bypass (as required per BR Standard P40-1D): Provisions for overpressure
 protection of a system downstream of a control valve station with a bypass shall be based on the flow
 rate equivalent of either the control valve or the bypass valve being fully open and the other being fully
 closed.

 263

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 7 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 2.3.11 Inadvertent Valve Opening

 The inadvertent opening of any valve from a source of higher pressure shall be considered, unless
 provisions are made for locking or car sealing the valve closed.

 2.3.12 Check Valve Leakage or Failure

 Total failure of a single check valve or leakage through more than one check valve in series shall be
 considered as a viable overpressure contingency in systems with high pressure differential (defined for this
 possible scenario as the high pressure side operating pressure greater than the low pressure side hydrotest
 pressure), pressure surges, or dirty service. Two check valves in series shall be considered sufficient to
 eliminate the total failure scenario, but shall not be considered sufficient to eliminate the leakage scenario.

 2.3.13 Hydraulic Expansion

 a. Use of pressure relief valves for thermal expansion should be minimized. Within process areas, the
 requirement for thermal pressure relief valves shall be evaluated for piping, pressure vessels, and
 equipment, which can be manually blocked in while filled with liquid and subsequently heated by heat
 tracing, process, or atmospheric effects. Such equipment includes the cold side of heat exchangers that
 can be blocked in.

 b. Documented administrative control procedures stipulating that the system is to be drained whenever it is
 blocked in are an acceptable alternative solution to prevent overpressure due to hydraulic expansion.

 c. Thermal relief shall be provided for piping outside of the process areas if the piping design pressure can
 be exceeded as a result of a blocked-in condition. Piping outside of the process areas is considered not
 to be drained when taken out of service because of remote location and lack of facilities to contain the
 liquid volume from long pipe runs.

 2.3.14 Pressure Transients

 a. Normally, a pressure transient analysis is not to be provided for liquid filled systems in process plants.
 In general, these systems should not have quick closing valves and overpressure protection for a
 pressure transient should not be required. For upstream oil and gas production facilities (offshore or
 onshore), a transient analysis is normally required for large liquid filled systems such as seawater and
 firewater systems.

 b. Utility distribution systems which are subject to pump failure (e.g., water, liquid feed, or rundown from or
 to offsites), normally require a pressure transient analysis. Such systems shall be designed for
 expected dynamic impact forces if the analysis determines that the peak pressure varies significantly
 from operating conditions. Short term overpressure allowance within ASME Piping Code rules should
 be utilized in preference to pressure relief protection if approved by the Owner.

 2.3.15 Runaway Reactions

 If runaway chemical reaction is determined to be a credible overpressure scenario and the process causing
 the reaction is not a BR-developed process, the Owner or process developer is responsible for providing
 the required heat release curves and/or flow rates and conditions to be used for relief device sizing. BR is
 not responsible for overpressure consequences that occur because of any inaccuracies in the information
 provided.

information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 8 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  2.4 Equipment Protection

  2.4.1 Heat Exchangers

  a. BR standard shall be to provide overpressure protection for tube failure in shell and tube exchangers if
 the low pressure side MAWP, including upstream and downstream systems, can be exceeded. Unlike
 API STD 521, ASME Code does not permit pressure accumulation above the MAWP for exchanger
 internal failure. Tube failure can vary from a pinhole leak to a complete tube diameter failure and is
 subject to the following considerations:

 1. Pressure relief protection shall be specified if the flow through a complete tube failure cannot be
 absorbed by the low pressure system without causing overpressure. A complete tube diameter
 failure is an unlikely event and shall not be considered simultaneous with a blocked in condition.

 2. If applicable, pressure relief shall be specified and located to adequately provide protection from
 maximum dynamic pressures caused by high pressure vapor or flashing liquid flowing through a
 complete tube rupture to a much lower pressure liquid full system. Complete and sudden tube
 rupture transient effects shall not be considered if the differential between the high pressure side
 and low pressure side operating pressure is less than 6895 kPa (1000 psi).

 3. A small tube leak (i.e., undetected during operation) shall be considered simultaneous with a
 blocked in condition if the exchanger can be blocked in during operation (e.g., caused by an
 inadvertent closure of one block valve or a control valve). Therefore, pressure relief shall be
 provided for a leaking tube or tubesheet if a blocked in condition can cause overpressure.
 However, if the exchanger need only be blocked in for maintenance, overpressure protection shall
 not be provided for a small tube leak because administrative procedures are expected to avoid the
 overpressure condition.

  b. Double pipe exchangers are considered to be piping components if they are designed using schedule
 pipe or equivalent heavy gauge tubes for the inner conduit. As such, they are not likely to rupture and
 shall not be protected from an internal failure.

  c. Exchangers shall be protected if one stream can be blocked in during operation (e.g., caused by an
 inadvertent closure of one block valve or a control valve), and the vapor pressure at maximum ambient
 design temperature exceeds the design pressure.

  d. Exchangers shall be protected if the cold side can be blocked in during operation (e.g., caused by an
 inadvertent closure of one block valve or a control valve), and the cold side liquid vapor pressure at the
 inlet temperature of the hot side exceeds the cold side design pressure (See Paragraph 2.3.13:
 Hydraulic Expansion).

  e. Overpressure due to an open external fire shall not be considered a viable contingency for spared
 exchangers that are drained and vented when not in service.

  2.4.2 Air Coolers

  During emergencies, reduced duty of air cooled heat exchangers when sizing pressure relief systems could
  be caused by:

  a. 100% failure of fans
  b. Louvers closed because of process control
  c. See Paragraph 2.3.5 on external fire and Paragraph 2.3.2 on power failure for other considerations

 265

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 9 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  2.4.3 Vessels

  Vessels shall be protected against all causes of overpressure as outlined in the preceding sections.
  However, protection shall not be provided for a vacuum condition caused by draining water after testing or
  condensing steam after steam out. The Owner s administrative procedures will provide adequate protection.

  2.4.4 Multiple Equipment

  Parallel equipment or inter connected equipment may be protected by a common relieving device provided
  that they are connected by a line of sufficient size with no intervening isolation valves and the set pressure is
  such that no equipment can be overpressured or that the relieving device prevents the pressure source from
  overpressuring the equipment (e.g., a pressure relief device on a steam header that is set to protect all of the
  downstream exchangers in that steam service from a blocked outlet case).

  2.4.5 Furnace Coils

  Fired coils in process service shall not be provided with overpressure protection, except when mandatory
  under the applicable code. Overpressure and safety protection for superheaters and boiler feedwater
  preheat coils shall be considered in accordance with the applicable code.

  2.4.6 Steam Turbines

  The need for overpressure protection shall be based on the pressure rating of the turbine casing and the
  closing of the outlet block valve. If required, the pressure relief valve shall be located in the exhaust piping
  upstream of the block valve and shall be sized to relieve the actual steam flow rate through the turbine at the
  allowable outlet overpressure. Sentinel valves are warning devices only and should not be used for
  overpressure protection.

  2.4.7 Reciprocating and Positive Displacement Pumps

  These pumps shall be protected with a pressure relieving device. Dynamic fluid pulsations shall be taken
  into account when determining a suitable margin between the set pressure and the overall peak to peak fluid
  pressure pulsations.

  2.4.8 Centrifugal Compressor Circuits

  a. If possible, the compressor casing design pressure for each stage should be higher than the maximum
 pressure, which can be developed by that compressor stage during any upset or normal operating
 condition. Examples of upset conditions are inadvertent closing of valves, loss of cooling, or
 unscheduled compressor trips. The maximum discharge pressure of each stage is the maximum
 possible suction pressure of that stage during any upset or normal operating condition plus the largest
 pressure differential of that stage coincident with that condition. The largest pressure differential occurs
 with simultaneous conditions of maximum continuous rated speed, maximum molecular weight, and
 minimum suction temperature at the incipient surge point of the compressor stage (surge differential
 head).

  b. The maximum operating pressure is compared with the maximum settling out pressure, which occurs
 during a compressor trip or shutdown. Increase of settling out pressure caused by warm up to the
 maximum design ambient temperature shall be considered for colder than ambient systems, such as
 refrigeration systems. The discharge piping for each stage shall be designed for the maximum potential
 discharge pressure for that stage or the compressor casing design pressure for that stage, whichever is
 less.

 266

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 10 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 c. As a minimum, the compressor stage shall be designed for the initial settling out pressure or the normal
 operating pressure plus a suitable margin, whichever is larger. If it is not practical for the compressor
 stage design pressure to be higher than the maximum pressure, which can be developed by the
 compressor, overpressure protection shall be provided by a pressure relieving system.

 d. To estimate the maximum discharge pressure and/or relieving load the characteristic curve of the
 proposed machine or a similar one should be used. This data shall be confirmed on receipt of Supplier
 data.

 3. PRESSURE RELIEF DEVICES

 3.1 General

 a. This section summarizes the design approach to the sizing and selection of pressure relief devices to
 protect equipment against overpressure from operating and fire contingencies.

 b. All pressure relief devices shall be stamped with the ASME Code Symbol for Section I or for Section VIII
 applications as required.

 c. All pressure relief valves shall be bench tested by the Owner, or by a representative designated by the
 Owner, to verify the set pressure prior to final installation, except those requiring in situ testing for ASME
 Section I applications.

 d. In specifying and purchasing pressure relief valves, BR and API standards shall be used as
 appropriate (e.g., BR Standard I47-1E, and API 526 and API 527).

 3.2 Sizing

 The various sizing methods for pressure relief valves used in liquid, two-phase, steam, gas, or vapor service
 shall meet the requirements of the codes and practices as listed in Article 1.2 (e.g., API 520 Part I and
 API 2000).

 3.3 Selection

    3.3.1 General

 a. For reasons of standardization, pressure relief valves shall be selected according to the standard size
 range provided in API 526, Flanged Steel Safety Relief Valves , not exceeding 8T10 size.

 b. Valve material shall be suitable for the inlet and outlet temperatures that result from the extremes of
 operating and emergency conditions. These include the effect of cooling as a result of reducing
 pressure through the valve during relief, but do not include the effect of chilling as a result of valve
 leakage. A fire condition resulting in a temperature above equipment design conditions is excluded from
 consideration for material selection.

 c. Typically, inlet pressure loss shall be limited to less than 3% of the valve set pressure (gage) per
 API 520 Part II requirements. If suitable for the application, a remote sensing pilot type valve can be
 specified for systems with excessive inlet pressure loss.

 d. Unless determined to be larger by calculation, the standard size to be used for a thermal pressure relief
 valve is a 3/4 inch x 1 inch threaded valve with a minimum orifice area of 0.71 cm2 (0.11 in2).

 CONFIDENTIALITY NOTICE: This document contains information which is proprietary to BR, Inc. and/or its affiliates ( BR ). This
information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 11 OF 19
  Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 e. A pressure relief valve provided with an upstream rupture (burst) disk shall have, as a minimum, a
 pressure gauge and a free vent to safe location between the two devices to prevent pressure build up
 and to detect premature disk failure or leakage. A local or control room alarm on high pressure ( 10%
 of set pressure) should be considered.

 3.3.2 ASME Section I Applications Steam Services

 a. All safety valves shall be of the direct spring loaded pop action type.

 b. A weld end inlet connection shall be provided and the valve shall be mounted directly to the vapor space
 of a steam boiler drum.

 c. The spring shall be fully exposed outside of the valve casing.

 d. Multiple valves shall be mounted on individual inlet connections. The smaller of two valves of different
 sizes shall have a relieving capacity not less than 50% of the larger valve. Set pressures shall be
 staggered.

 e. The valves shall discharge the required flow rate at no more than 6% above the maximum allowable
 working pressure.

 f. All safety valves shall have a plain lifting lever by which the valve disk may be positively lifted from its
 seat to ensure the valve is free to operate. Test gags shall be provided on all steam drum and
 superheater safety valves.

 3.3.3 ASME Section VIII Applications

 a. Conventional or Balanced Type

 1. Generally all valves shall be direct spring loaded, pop action type for steam, vapor, and gas
 services.

 2. Normally, for conventional valves, built up backpressure is limited to 10% of the set pressure.

 3. Valves in steam and air service shall have a plain lifting lever and may have an exposed spring
 (open bonnet). Valves in hot water ( 60 C (140 F) and above) service shall have a packed lifting
 lever and a closed bonnet.

 4. The blowdown shall be set and sealed between 5% to a maximum 7% of the set pressure or 3 psi,
 whichever is greater.

 5. The difference between the set pressure and the maximum expected operating pressure should be
 greater than the blowdown, e.g., typically 10% or 69 kPa (10 psi) whichever is greater. This
 requirement provides adequate operating margin and assurance that the valve is capable of
 reclosing.

 6. The main reasons for balanced valves are to:

 i. reduce the size of the discharge system (relief discharge manifold and header) by allowing a
 higher back pressure.

 ii. prevent the corrosion of the valve internals.

 CONFIDENTIALITY NOTICE: This document contains information which is proprietary to BR, Inc. and/or its affiliates ( BR ). This
information is to be held in confidence. No disclosure or other use of this document, except as necessary for operation of the Plant within

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 12 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 7. Balanced bellows or balanced piston valves should not be used in services where there is potential
 for bellows damage or failure. Examples are coking, fouling, waxy, polymerizing, or vibrating
 service, or freezing of the fluid on the bellows.

  b. Pilot Operated Type

 1. The use of pilot operated valves should be selective and is to be based on the operating
 characteristics. If selected, these valves shall normally be specified to have no-flow pop action
 type pilots, adjustable blowdown, a backflow preventer, and elastomer seat and seals. Integral
 pressure pick-up points shall normally be specified unless remote sensing is required because of
 excessive inlet pressure loss.

 2. Typically, these valves shall only be used in clean vapor service, where plugging and freezing of the
 pilot line is not anticipated. It is recommended that these valves should not be used in crude oil
 separator systems where they may be exposed to wax or solid contaminants. Their use is
 recommended for any of the following conditions:

 Built up backpressure exceeds that allowed by other valve types.

 The margin between the set pressure and the maximum operating pressure is less than that
 recommended by ASME Section VIII, Appendix M, which requires reduced blowdown (repeatable
 as low as 3% for most pilots).

 Reduced simmer and fluid loss at operating pressures close to the set pressure are desirable.

 Low set pressures are required (offsite storage tanks).

  3.3.4 Rupture Disks/ Buckling Pins

  a. Generally, rupture disks shall not be installed in the pressure relief valve inlet or discharge piping.

  b. Installation of rupture disks should be considered for slurry, corrosive and erosive services, or to
 minimize leakage of valuable, hazardous, or toxic fluids. The use of a pressure relief valve combined
 with an inlet rupture disk shall be evaluated on a case by case basis if corrosive fluids dictate the valve
 metallurgy.

  c. Generally, buckling pins shall only be considered on a case by case basis and shall not be specified
 without approval of the Owner.

  4. INSTALLATIONS

  4.1 General

  Pressure relief valves should be located such that the fluid temperature at the valve during normal plant
  operation is near ambient conditions when the valve is inactive to minimize thermal cycling. If ambient
  conditions affect performance, the valves shall be suitably (e.g., heat tracing might be provided to prevent
  liquid solidification and subsequent valve blockage in a freezing climate). Inlet line installation and
  insulation details should be reviewed by Process Engineering when piping is finalized.

 269

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 13 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  4.2 Inlet/Discharge Piping

  4.2.1 For ASME Section I applications, code requirements shall be followed. For ASME Section VIII
  applications, basic design features shall include:

  a. Inlet piping tie in should be located in the piping system as near as practical to the equipment protected.
 Equivalent length of the inlet piping should be kept to a minimum in order to avoid increasing the line
 size due to inlet pressure loss limitation.

  b. The nominal flow area through all pipe and fittings between a pressure vessel and its pressure relief
 valve shall be equal to or greater than the valve inlet nominal flow area. For compressible flow from a
 non-modulating type relief valve, the pressure loss of the inlet piping shall be based on the pressure
 relief valve rated capacity.

  c. When two or more pressure relief valves (spares not included) are placed on one connection, the cross
 sectional nominal area of this connection shall be at least equal to the combined nominal inlet areas
 of the valves, and the normal pressure loss requirement shall apply for the combined rated flow of the
 valves.

  d. Piping swages at the inlet and discharge valve connections should be fitting to fitting.

  e. Inlet and discharge piping shall be self draining from each valve.

  f. Discharge piping to the atmosphere shall have a 6 mm (0.25 in) minimum diameter weep hole at the low
 point. The weep hole shall be located such that any leaking fluid does not impinge on equipment or
 personnel.

  g. Inlet and discharge piping shall be insulated for heat traced systems. Only personnel protection
 insulation or guards shall be provided on hot service inlet leads 66 C (150 F). External heating or
 purging of the valve body shall be provided if determined that the discharged fluid may solidify.

  h. Calculations for sizing the inlet and discharge leads shall be checked and updated as required to reflect
 the approved piping arrangements and to ensure a safe system design.

  4.3 Multiple Valves (ASME Section VIII Applications)

  4.3.1 Multiple valves which protect equipment requiring more than one valve for the same overpressure
  contingency should generally have staggered set pressures. For these applications, the maximum allowable
  pressure accumulation at the protected equipment shall be 16% of the MAWP (maximum allowable working
  pressure) for non-fire cases and 21% of the MAWP for fire cases. If practical, inlet leads from the piping
  branch connection should be routed separately to each valve to mitigate excessive turbulence.

  4.3.2 Attention shall be given to relief situations which result in a wide range of relieving flow rates for
  different viable upset conditions. In this case, consideration shall be given to the selection of two valves a
  smaller pressure relief valve set at the equipment design pressure to handle the upset conditions resulting in
  lower relieving rates, and a larger valve set at 105% of equipment design pressure for the largest relieving
  case. This arrangement minimizes the problems resulting from valve chattering.

  4.4 Isolation Valves

  4.4.1 Inlet or outlet block valves are not permitted for ASME Section I applications and are not
  recommended for ASME Section VIII applications.

 270

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 14 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  4.4.2 Should the Owner provide a written request for the installation of block valves for ASME VIII
  applications, the following applies:

  a. Inlet block valves shall have a port cross sectional nominal area equal to or greater than the cross
 sectional nominal area of the inlet of the pressure relief valve and shall be locked open (LO) for all
 applications.

  b. Outlet block valves shall be locked open (LO) for all applications and shall have, as a minimum, a port
 cross sectional area that cannot reduce the relieving capacity below that required or otherwise adversely
 affect the proper operation of the pressure relieving device.

  c. A valved vent connection is required between the pressure relief valve and the inlet and outlet block
 valves.

  d. A pressure indicator shall be provided that can be monitored for operation of the bypass valve when the
 pressure relief valve is out of service.

  e. Pressure relief valves which discharge via individual outlets to atmosphere shall have no outlet block
 valves.

  f. The Owner assumes responsibility for the administrative control of these valves in strict accordance with
 ASME Section VIII, Appendix M.

  4.5 Sparing Philosophy

  4.5.1 Providing installed spare pressure relief valves is not permitted for ASME Section I applications and is
  not recommended for ASME Section VIII applications. It is assumed that routine or scheduled valve
  maintenance is done when the equipment is taken out of service.

  4.5.2 Should the Owner provide a written request for installed spare pressure relief valves for ASME Section
  VIII applications, a mechanical linkage or key interlock/valving system is recommended such that the
  installed spare provides full overpressure protection before the operating valve can be removed from service
  without relying on the operator to follow administrative procedures.

  4.5.3 Should the Owner provide a written request for installed spare pressure relief valves with non
  interlocked isolation valves for ASME Section VIII applications, the requirements for isolation valves stated in
  Article 4.4 of this document apply except that only the operating pressure relief valves isolation valves shall
  be locked open. It is recommended that the installed spare PRV isolation inlet valve be locked closed and its
  isolation outlet valve be locked open.

  5. DISPOSAL SYSTEMS

  5.1 General

  5.1.1 This section outlines the general design approach and methods for the safe disposal of vapors, gases,
  and liquids to prevent hazardous conditions.

  5.1.2 The system design approach includes provision for the disposal of warm or cold fluids to separate
  warm or cold closed collection systems discharging to a flare and the venting of gases directly to the
  atmosphere, depending on the fluid properties, molecular weight, and temperature without compromising
  safety to equipment or personnel.

 271

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 15 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  5.1.3 All direct atmospheric releases shall be made within the limitations of environmental regulations. The
  safe disposal approach includes an analysis for thermal radiation levels from flares and atmospheric vents
  which can ignite. This analysis determines the location and elevation at the point of discharge to ensure that
  the allowable levels as specified are not exceeded. Noise levels from operational vent systems shall be
  analyzed to ensure compliance with the allowable levels.

  5.1.4 The following guidelines shall be considered in the design of closed disposal systems:

  a. The sub headers shall be free draining toward the main headers.

  b. All relief headers shall be free draining toward their respective knock out receivers.

  c. Locating a pressure relief device below a header in closed systems must be avoided. Laterals from a
 device that absolutely must be located below the header shall rise continuously to the top of the header
 entry point and means shall be provided to prevent condensate accumulation in the valve discharge.

  d. Discharge piping from operational vents shall be insulated for a certain length where prevention of
 external ice formation is desirable in a specified plot area for personnel protection. Similarly, discharge
 piping from operational vents in hot service shall be insulated or shielded for personnel protection.

  5.1.5 Nitrogen or natural gas from a reliable source shall be continuously introduced as a purge into the
  vapor disposal systems. Purge gas connections shall be provided in every disposal system at the header
  extremities depending on final plant layout. A minimum velocity shall be maintained at the flare tip to
  minimize ambient air ingress. This flow velocity shall be verified based on the selected proprietary flare tip or
  molecular seal design. A separate seal drum or an automatically controlled emergency purge gas source
  may be required to avoid vacuum and subsequent air ingress into the flare header caused by hot relief
  vapors condensing.

  5.1.6 The hydraulic profile of the closed disposal systems shall be verified and calculations updated as
  required to reflect the approved piping arrangements, blowdown receiver drums, and flare installation. Also,
  the system shall be designed according to criteria for avoiding fatigue failure from acoustically and flow
  induced vibration.

  5.2 Flare Header Sizing

  5.2.1 General

  Typically, all fluids discharged to the closed disposal system from pressure relief devices or vent valves
  within the process and utility areas are routed via one header to the main flare header located outside unit
  battery limits. Separate vapor collection systems are provided for warm, cold, high pressure, and low
  pressure releases as required. Sometimes a separate disposal or flare system is provided because of the
  specific fluid being discharged (e.g., acid gas is normally segregated from other systems). The following
  flare header sizing philosophy is considered BR standard practice:

  a. Individual relief valves and leads to unit header

 Individual relief valves shall be designed to relieve the required flow rate determined from the controlling
 cause of overpressure. For compressible flow from a non-modulating type relief valve, the outlet lead(s)
 from the valve(s) to the sub-header or main header is sized based on the rated capacity of the device
 which is consistent with the value used to size the inlet lead. No allowance is taken for any mitigating
 action from installed trip systems.

 272

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 16 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  b. Individual unit headers

 1. Each individual unit flare header shall be designed to handle the worst single relief rate or the
 largest combined rate from the relief valves within that unit as a result of any common event.

 2. Partial credit may be taken for the operation of dedicated shutdown/trip devices that are actuated as
 a result of the event being considered. Because of the number of safety shutdown/trip systems that
 may be part of the unit instrumentation system, there is always the possibility that one or more of
 these systems may not work when required. For determining flare loading, a dedicated
 shutdown/trip device is defined as a single valve and transmission source that are both dedicated to
 shutdown and operate separately from the basic process control of the unit. The transmission
 source is preferred to be two out of three voting to minimize spurious trips.

 3. In establishing the unit flare loading, credit may be taken for all of the shutdown/trip systems
 excluding that one system or device that causes the highest loading if it fails to operate, providing
 that this single case is a large proportion of the total load. For example, a separate trip to close
 could be provided on each of the reboiler heating medium sources for a group of fractionation
 columns. If more than one trip device is provided for the same reboiler heating medium source only
 one is assumed to fail to operate.

 4. The single failure case is considered to be the trip responsible for the largest relieving flow.
 Therefore, the individual column with the largest overhead vapor flow of all of the columns will
 normally be included in the controlling cooling water or power failure case ( loss of reflux ).

 5. The signal source activating the trips may originate from pressure, temperature, power loss, cooling
 water flow, or any combination of these. If the common event involves many individual systems in
 which the failure of each one would result in only a small percent increase of the total load, more
 than a single system failure should be considered. BR Office of the Chief Engineer shall be
 consulted for clarification of the criteria.

  c. Main flare header

 1. For facilities consisting of a number of interrelated process units, the main relief header shall be
 sized for rates that are the sum of the worst case loads from each of the individual units for the
 common event(s) being considered, or the worst case sum of related events that are not necessarily
 common to all units. Credit for the operation of the shutdown/trip systems for each unit shall be as
 defined above when determining each unit s worst case loading.

 2. Where it can be demonstrated that a single unrelated event within any one unit does not affect the
 total facilities, appropriate consideration of this fact shall be given in determining the loads. In sizing
 the main flare header, it is not required to combine the total loadings resulting from all single
 unrelated events in each of the different units because the simultaneous occurrence of these
 unrelated events is not considered credible. BR Office of the Chief Engineer shall be consulted for
 clarification of the criteria.

  5.2.2 Dynamic Analysis

  Dynamic analysis of the events occurring during a plant wide failure scenario may indicate reduced overall
  loads based upon the sequence of the relief loadings considered. This type analysis is considered to be
  valid for a plant wide upset event providing it is reviewed and approved by BR Office of the Chief Engineer.

 273

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 17 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  5.3 Flare Systems

  5.3.1 General

  Flare systems shall be designed to meet the minimum requirements of API STD 521. The exit flow velocity
  for an emergency case may exceed the limits of this recommended practice as considered prudent to
  improve the performance of the overall flare system. This philosophy follows common recent industry
  practice.

  5.3.2 Warm Relief (Hydrocarbon Vapor) Collection

  a. The warm relief collection system terminates in a warm knock out receiver where disengaging liquid
 particles are separated and collected. Condensed liquid in the warm header system drains to and
 collects in the receiver. All vapors, gases, and vapor/liquid mixtures which contain components likely to
 condense, form hydrates, or freeze at 0 C (32 F) or above are routed to the warm header system. The
 pipe shall be free draining towards the receiver.

  b. The warm knock out receiver shall be sized based on the calculation method presented in API STD 521.

  5.3.3 Cold Relief Collection

  a. Similar to the warm system, the cold vapor collection system terminates in a cold knock out receiver.
 All vapors, gases, and vapor/liquid mixtures which expand isenthalpically through the relieving devices
 to a downstream temperature of less than 0 C (32 F) not containing any components likely to freeze,
 are routed to the cold header system. The cold vapor collection system shall be uninsulated. This is
 inclusive of the receiver, blowdown vaporizer and superheater.

  b. The cold knock out receiver shall be sized based on the calculation method presented in API STD 521.

  5.3.4 Liquid Disposal

  a. Liquid relief to the flare collection system should be avoided. If this cannot be prevented, careful
 consideration shall be given to potential problems associated with liquid disposal from pressure relief
 devices and liquid de inventorying into the flare systems. Appreciable quantities of liquid discharged to
 the flare during an emergency vapor release could cause an undesirable flow regime in the horizontal
 line and the entrance to the knock out receivers, raising the risk that the receivers may not drop out the
 entrained liquid droplets down to a size the flare can handle.

  b. Alternative installations which reduce this potential risk shall be considered in the design. These
 include:

 1. Discharging liquid streams via a closed system to a lower pressure system or vessel.

 2. Providing separate liquid collection systems to the respective knock out receivers.

 3. Providing disengaging drums and collecting liquids separated with overhead vapor or gas vented to
 the disposal systems.

 274

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 18 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

  5.4 Atmospheric Vents

  5.4.1 Steam, instrument/plant air and nitrogen vapors shall be discharged to the atmosphere through a tail
  pipe terminating at safe location. Refer to Article 4.2 for condensate or rain water removal (i.e., weep hole).

  5.4.2 Air or steam used during start up operations or for maintenance or for decoking and regeneration
  purposes shall be discharged to the atmosphere and not to the closed disposal system.

  5.4.3 In general, hydrocarbons or other non-hazardous flammable vapor mixtures shall be permitted to vent
  to the atmosphere at a safe location only if all of the following apply:

  a. The released gases are diluted to below the lower flammable limit before creating a hazard at grade or
 other plant operating level. API STD 521 states that such dilution occurs for any vapor which meets a
 minimum Reynolds number requirement at the exit point as defined by API STD 521. Discharge
 velocities greater than 30.5 m/s (100 ft/sec) are jet dominated and satisfy this API criterion. However,
 the lowest velocity release case must be known and checked against this criterion if it is to be applied.

  b. The released gases do not condense. API STD 521 states that vapor condensation in appreciable
 quantities depends on many factors. Because a specific criteria is not defined and because the factors
 involved are difficult to identify for all possible release cases. BR conservatively assumes that no
 condensation occurs only if the lowest defined atmospheric temperature (i.e., in the project design data)
 is above the dew point of the vapor released. Also, condensation resulting from auto-refrigeration
 effects must not occur.

  c. The gases are permitted to be released to atmosphere per environmental regulations. The duration,
 quantity, type (i.e., emergency), and location of the release may have to be analyzed before the exact
 environmental requirements to be applied are known.

  d. Ignition of the emission at the outlet point does not occur, or if it occurs, the heat radiation produced is
 within the allowable limits. According to BR standards, inert gas shall be provided as a means of
 purging the outlet piping and shall be controlled from grade. Special consideration should be given to
 releases containing hydrogen or other very volatile gases because they are susceptible to auto ignition
 or by static electricity.

  5.5 Thermal Radiation

  5.5.1 General

  a. The following information shall be the basis for selecting the allowable thermal radiation levels emitted
 from any flare plume and the duration of exposure to protect operating personnel under administrative
 control of the plant from the effects of radiation.

  b. The effects of solar radiation for any plant location shall be included in the assessment of acceptable
 time exposure. Solar radiation is typically in the range of 788 - 945 W/m (250 to 300 Btu/hr ft2).

  c. Refer to API STD 521 for full details. General guidelines are:

 1. 0 - 1577 W/m (0 - 500 Btu/hr ft2): A level generally taken to be acceptable for continuous exposure
 at any location.

 275

 PHILOSOPHY P50-1D-US-BO-M&U

DATE: 09 OCT 2019 ENGINEERING SERVICES BY KBR TECHNICAL SERVICES, INC. PAGE 19 OF 19
 Spec. Number Suffixes: US based on US Codes and Standards, BO applicable for onshore and offshore Projects, M&U units used:

 2. 1580 - 2524 W/m (501 to 800 Btu/hr ft2): An exposure level recommended for the public or
 personnel not under the administrative control of the plant outside the property perimeter. Warning
 signs shall be provided at public roads or perimeter access areas. This level also applies where
 operators would be required to perform duties without the benefit of protective clothing. The
 recommended exposure time is no more than 15 minutes.

 3. 2527 - 4732 W/m2 (801 to 1500 Btu/hr ft2): A level recommended in operating areas at grade or on
 elevated operating platforms where operators are under administrative control by the plant and are
 wearing appropriate protective clothing. Any ladders, platforms, or equipment in this area of
 radiation requiring an operator s presence should be oriented in such a manner that operators are
 shielded from the flame radiation. As an alternate, nearby shelters or radiation shields should
 provide cover for the operator.

 A level recommended at the edge of the nearest plant roadway to the flare, at the blowdown
 receivers, and at the flame front generators.

 The recommended exposure time is no more than 3 minutes. This limit provides personnel ample
 time to seek cover or leave the area.

 4. 4735 - 6309 W/m (1501 to 2000 Btu/hr ft2): A level recommended in areas requiring emergency
 actions only up to a maximum of 30 seconds for operators under administrative control of the plant
 and wearing appropriate protective clothing.

 5. Above 6309 W/m2 (Above 2000 Btu/hr ft2): Radiation levels above this heat intensity are not
 acceptable at any location to which operators need access. Injury to personnel can occur due to
 insufficient time to seek cover.

  d. Calculation method

 API STD 521 shall be used to determine the flame radiation levels at all points of interest, e.g., at grade
 and elevated platforms. Suppliers proprietary calculations based on the selected equipment shall be
 obtained for verification.

1. PURPOSE AND SCOPE

 The purpose of this document is to provide guidelines for specifying isolation devices to be
 provided in the facilities design to isolate equipment or sections of plant to permit a safe
 operation and working environment and provide safe access for maintenance or inspection
 to the process production and utility systems.
 It also serves as design guidelines to assist in the development of a consistent set of P&IDs,
 allowing segregation of process units and utility systems to avoid contamination and provide
 adequate isolation for hydraulic testing, pre-commissioning, commissioning, and operational
 requirements.
 This philosophy applies to process and utility piping, equipment, and units but specifically
 excludes isolation requirements for instrumentation and electrical components.

 281
 Page 4 of 23

                 KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                       Rev.: B

3. GUIDELINES

 METHODS OF ISOLATION
 Maintenance shall be performed when unit or a system is shutdown, isolated, and
 depressurized to atmosphere, drained and free from flammable and toxic gas. It is necessary
 that items of equipment or groups of equipment can be isolated in order to facilitate their
 shutdown for maintenance, inspection, tie-ins, and loss prevention. The requirement for
 isolation to enable maintenance or inspection is dependent on the operating philosophy,
 extent of shutdown, nature of the fluids, and operating pressure of the system. As the degree
 of risk increases, so does the required effectiveness and security of the isolation method. Note
 that high risk is not only associated with traditional hazards such as harmful fluids, high
 pressures, and high temperatures. There are also other risks, such as the likelihood of a
 production outage, which results in a quantifiable loss of revenue, a lower production figure,
 and damage to the environment.
 Isolation philosophy is based mainly on fluid characteristics, pressure class related to the
 maximum operating pressure, and equipment / piping work involved. Isolation methods are
 as listed below:
 • Positive isolation.
 • Double block and bleed.
 • Single block and bleed.
 • Single block valve.
 Above mentioned may be applied singularly or in combination.
 POSITIVE ISOLATION
 Positive isolation is a method of isolation that removes all practical risk of accidental
 reconnection to source of risk. These are used where no leakage can be tolerated for
 safety and/or contamination reasons. Positive isolation is a mandatory provision for
 the following instances:
 • Confined space entry.
 • Long duration isolation (greater than 1 week).
 • Process fluids above auto-ignition temperature.
 • Equipment mothballing.
 • Hot work.

 283
 Page 6 of 23

   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                        Rev.: B

  • Where risk assessment is determined is necessary.
Positive isolation would include:
  • Insertion of spectacle blind or spade.
  • Removal of pipe spool and installation of blind flanges.
Spectacle blinds, ring spacer and removable pipe spools to be used for positive
isolation are shown in Figure 3-1.

 FIGURE 3-1 POSITIVE ISOLATION FITTINGS

Spectacle blinds are preferred method of isolation in comparison with separate spades
and spacers as they provide immediate visual determination that positive isolation is
in place.
Spectacle blinds shall be designed such that the pressure rating is equal to the flange
rating of the system. It shall not be designed to meet the system process design
pressure.
Spectacle blind material shall be of same material group as the pipe flanges (ASME
B.16.5 & B16.47). All sealing surfaces of spectacle blinds shall be machined in
accordance with the same standard and surface used for the system pipe flanges.
Spades and spacers are the least preferred option when compared with spectacle
blinds because of the risk of miss-identification. Identification of spades and spacers is
possible by markings on the handle, which could vary between different
manufacturers. In order to minimize the risk of miss identification it is recommended
to have a double hole in the handle to represent a spacer; and a single hole to
represent a spade. Spades shall be supplied with a hook for ease of installation /
removal for 10” and above lines.

 284
 Page 7 of 23

   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                          Rev.: B

DOUBLE BLOCK AND BLEED (DBB)
In double block and bleed arrangement - isolation integrity is provided by the bleed
valve preventing a high differential pressure across the second isolation valve in case
the upstream valve leaks in tight closed position. The bleed valve shall be terminated
locally such that it can be monitored to confirm that the isolation valve is effective. The
distance between two block valves shall be the minimum practicable. Double block and
bleed arrangements can also be used for positive isolation when blinds are inserted
between the blocks and generally used at battery limits and for isolation. This
arrangement is shown in Figure 3-2. This application could be with one or two blinds
as the case warrants. Without the blind, this arrangement is used for general isolation
of start-up lines, to prevent contamination between streams, etc.

 FIGURE 3-2 DOUBLE BLOCK AND BLEED

Double block valves and double block bleeders shall be provided with spectacle blinds
or spade/spacers for systems of ANSI Class 600 rating and above. If process lines are
required to have double block valves (for example ANSI Class 900 and above), then the
drains and vents should also follow this requirement.
Double valves below 600# rating shall be considered for:
  • Toxic service.
  • Sour service.
  • Systems isolating high temperature fluids at or above auto ignition.
  • Systems which are regularly isolated for routine operations/maintenance.
  • Drains in lethal service.
  • Other hazardous services.
Lethal Fluid
  • Fluids with health risk in accordance with Category 4 of NFPA 704, whichever is more
 stringent.

 285
 Page 8 of 23

   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                        Rev.: B

Sour Fluid
  • Fluids with health risk in accordance with Category 3 of NFPA 704, whichever is more
 stringent.
SINGLE BLOCK AND BLEED (SBB)
The principle is similar to the double block and bleed arrangement but without the
second valve. The bleed valve is used to drain and depressurize the piping work
downstream of the block valve. A single block and bleed is adequate for enabling the
installation of a spectacle blind.
A spectacle blind is used to further isolate hydrocarbons or fluids from entering the
maintenance side of the valve.

 FIGURE 3-3 SINGLE BLOCK AND BLEED

SINGLE BLOCK VALVE
Single block valves provide the most common process isolation where the pressure
boundary downstream of the valve is secure.

 286
 Page 9 of 23

                  KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                        Rev.: B

4. ISOLATION PHILOSOPHY

 Maintenance or inspection work shall be implemented on process units or systems which are
 to be isolated and shutdown, depressurized to atmospheric pressure, drained, and purged
 out.
 It may be impractical or uneconomical to shutdown complete facilities or systems for
 maintenance or inspection. Equipment could remain connected to live facilities or systems
 during inspection or maintenance, with consequent potential for hazard to personnel and
 process unit. In these cases, the design of the facilities shall incorporate means of positive
 isolation of individual equipment undergoing inspection or maintenance from remaining live
 facilities, to protect personnel from hazards of flammable and / or toxic liquids and gases.
 The method of isolation is dependent on factors such as type of maintenance and inspection
 work involved, i.e. of a major, routine, or minor nature; the properties of the process fluid in
 operation; and the operating pressure / temperature. These factors determine whether
 isolation should consist of single or double block valves, together with bleeds and inserted
 spades or closed spectacle blinds.
 It is necessary to isolate the systems and equipment for maintenance to enable the activity
 in a safe manner. All factors affecting isolation shall be considered prior to determining the
 required type of isolation. The factors would include but are not limited to the following:
 • Equipment and pipe size.
 • Operating pressure.
 • Duration of maintenance.
 • Maintenance frequency.
 • Type of service that is to be isolated, e.g. fuel gas, crude oil, etc.
 ISOLATION FOR SHUTDOWN MAINTENANCE
 Shutdown means the Plant, Unit, or System has been isolated and that it is at
 atmospheric pressure, drained, purged, and free of hydrocarbons or chemicals. For
 complete unit shutdown, the feed, products, and utility lines shall be isolated at unit
 battery limit. Spectacle blinds or spade / spacers shall be fitted on the battery limit
 lines. During the swinging of the blind, both the block valves will be closed, and bleed
 valve will be left open. It is important that the isolation is positive and does not rely on
 valves alone, which may leak.

 287
                                                                                          Page 10 of 23

     KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                       Rev.: B

 ISOLATION AT UNIT BATTERY LIMIT (B/L)
 The battery limit arrangement is shown below for hydrocarbons and utility. It
 includes block valve, spectacle blind, and vent and / or drain. The requirement for
 a check valve is to be decided on a case-by-case basis and is not required in majority
 of cases.
 Check valve in battery limit is required for:
 • Nitrogen service connection.
 • Incoming line is connected to a common header inside battery limit without any
 check valve.
 • Outgoing line is connected to a common header outside battery limit without any
 other check valve and there is possibility of back flow.

FIGURE 4-1 PROCESS BATTERY LIMITS – GENERAL LOW-PRESSURE HYDROCARBON

ISOLATION OF PERSONNEL PROTECTION
 Entry by personnel to enclosed spaces shall be permitted only after positive
 isolation (or deemed necessary by risk assessment) by installing blinds, blind
 flanges, or other means of positive isolation at all connections, except those used
 for atmospheric ventilation.
For entry into single items of equipment it should be noted with respect to pipe
specifications that:
 • All vessels, columns, reactors, drums and confined spaces which have man-ways
 and hand-holes shall be designed for positive isolation by blinding or spool
 removal, etc.
 • Provision for isolating all inlet and outlet lines to and from the item should be
 made.
 • Vents should be available on the equipment side of the isolation (e.g. on a vessel).
 • In the case of a pumped liquid outlet from a vessel, isolation may be achieved at
 the pump suction to isolate pump only. Ensure the pump suction line can be
 drained.
 • Permanent spectacle blinds shall be included in the design.
 • Spades shall be of standard thickness for the line piping class.
 • Permanent spectacle blinds are installed where piping is too stiff to “spring”. Ring
 spacers will be specified for large pipe sizes where spectacle blinds would not be
 handled easily. The basis shown in Table 4-2 below may be used as a guide.
 • Relief valve nozzles whether on the vessel or the piping shall not have spectacle
 blinds or blind spacers.
 • Block valves shall not be provided on vessel overhead vapor lines, transfer lines,
 reboiler lines, or side stream vapor return lines as means of isolation unless the
 line can be isolated with the vessel in operation (e.g. spare reboiler). When the
 isolation valves are not required, removable spool pieces or ring spacers shall be
 provided for positive isolation.

 292
                                                                           Page 15 of 23

   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                        Rev.: B

 • Pumps with a spare.
 • Compressor with a spare.
 • Parallel exchangers.
 • Control valves.
 • Filters.
Special equipment (e.g. recycle compressors, standby steam/fuel gas turbine drivers
etc.), which may be repaired with plant under pressure.
For control valves and smaller pumps only isolation valves are normally provided.
Requirements for major pumps and special equipment shall be considered on an
individual basis.
As a minimum, pumps in hot hydrocarbon service (operating temperature 150°C and
above) shall be provided with spectacle blinds / ring spacers at suction and discharge
valves in accordance with the size / rating criteria given in Table 4-2 Application of Type
of Blinds. Figure 4-7 shows typical sketches for pumps.

ISOLATION FOR CONTROL VALVE
Double block and bleed valves required for 600# and above or in toxic service, see
section 3.1.2.
Control valve bypass is not provided in the following instances.
 • If the control valve can be taken out of service without reducing safety, reliability, or
 capacity, then no bypass or handwheel is required. Control valve bypasses shall not be
 installed if manual control of the bypass is difficult or not desirable, e.g. anti-surge
 control or fuel control to boilers that may result in a hazardous situation.
 • If the control valve is part of non-critical or intermittently operated equipment, which
 can be isolated by other means without affecting capacity.
 • A bypass is not required if it would adversely affect the process because of pressure
 drop, residence time, dead legs, etc.
 • Control valves which are part of a trip system shall not be provided with a bypass.
 Control valves used for safety instrumented systems (SIS) and credit is taken for the
 control valve in the SIL verification calculations. The control valve will be closed or
 opened from the SIS to the DCS then to the solenoid valves on the air supply. SIL
 verification calculations shall determine if the single control valve is adequate or
 additional ESD valve(s) are required to meet the SIL classification.
 • Emergency shutdown system valves shall not be provided with bypass.
 • Bypasses are not required around pressure let down valves for continuous operation
 purposes. For utility services, on sizes smaller than 6-inch by-pass with restriction
 orifice may be installed. This criterion does not exclude installation of pressure
 equalization bypasses when the conditions require.
If a bypass is provided follow the details below.

 296
                                                                               Page 19 of 23

   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                           Rev.: B

  Relief valves fitted to steam turbine low pressure exhausts may be equipped with a
  full flow by-pass valve for temporary operation, exhausting to atmosphere.
  These services requiring relief valve bypass lines are not applicable to this project
  so relief valve bypass lines are not required.
  BONNET VENTS
  Balanced bellows valves have a vent connection from the bonnet. These should be
  piped to a safe location and should not be provided with isolation valves.
ISOLATION OF START-UP BYPASSES DURING NORMAL OPERATIONS
 Isolation philosophy for start-up bypass lines during normal operations is as follows:
 • Isolation is normally by valve closure for any lines to be used during normal operation,
 or in reaction to emergency conditions. The fluid remaining in the piping shall be left
 in-situ.

 • If the fluid has a high pour point, the line should be either heat traced or provision
 made for the line to be flushed and drained down.

 • Thermal relieving facility may need to be provided for a liquid, if the line can be
 isolated and subjected to heat input from any source.

 • Where the line is used only for pre-start-up circulation and flushing, blind isolation
 should be provided for closure in normal operation.

 • A blind shall be installed where leakage through any start-up or bypass line can cause
 product contamination. Typically, a blind shall be provided inside a double block and
 bleed arrangement.

 • Temporary piping shall be removed.

 • Swing elbows / removable spools may be employed where practical.

ISOLATION OF A SINGLE TRAIN OF A MULTI-TRAIN UNIT
In units which have two or more trains, there is a possibility that one train is shut down
while the other is in operation. Accordingly, isolation philosophy for one of the two or
more trains shall be as follows:
 • Spectacle blinds, spades, or removable spools, with suitable valving shall be
 specified where isolation is required when one train is shutdown.
 • Adequate vents and bleeds should be provided for safe installation and removal
 of blinds.

 299
                                                                              Page 22 of 23

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-001                             Rev.: B

 TYPICAL ISOLATION OF UTILITIES FROM PROCESS SYSTEMS

 FIGURE 4-11 TYPICAL ISOLATION OF UTILITIES
Notes:
 1. The valve is to be included in the higher specification.
 2. A removable spool or swing elbow may be provided for maintenance isolation.
 3. Use of temporary piping / hoses for steam out and purging is to be considered during
 detailed engineering.
 4. When nitrogen may be frequently used during normal operation use this detail. When
 used just as an alternate to steam out use same detail as for steam out.
 5. Header valve. This may be used for a group of users. Shut during normal operation.
 6. Steam pipes connected to process pipes shall meet the following:
 • If the pressure of the process system can be higher than that of the steam system,
 provide the steam line with two check valves upstream of the block valve(s) with
 a bleed valve between the downstream check valve and the (last) block valve.
 • Position the block valve and check valve close together and close to the process
 pipe.
 EMERGENCY ISOLATION
 There are no systems above auto-ignition temperature; therefore, API 553 does not
 apply.
