1. PURPOSE AND SCOPE

 This document covers the minimum requirements for protection of process and utilities and all
 associated equipment, flow lines, and instruments against the temperature which would cause
 congealing or freezing of contents, interfere with operation or cause damage to equipment or
 pipelines and for heat conservation requirement as would be determined by process conditions.
 The heat conservation system shall be designed for continuous operations while winterizing shall
 be for seasonal operation.

2. ABBREVIATIONS

 DMinDBT – Design Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature
 ExMinDBT – Extreme Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature

3. DEFINITIONS

 TERMS
 Freeze Protection - Heat tracing used to prevent liquid contents from freezing.
 Process Temperature Maintenance - Heat tracing used to prevent an undesirable property
 change to a process fluid due to ambient temperature.
 Heat Tracing - Any combination of steam, electric, or other medium traced on piping or
 equipment for the purpose of maintaining a temperature for freeze protection or process
 considerations.
 Design Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature - The ambient temperature at a particular site used
 to determine the need for protection from cold weather for each fluid service.
 Extreme Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature - The coldest recorded ambient temperature at a
 particular site.
 Winterization - A generic term referring to a number of techniques, including, but not
 limited to heat tracing that can be used to prevent undesirable occurrences in fluid contents
 due to cold ambient temperature.

 305
 Page 4 of 10

                 KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                           Rev.: A

4. WINTERIZATION AND FREEZE PROTECTION

 GENERAL REQUIREMENTS
 The need for winterization will be identified during process design and when applicable
 indicated on the P&ID.
 FLUIDS REQUIRING WINTERIZATION
 Typical fluids that require freeze protection:
 • Liquids that freeze above the Design Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature or the Extreme
 Minimum Dry Bulb Temperature.
 • Liquids that change important process properties (such as high viscosity or precipitation
 of dissolved chemicals) above the DMinDBT.
 • Vapors that condense above the DMinDBT or ExMinDBT if the resulting liquid interferes
 with proper operation (such as leads to sampling systems or leads to differential pressure
 instruments).
 • Vapors that can cause corrosion if condensation occurs above the DMinDBT.
 WINTERIZATION TECHNIQUES
 Winterization can be provided in several different ways. Generally, the least
 expensive method that is practical for all plant operating modes should be chosen.
 Heat tracing or insulation shall be used only if other freeze protection techniques are
 not applicable.
 Freeze protection equipment (tracing, insulation, etc.) should be eliminated when
 operating procedures are sufficient to provide freeze protection.
 The following list of freeze protection methods is given in order of increasing cost.
 • Vary liquid composition to avoid freezing.
 • Provide for draining of pipe and equipment when not in service. Draining includes
 opening a low point valve or designing the line to be free draining. Draining must be
 consistent with plant operating procedures and must be reasonable to accomplish in
 case of an unplanned shutdown.
 • Blow liquid out of the line by gas pressure. Blowing must be consistent with plant
 operating procedures and must be reasonable to accomplish in case of an unplanned
 shutdown.
 • Install bypass lines around equipment that may be isolated to maintain circulating flow in
 the pipe (such as water lines around exchangers).

 306
 Page 5 of 10

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                            Rev.: A

 • Continuous circulation of fluid during temporary interruptions of the process. Continuous
 circulation must be consistent with plant operating procedures.
 • Insulate lines to preserve the available heat. Many lines that have available heat are
 already insulated for heat conservation. The heat conservation insulation may be
 sufficient for freeze protection.
 • Provide heat tracing with the appropriate insulation.
 • Provide heat jacketing.
 • Bury lines underground below the freeze line. This option is rarely used except for severe
 climates. Portions of cooling water and firewater lines are sometimes buried for other
 reasons and consequently are also protected from freezing.
 • Different portions of the same line or lines in the same service do not always need the
 same winterization technique. Above ground portions of mostly buried systems may
 require draining or heat tracing. A service constantly operating at elevated temperature
 may be in jeopardy during a planned or unplanned shutdown.
FREEZE PROTECTION DESIGN REQUIREMENTS
Service fluids, such as cooling water, that are maintained at a temperature above the
DMinDBT while flowing, may use freeze protection techniques of insulating, draining,
blowing clear, or bypassing. However, lines in these services may freeze, where or when the
fluid becomes stagnant and cools. The locations where or the occasions when these
conditions occur need additional provision for freeze protection.
To avoid freezing in water lines, electrical tracing will be added on water lines with
diameters 4" or below. Electrical tracing is not required on water lines with diameter 6” or
greater.
The following is a list of some special situations that may also require freeze protection.
• Dead legs in lines and lines used for draining upstream of the drain valve. However, standard
 low point drains are not considered dead legs (i.e., minimal amount of piping) and are
 winterized the same as the main line.
• Lines that have stagnant fluid during a planned/unplanned shutdown or maintenance.
• Inactive condensate lines and non-self-draining steam traps (such as bucket type).
• Liquid lines from liquid knockout drums or water draw off lines from vessels or tanks.
• Flush and purge lines or other lines that are typically in intermittent use.
• Relief valve inlet lines.
• Safety showers, underground valves, and hydrants are to be self-draining.

 307
 Page 6 of 10

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                              Rev.: A

• Pump minimum flow bypasses. Even if the bypass flow is normally continuous (i.e., bypass has a
 restriction orifice), heat tracing may be required if the bypass is not flowing and cannot be
 completely drained during pump maintenance.
• Pipe leads upstream of inlet valves and downstream of outlet valves for spared equipment
 (including pumps). When the equipment block valves are closed for maintenance, these lines
 become stagnant and cannot be drained, assuming the other equipment item is operating. The
 same applies for equipment items which can be bypassed during plant operation.
• Liquid outlet lines from separation vessels which normally contain minimal amounts of liquid
 such that the outlet valve, even if a level control valve, can remain closed for indefinite periods
 of time (e.g., liquid outlet lines from fuel gas knockout drums or compressor suction drums).
• Non-operating vapor lines in which condensation can occur and collect (e.g., normally closed,
 pressure-controlled vent systems).
• Potable water systems (i.e., supply to safety shower and eyewash stations use some type of
 protection that prevents overheating and provides a temperate flow of water to personnel).
• Intermittent blow-off lines upstream of the blow-off valve and, unless the line is self-draining,
 also downstream of the blow-off valve.
• Instrument bridles.
• Control valve bypasses and portions of control valve stations that cannot be drained during
 maintenance.
• A water/hydrocarbon solution not normally subject to freezing that produces a water portion
 subject to freezing if allowed to separate. For example, a vessel containing a hydrocarbon/water
 interface has associated piping, such as the level bridle and drain attached to the water boot or
 vessel bottom, containing only water that will freeze unless proper winterization is applied.
• A heat tracing medium that has a maximum temperature hot enough to adversely affect the
 system (e.g., via thermal expansion or changes in protected fluid properties).
• Startup/shutdown operations. Examples are if a liquid subject to freezing is used instead of
 normal process fluid or if systems containing continuously flowing liquid subject to freezing
 during normal operation are stagnant during startup/shutdown. If freezing can occur during
 other than normal operations, proper winterization must be applied.
WINTERIZATION OF EQUIPMENT
The following winterization techniques are for equipment containing fluids that require

 308
 Page 7 of 10

                 KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                      Rev.: A

 protection.
 Tanks - The contents are warmed using internal coils.
 Vessels - Vessel mounted instruments (such as for level or pressure), their associated leads,
 and bridles containing a fluid requiring freeze protection should be heat traced.
 Exchangers - The contents are drained when the exchanger is not in operation. Air coolers
 may require freeze protection techniques incorporated into the design, such as warm air
 recirculation.
 Pumps - Pumps with spare pumps on ready standby containing a liquid requiring freeze
 protection should have either the suction and discharge manifold piping to both pumps
 heat traced or a restricted flow bypass (normally ¾" with restriction orifice) around the
 discharge check valve to ensure continuous circulation. Usually in this type of service the
 pump bodies are insulated but not traced. Pumps that are normally idle and not subject to
 immediate startup are freeze protected by draining.
 Machinery - Sometimes a shelter against freezing weather is provided for major machinery.
 For "severe" climate: air intakes on fans, blowers, and compressors should be fitted with an
 inverted hood arrangement, or equivalent, to minimize intake of rain, snow, and dust.
 Warm air recycling, direct suction heating, or other means should be provided to prevent
 ice buildup at air intakes, e.g., suction screens or filters.
 FREEZE PROTECTION OF INSTRUMENTS AND INSTRUMENT LINES
 The P&ID identifies all instruments that require heat tracing. If the line is traced, the
 instrument is traced with the same type of tracing. If the line is not traced but the
 instrument requires tracing, the P&ID indicates tracing type for the instrument. Local
 instruments and their leads are freeze protected using the same technique as the attached
 line. Vessel mounted instruments (such as for level or pressure), their associated leads, and
 bridles containing a fluid requiring freeze protection are heat traced. Instrument leads on
 process air lines are sometimes heat traced to prevent freezing of accumulated water.

5. HEAT TRACING AND CONSERVATION

 HEAT TRACING METHODS
 STEAM TRACING
 Steam tracing systems utilize steam at elevated temperatures to transfer heat to a
 pipeline. The steam is contained in a tube or a small pipe attached to the pipe being
 traced. The condensate is recovered in the condensate system.

 309
 Page 8 of 10

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                      Rev.: A

 ELECTRIC TRACING
 Electric tracing systems convert electric power to heat and transfer it to the pipe and
 its contained ﬂuid. The majority of commercial electric heat-tracing systems in use
 today are of the resistive type and take the form of cables placed on the pipe. When
 current ﬂows through the resistive elements, heat is produced. Other specialized
 electric tracing systems make use of impedance, induction, and skin conduction
 effects to generate and transfer heat.
SELECTION CRITERIA FOR TRACING METHODS
Factors to consider in selecting a heat tracing system.
• Relative cost of different types of heat-tracing.
• Availability of steam and/or electricity.
• Amount of heat loss which must be made up.
• Requirements for temperature control.
SPECIAL TRACING METHODS
Additional heating methods that may be considered.
 JACKETED PIPE
 Jacketed piping provides the most uniform application of heat to the process and
 maintains the most uniform process temperatures. Jacketed piping is used most
 frequently with processes that have a narrow temperature range, require very
 uniform temperature maintenance, and/or must have maximum heat input for melt-
 out or heat exchanger service. Jacketed piping features a core pipe, which is
 completely surrounded by a jacket pipe. Process flows through the core pipe, while a
 heating medium (or cooling medium) flows in the annular space between the core
 and jacket pipes.
 SKIN EFFECT HEAT TRACING
 The skin effect current tracing system is an electrically traced pipe heating system
 designed to provide heat to the carrier pipe evenly along the insulated pipeline.
 The heating element consist of a ferromagnetic tube, that is welded to the pipe
 which needs to be heated, and a special electrical cable that is placed into the
 ferromagnetic tube. When the electricity is on, the electrical power flows over the
 inside of the ferromagnetic tube and generates heat that is carried over through
 contact to the pipe surface.

 310
 Page 9 of 10

                  KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PR-G-PHL-003                             Rev.: A

 This type of system is used for long pipelines.
 HEAT CONSERVATION
 Heat conservation insulation is used for all vessels, equipment and piping where the fluid
 temperature is required to be maintained or heat loss is not expected. Heat conservation
 will be applicable for:
 • Fluid which has been heated by passing through heat exchange equipment.
 • Fluid from which process heat is to be recovered.
 • Fluid which will become too viscous if allowed to cool.
 • Superheated or saturated steam lines.

6. OTHER TYPES OF INSULATION

 PERSONNEL PROTECTION
 Personnel protection insulation shall be used for accessible piping and equipment (including
 vendor packages) operating above 140°F or at 14°F or below:
 • Application of personnel protection shall be restricted to locations within 6.6 ft vertically and 3.3
 ft horizontally from any platform, stairway passageway, ladders and stairs, which are normally
 used by operating personnel during normal plant operation.
 • Insulation for personnel protection shall only be applied if the surface is not insulated for other
 reasons (e.g. heat conservation/cold conservation or noise).

1. CONSTRUCTABILITY VIP WORKSHOP SUMMARY
 INTRODUCTION
  RTI International, with CEMEX Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., are participating in a joint effort to perform a
  Front-End Engineering Design (FEED) study for carbon dioxide (CO2) capture from cement flue gas.
  The FEED study will evaluate the integration of a CO2 capture system at the Balcones Cement Plant
  in New Braunfels, Texas. This document establishes the Constructability workshop basis for the NAS
  FEED study for the construction and operation of the proposed CO2 capture unit.
  KBR selected the Value Improving Practice (VIP) of Constructability as applied to major projects, and
  promulgated by the Construction Industry Institute, to conduct a project Constructability Review.
  Pinnacle Results, LLC was hired to facilitate this review.
 OBJECTIVES
  The Constructability VIP workshop, which was held on May 9-10, 2024 at the CEMEX Office in New
  Braunfels, Texas, included the assessment, development, and implementation of value improving
  items, and potential future design and execution decisions during the following stage of the project.
  The objectives of the Constructability VIP exercises are generally to:
 • Provide insights that could provide a logical and systematic manner for integrating potential
 construction methods into the project as it moves forward.
 • Matching the design intentions with good construction practices during the FEED study.
 • Understand major construction methods suitable for this project.
 • Identify various project site construction layout options.
 • Identify construction methods that could improve the construction schedule.
 • Examine alternative logistical opportunities available at the proposed site.
 • Identify sources of materials and equipment that could be used for construction.
  The following project specific information was available to the participants prior to the workshop to
  familiarize themselves with the project scope:
 • Technical Basis of Design
 • Plot Plan
 • Equipment Lists
 • Process Flow Diagrams

 358
 Page 4 of 17

                   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-001                              Rev.: A

2. CONSTRUCTABILITY REVIEW SUMMARY
 GENERAL
 During the constructability review, several design/construction challenges were considered
 with their impacts to construction access, laydown areas, and construction sequencing.
 These design challenges were grouped into two categories: those due to site conditions or
 requirements, and those due to equipment size, as summarized below. Additional access,
 laydown, and sequencing considerations are further discussed in Sections 2.2, 2.3, and 2.4.
 The proposed location for the CO2 capture plant is on a greenfield plot, South of the New
 Braunfels facility, with a plot plan of about 15 acres. Additional Cemex property is available
 to the South and West of the plot plan for potential laydown areas.
 There are currently plans for Cemex to perform a Geotechnical survey of the plot area. It is
 also recommended that an exploratory excavation survey and soil testing for hazardous
 materials be performed based on greenfield unknowns.
 DESIGN CHALLENGES DUE TO SITE CONDITIONS / REQUIREMENTS
 Cooling Water Constraints
 Due to limited make-up water availability at the New Braunfels facility, cooling water studies
 were conducted to determine the appropriate utility configuration for process cooling. The
 cooling system optimization study resulted in utilizing air coolers for a total of 5 services, with
 146 bays of cooling, with an estimated total plot area of approximately 4 acres of the total
 15-acre facility plot plan (not including retention pond or laydown areas).
  Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
 High Water Table Level
 CEMEX advised during the constructability review that the proposed plot area has a high-
 water table of 7 feet. The project team agreed to minimize underground services to the
 maximum extent possible and only consider items such as equipment drains and grey water
 services. A high-water table could also potentially require an engineered drainage system,
 such as a well point system. This system may require a collection pond.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
 Carbon Capture Plant Located Off-Site
 Due to the limited available area at the existing Cemex plant for the carbon capture plant,
 the CC plant is remotely sited across WALD Road. This requires routing a large 10 ft x 10 ft
 cross sectional flue gas duct approximately 1,500 feet, across the road, to the DCC/absorber
 columns.

 361
 Page 7 of 17

           KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-001                          Rev.: A

  Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
 CEMENT Facility is Governed by MSHA not OSHA
The CEMEX New Braunfels site falls under the jurisdiction of the Mine Safety and Health
Administration (MSHA). The Project Team acknowledged there could be considerable
differences between MSHA and OSHA regulations which may impact design, construction,
commissioning, and eventual operations of the CC plant. MSHA or OSHA jurisdiction will need
to be investigated and established to ensure design and construction safety implications are
clearly understood by the detail design and construction stakeholders.
A high cement dust content exists in the plant and the adjacent quarries. Another safety
consideration, whether MSHA or OSHA applies, is caution for proper protective personnel
equipment due to the potential for air contaminants in the proposed work areas. An adverse
effect on productivity, cost and schedule may be influenced by lack of training and PPE
specifications.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: SAFETY
 DESIGN CHALLENGES DUE TO EQUIPMENT SIZE
 Absorber Column
The absorber column selected may be based upon construction technology developed over
many years for FGD vessels. The concept is shown below.

 o
This field erection strategy including laydown and sequencing would require careful planning
and execution as the absorber column is closely located with other critical processing
equipment. The field fabrication of the Absorber Column must be supported by a detailed
construction schedule. The erection strategy must consider the long-term impact on the rest
of the project. The bidders shall provide the amount of area it requires at the foundation site
for how long and the type of heavy equipment they plan to use.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING

 362
 Page 8 of 17

 Packaged Boilers
 Steam demand can be met with (2) 50% packaged boiler, each nominally rated at 300,000
 Lbs/hr. This size is at the upper end of the boiler supplier’s packaged boiler design capacity,
 each 25.5 ft H x 22.3 W x 52 ft L. Each boiler weighs approximately 150 tons. In the event the
 logistics study finds this size boiler is not truckable, smaller size boilers are available, in a (3)
 x 33% configuration. Cost impacts based upon previous studies are close between these two
 boiler configurations.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS
 Interchanger HX Quantity and Size
 Due to the heat exchange required between the absorber column and regenerator, (16) plate
 and frame HXs are required. The HXs banks are in a section of the facility that facilitates
 construction access and laydown. Stacking of the plate and frame HXs was considered by the
 project team but determined to present concerns with maintenance access during the
 cleaning of the HX plates and subsequent draining of wastes and cleaning solvents in the
 structure.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
 Stripper Column
 The Stripper column due to its diameter is being evaluated for both shop fabrication and field
 erection execution strategy. Discussions with vessel fabricators in Texas suggest some shops
 can shop fab 26 ft. and preliminary feedback also suggests truck transport is feasible. A final
 decision on stripper column fabrication strategy will need to be made once logistics study is
 conducted.
  Potential Constructability Impacts: ACCESS, LAYDOWN and SEQUENCING
 CONSTRUCTION ACCESS
 LOGISTICS STUDY
Carbon Capture necessitates deployment of large equipment along with large pre-assembled
units to drive a cost-effective solution. The logistics study will eventually be needed to determine
the limitations for truckable modules and pre-assembles units (PAUs). There is no waterway
access for barge shipment of units. Logistics study should be from point of origin to site (Logistics
study establishes shipping envelope and also determines best method for shipment of heavy haul
items).

 363

               KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-001                            Rev.: A

Based upon discussions with some local fabrication shops in Texas, the equipment selected (as
described above in the GENERAL section) should be truckable to the New Braunfels site.
However, this needs to be validated by the logistics study.
Cement plant is surrounded by residential property, large cement quarries, rail lines and heavy
traveled truck route from quarries to cement plant. Cemex has indicated there is truck congestion
outside of the plant, particularly in the early mornings, which could impact site access.
 ELECTRICAL AND COMMUNICATION OVERHEAD LINES
Overhead high voltage 138kv power lines, bisecting the plant East-West, must be managed.
Preliminary discussions with the local utility suggest raising the HV power lines and power and
communication lines on Wald Road can be handled to accommodate transportation of
equipment to site.
 ACCESS TO POTENTIAL LAYDOWN AREA ON EXISTING CEMEX PLANT
The East area of the CEMEX property is a potential laydown area, however, access to the area is
sometimes blocked due to loading and staging of rail cars. Develop strategic transport plans for
trucks to enter site during off-peak (if possible) and consider all alternate routes.
 SOUTH PLOT ACCESS
The plot plan area South of Wald Road can get saturated and can cause vehicles / trucks to get
stuck. Consider all routes and have a fallback plan to remove vehicles from mud if necessary.
 CONSIDER RAIL FOR SHIPMENT OF LARGER EQUIPMENT
CEMEX New Braunfels site has existing rail for feed and product loadout. CEMEX mentioned
this rail may have limitations regarding size and especially width of cargo. This aspect of rail
limitations should be addressed in the logistics study to confirm load/sizing limitations, if any.
 LAYDOWN AREAS
 LAYDOWN AREA: PLOT PLAN SITE CONSTRAINTS ON OPTIMUM LAYDOWN AREA
The plot area as currently envisioned does not provide what may be considered the “ideal”
laydown space (relative to a “factor” of total plot area). Considering the large area of the air
coolers and utilizing some of the corners of adjacent facility designated space; the strategy of
‘just-in-time’ deliveries was considered one key approach to maintain maximum laydown areas
during peak construction activities.
The following equipment was identified as candidates for ‘just-in-time’ delivery.
  Air coolers
  Flue Gas duct sections

 364
                                                                                   Page 10 of 17

               KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-001                           Rev.: A

 ▪ Tray and packing installation and storage
 TEMPORARY CONSTRUCTION FACILITIES
Laydown areas shall also consider temporary construction facilities, which could be up to 2.0
times the total build area, depending on the project. This is to allow for the following temporary
facilities need to support construction:
 ▪ CM Office, Supervisor's offices, Safety facility, Quality office, Permit office,
 Warehouse (including Warehousing of temperature control sensitive equipment),
 Welfare tents (Break tents), Area for stockpiling re-usable and non-reusable soil
 SEQUENCING OF CONSTRUCTION
 LIFTING PLANS
The development of lifting plans/procedures should be completed early. Due to the constraints
mentioned above, this will be critical to orchestrate crane usage, costs, and safety.
 SEQUENCING OF EQUIPMENT
Development of proper sequencing of key equipment deliveries and installation such as
Absorber Column, DCC, Boilers, Air coolers.
The optimum path of construction and schedule will need to reflect a staging of key erection
and lift and setting plans for the critical pieces of equipment identified in Section 2.1.2
Engage an EPC for final path of construction and develop appropriate Advanced Work Packages
(AWP). The following can be packaged by the project EP team:
 EWP – Engineering work package
 PWP – Procurement work package
 CWP – Construction work package
 IWP – Installation work package (The IWP process is typically a function of the
 construction execution contractor)
 TIE-IN SEQUENCING OF CEMENT PYRO PROCESS
Develop the sequence for shutdown / cooldown of the cement pyro process system for tie-in of
CC Plant equipment. Tie-ins must be planned during schedule outages. Ensure PHA review
addresses tie-in logic and cut over in design.
Consider a complete field survey of each tie-in point, irrespective of discipline. Then provide a
tie-in package for each tie-in which can include a location drawing, P&ID, data sheets,
photographs and description and purpose of the tie-in.

 365
                                                                                  Page 11 of 17

                     KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-001                           Rev.: A

 TIE-IN SEQUENCING OF ELECTRIC POWER SOURCES
 Investigate electric power sources and eventual tie-ins. In support of commissioning activities,
 identify power sources and early tie-in strategy if needed. It is recommended to consider
 whether alternative power sources are needed.
 CONSTRUCTION SEQUENCING PATH
 The path should set up at a minimum the following objectives:
 o A construction execution schedule
 o High level Construction Organization
 o HSSE Strategy
 o QA Strategy
 o QC Strategy
 o Path of construction (Plot plan, CWA)
 o HHR and HHL Plans
 o TCF Layout and work sequence
 o HSSE
 o Logistics (project freight)
 o Logistics strategy (Local to plant traffic/workers)
 o HHL/HHL Strategy

1. INTRODUCTION

 PROJECT QUICK SUMMARY
Research Triangle Institute International is developing a non-aqueous Solvent (NAS) CO2 capture
process as an advanced, next generation post combustion capture technology for CO2 removal from
power generation and industrial flue gas streams. The technology for this project is owned by Research
Triangle Institute International.
The CO2 capture plant is designed to remove at least 95% of CO2 from the flue-gas from the Balcones
cement plant kilns K1 and K2, and the associated natural gas boiler for steam generation. The captured
CO2 has a purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, polished to meet pipeline specifications, and compressed
to be made ready for transportation. The capture plant consists of an integrated direct contact cooler
(DCC) and SO2 polisher, a flue gas blower, an absorption column with intercoolers, a regeneration
column, associated pumps, storage and receiver vessels, and CO2 compression, dehydration, and
polishing train.
 SITE LOCATION PLAN
The proposed site is at CEMEX Balcones cement plant in New Braunfels, TX.

 Latitude: North 29.709°
 Longitude: West 98.046°
 Plant Area: South of Wald Rd
 Elevation Above Sea Level: 645 ft (196.6 meters)
 Total Plot Area: Approximately 15 acres
 Boundary Description: See plot below highlighted in Red

 376
 Page 4 of 22

                     KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PM-G-PLN-003 Rev.: 0

3D Model Snip

 PURPOSE OF THIS DOCUMENT
As a part of FEED, KBR has developed the Level 2 EPC schedule, and the objective of this document is
to narrate the basis and assumptions considered while developing the Schedule. It also documents
major Project strategy and decisions that will act as a guideline for project execution to meet the
project objectives.

This Basis of Schedule broadly covers the following:

 • Key Durations
 • Key Milestones
 • Basis & Assumptions
 • Detailed Design/ Engineering
 • Procurement
 • Construction

 377
 Page 5 of 22

                     KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PM-G-PLN-003 Rev.: 0

 This schedule is developed by KBR to enable the key milestones for the Engineering, Procurement
 and Construction functions to be established. The project scope is not exhaustively represented in
 the schedule). The level 2 EPC schedule is developed with the best of available details, feed
 deliverables and discussion with subject matter experts. Upon contract award a more detailed Level
 3 EPC schedule must be developed.

 UNITS
The various units present in this project are

 • Unit 01- CO2 Capture
 • Unit 02- CO2 Compression, Dehydration, and Polishing
 • Utility Systems

Detailed Description of the units

Unit 01 - CO2 CAPTURE
This unit contains the Direct Contact Cooler, the Absorber with integrated water wash and acid wash
sections, the Lean/Rich Solvent Exchange, and the Regenerator.

UNIT 02 – CO2 COMPRESSION, DEHYDRATION, AND POLISHING
The CO2 product gas from the Regenerator is sent to the CO2 compression unit. The CO2 stream is
compressed and conditioned to meet pipeline specifications for transport.

UNIT 03 – UTILITY SYSTEMS

Unit 15 – Cooling Water System

8. CONSTRUCTION ASSUMPTIONS

 INTRODUCTION
Construction is a crucial stage in project execution and typically follows the design phase.
 CONSTRUCTION WBS
The construction part of the schedule is split into 6 as listed as follows
 • Early Works
 • Underground Works
 • Above Ground Works
 • Flue Gas Run
 • Commissioning and Startup
 EARLY WORKS
The construction part of the schedule is considered to start 8 months after the award of contract. The
first activities in construction are considered the Early works, The Early works consists of the site
clearance if required, contractor mobilization. After this temporary site facilities are set up. During this
time period the engineering should be mature enough to provide the required details to site for start
of any construction related activities. We have early IFC drawings after the 30% model review.

Once the site is cleared, we have the site preparation activities. Site Survey works, Expansive Soil
Removal, Filling and Compaction are considered in this activity.

 UNDERGROUND WORKS
The underground work is considered any work that needs to be done below the site level. It includes
the following but not limited to excavation, installation of underground tanks, fire water ring mains,
collection heads and backfilling followed by compaction.

 ABOVE GROUND WORKS
 1. The above ground works consists of all the foundation works, steel works, main equipment erection,
 pipe rack erection, cable tray, cable pulling, substation works, instrument erection, painting and road
 works
 Foundation / Civil Works
 2. Steel works
 3. Erection
 4. Piping works
 5. Electrical & Instrumentation Works
 6. Painting, Installation & Finishing works

 389
                                                                                                Page 17 of 22

                      KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-PM-G-PLN-003 Rev.: 0

 FOUNDATION AND CIVIL WORKS
The Pipe rack foundation consists of foundation required for the pipe racks inside the plant. Then the
equipment foundation works is started. During the equipment. The pre-engineered building
foundation works are done in parallel to the equipment foundation works. The paving work and road
work are also considered under this section. The Roads and paving work run in parallel to the
commissioning.

 STEEL WORKS
The steel works are split into primary steel structure and secondary steel structure. The primary steel
structure defined all the structural works required for the main equipment foundation and the
secondary steel refers to all the other steel works.

 ERECTION
Equipment Erection
Upon arrival of the equipment at site, they will be erected directly on the equipment foundations.
There is no layover time considered for this. The erection of Absorber and direct contact cooler is
around 10 months and the longest erection activity. The air cooler will arrive in batches and be erected.
The air cooler erection will commence soon after the first batch is received at site.

Electrical and Instrument Erection

The substation works are also covered under this section which includes but not limited to incoming
power supply cable works, substation erection, main equipment erection, DCS erecting, cable tray
laying, Cable pulling and then glanding and termination. The instrument erection is also shown under
a separate section.

Piping erection

The Piping Works considered are Piping prefabrication work, erection of pipes and flanges and
Hydrotesting.

Finish Works

Once all the installation work is complete, we start the finishing activities. This section consists of the
following activities. Insulate General Area Instruments, Equipment & Pipework, Paint Area Pipework,
Equipment & Pipe racks.

 FLUE GAS RUN
There is a separate section shown in the schedule for the duct work to the existing cement plant. It
includes excavation, foundation, backfilling, racks and associated duct works.

9. COMMISSIONING AND START UP

Commissioning is the process that confirms all components of a system are designed, installed, tested,
and maintained to perform according to the owner's requirements and design intent. The
commissioning is a significant step before the trail run to ensure that the plant is ready to start
processing the raw materials.

The commissioning starts after the erection of all electrical, mechanical and Instrumentation
equipment and components. The commissioning in this schedule also involves the site testing of the
equipment (no-load and load test). The loop check is also part of the commissioning activities

In this project commissioning is considered to be 3 months duration

1. PROJECT REVIEW:

 1.1 Project Information:

 The Research Triangle Institute (RTI) International requested a Front-End Engineering &
 Design (FEED) study to build a greenfield capture facility at the CEMEX Balcones Plant in New
 Braunfels, Texas USA. The facility removes carbon dioxide (CO2) from either power
 generation or industrial flue gas streams.

 The CO2 capture plant is designed to remove at least 95% of CO2 from the flue gas from the
 Balcones Cement Plant kilns K1 and K2, which collectively produce CO2 emissions of 1.6 MM
 metric tonnes per year, and the associated natural gas boiler for steam generation. The
 captured CO2 has a purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, polished to meet pipeline
 specifications, and compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara) to be made ready for sequestration.
 The CO2 capture plant consists of an integrated direct contact cooler (DCC) and SO2 polisher,
 a flue gas blower, an absorption column with intercoolers, a regeneration column, associated
 pumps, storage and receiver vessels, and a CO2 compression, dehydration, and polishing
 train. CO2 product end use (sequestration, EOR, etc.) will be determined in future phases.

 420
 Page 5 of 26

              KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                         Rev.: A

1.2 Construction Introduction:

The goal of KBR’s CM team (CMT) is to provide critical management and logistical
contributions to the construction phase. This includes instilling cohesion in communication
and purpose, cultivating a culture of safety, adhering to standards of quality, and meeting
schedule and cost requirements. KBR CMT will draw on the unique advantage of working
with a general contractor (GC) as well as subcontractors, whichever is the best for the success
of the project.

1.3 Construction Execution Overview

The project area south of Wald Road and the length of the low duct supports area needs to
be over-excavated by 9’ since the soil is not suitable for structural backfill. This excavated soil
will need to be relocated to an approved landfill off-site. Once the areas are excavated
Imported non-expansive soil fill will to be brought into the site and compacted as needed to

                 KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                      Rev.: A

 meet the elevations for the new foundations and or pilings. During early site prep work
 preparation for the laydown and parking areas will begin at the site. The duct work inside the
 Cemex facility will be treated as a separate construction activity based on the information we
 have at this time. The path of construction will be based on the WBS information used during
 the FEED using the below list.

 • Unit 01 – CO2 Capture System
 • Unit 02 – CO2 Compression, Dehydration, and Polishing

3. CMT ROLES & RESPONSIBILITIES

 3.1 Construction Management Responsibilities

 The Construction Management Team (CMT) delivers several essential functions necessary for
 the operation of projects. These functions include:

 • Supervisory

 423
 Page 8 of 26

             KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                        Rev.: A

 • Quality Control
 • HSSE Management (including field safety professionals)
 • Project Controls
 • Scheduling
 • Material Management
 • Administrative
 • Document Control

The CMT is responsible for day-to-day construction operations, material warehousing, direct
work progress reporting, scheduling and cost reporting and field engineering support. The
objective of this group is to detail and refine the path of construction, develop the Level 2-4
schedules and the weekly work plan, establish construction control tools, and coordinate the
sourcing and processing of materials.

3.2 Construction Management Roles

 3.2.1 Construction Manager

 The construction manager (CM) reports to the project manager. The CM will be
 responsible for the development of a construction team that will be integrated with
 engineering and procurement. The CM will assist with the development of an
 integrated EPC schedule. As the project mobilizes, the CM will be the senior
 construction representative onsite and, through his staff, be responsible for all site
 activities. The CM will be the KBR designated representative to CEMEX on site for any
 communications concerning management and coordination of the work. The CM
 provides formal direction to the project team members to ensure that the project is
 executed in accordance with the Project Execution Plan and safety, quality, cost and
 schedule goals are successfully met.

 3.2.2 Field Superintendent

 The field superintendent (Supt.) reports to the Construction Manager. Each Supt. is
 responsible for the day-to-day coordination, supervision, and field administration of
 the construction work activities in their respective disciplines. The Supt. will work
 closely to improve productivity and provide efficient project completion activities. The
 Supt. will ensure that activities between the various crafts are closely coordinated,

 424
 Page 9 of 26

      KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                       Rev.: A

construction equipment utilization is managed, and project procedures related to
safety and quality standards are upheld. This project will utilize field Supt. with
respective knowledge in mechanical, electrical and instrumentation (E&I), and
civil/structural/architectural (CSA) disciplines.

3.2.3 HSEE Manager

The HSSE Manager functionally reports to the CM and administratively to KBR’s
corporate HSSE manager. The HSSE Manager is responsible for coordinating the
Project HSSE Program with RTI, and all Subcontractors on the project. During Stage 2
design, HSE Manager will develop the Project HSE Site Specific Plan and supporting
project safety procedures. The HSE Plan is a comprehensive safety and health program
encompassing a system for total loss prevention and control. This system is based
upon the principles:
• People are the Company’s most important asset
• All accidents and injuries are preventable
• Health Safety and environmental protection is a line management responsibility
• Management has the responsibility to train employees to work safely and to
 develop a “Zero Harm” culture.
 The HSSE Manager will monitor the overall safety performance of the project,
identifying unsafe conditions and procedures, and recommending and insuring the
implementation of corrective actions. The HSSE Manager ensures safety observations
are tabulated and analyzed for trends and opportunities to reinforce safe behaviors.
The HSSE Manager has a thorough knowledge of the federal, state, safety, fire, and
health regulations and a working knowledge of hazardous gases and chemicals as well
as the appropriate detection devices. The HSEE Manger will provide safety
information and guidance to the project team on proper safety procedures, current
and potential hazards and employee training requirements. This role will act as a
“coach” to all subcontractors and direct hire supervision to make certain that safe
work practices are the top priority for all personnel. The HSSE Manager is responsible
for the establishment and maintenance of all HSSE record keeping systems.

3.2.4 Quality Manager

Quality Manager (QM) is responsible for the establishment of the QC programs in the
field and for providing the technical direction during implementation at the site. The

 425
                                                                         Page 10 of 26

                  KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                      Rev.: A

 QM will develop a construction quality plan during Stage 2 design and will incorporate
 CEMEX site QA/QC requirements.
 The QM leads the selection and assignment of QC personnel and is responsible for
 their indoctrination, training, and certification.

 3.2.5 Materials Manager

 The Materials Manager will report directly to the CM. They will be responsible for
 field coordination of receiving all project furnished materials and plant equipment for
 construction and for transferring care, custody and control to the crafts and
 subcontractors. They will also be responsible for the field purchased materials and
 will approve all material and plant equipment invoices for payment. Material Manager
 supports project procurement and materials personnel and their associated activities
 to execute the work in a manner that achieves the objectives of the Project Materials
 Plan.

 3.3 Interfacing Parties

 The Carbon Capture project pulls together many integral engineering and construction teams.
 KBR will be responsible for overall stewardship of the interface management (IM process) at
 RTI's direction. Details of construction interface communication are discussed in Section 8.
 Major project interfacing parties affecting construction are:

 • RTI
 • KBR
 • CEMEX
 • Contractors

 4.1 EPC Pre-Planning

 The construction manager will be an integral part of the project management staff during FEL
 and pre-construction phase of the project. The construction manager will provide input to
 engineering and materials management and review construction execution plans.

 426
                                                                                    Page 11 of 26

              KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                        Rev.: A

In addition to providing input to the project controls procedures, the construction team will
also help identify construction control areas (work breakdown structure). These control areas
will be referenced on all engineering design drawings.
CMT will develop and implement a comprehensive and proactive constructability program
that will involve timely input to detailed engineering and materials management. Timely
inputs will be provided during the development of plot plans, specifications, drawings, and
where applicable, vendor data. The constructability program will be fully integrated with all
project entities and will strive to provide cost effective and timely input into the project work
processes.

4.4 Modularization & Pre-Assembly

Based on the technology capacity, design, large size erect in place columns, tanks, and very
large sized equipment, there is minor potential for modularization for the Carbon Capture
Plant during the FEED activities. Piperacks, Piping, Ductwork, may be preassembled at a local
fabrication shop or at the project site. Pre-engineered Buildings, Large Tanks, Absorber
Column, and the Direct Contact Cooler will be assembled on site.
Steel for air-coolers will be site erected and in place prior to the coolers arriving at site.
Cranes will be used to set modules. Hook-up interface locations will be made at deck level to
minimize the amount of scaffolding required.

4.6 Construction Completion

The CMT will manage project completion including inspection and testing, with complete
quality control documentation. The project will be systematically turned over to a
Commissioning Team to commence commissioning.
Prior to testing and turnover, Construction will coordinate with commissioning to jointly
inspect system. This is to ensure no changes are needed for safety or operability before final
construction testing. When a system is declared construction complete and turned over for
commissioning, the care and custody become the responsibility of the Commissioning Team
thus bringing the complete system.

 4.6.1 Turnover System

 If a Turnover system is needed for a project, KBR uses TurnOver System Tracking and
 Reporting (TOSTR). TOSTR is designed to monitor and inform the status of gradual
 transfer of care, custody, and control of the project to the owner by means of
 organizing and monitoring the status of the documentation required to certify the
 construction completeness of pre-designated portions of the job known as “Turnover
 Systems”. The program can utilize Tablet PCs to capture electronic Quality Control
 sign-off forms.
 The program utilizes information generated by the Project Engineering Design teams,
 including Equipment Mechanical and Electrical Lists, Pipeline Line List, Tie-Ins and
 Isometrics Lists, Instrument Index, and Relief Valve List to organize and cross-
 reference all components and items by "Turnover System."

 Some status reports generated by TOSTR include:

 • Equipment Status
 • Electrical Status

 429
                                                                                 Page 14 of 26

      KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-CM-G-CP-003                    Rev.: A

• Packaged Equipment Status
• Piping Testpack Status
• Instrument Local Status
• Instrument Loop Status
• Painting and Insulation Status
• Miscellaneous Status
• Punch List Status

4.6.2 Pre-Commissioning

Pre-commissioning will be conducted by Construction with the support of
Commissioning and will commence once the system, or subsystem, is complete and
safe to place under pressure for the purpose of blowing, flushing, or energizing for
electrical and instrument testing.
Pre-commissioning consists of the following primary activities:
• Line cleaning - All system pipe and static equipment will be cleaned by air blowing
 or flushing as required.
• Reinstatement of all system equipment- Following the cleaning of all breakouts,
 valves, instrumentation, etc. will be reassembled using permanent specification
 gaskets.
• Vessel Inspection and final closure - The cleaning and inspection of internals
 installation will be witnessed and signed off by Commissioning.
• Instrument full loop checking - Loop checking will be witnessed and signed off by
 Commissioning.
• Bump the motor – Confirm terminations are correct on equipment.
• Cold alignment checks and coupling installation of rotating equipment
• Installation of temporary and permanent strainers and filters- witnessed by
 Commissioning.
• Function testing of electrical equipment - UPS, emergency and instrument power
 systems, switchgear, grounding, lighting, etc. - Lockout Tag out (LOTO) procedure
 will apply.

Pre-commissioning is complete when the relevant QC documentation and
construction completion checklist items are signed off. Further details regarding
commissioning will be provided in the future by the Commissioning Execution Plan.

6. HEALTH, SAFETY, SECURITY AND ENVIRONMENTAL
 Commitment to HSSE performance is foremost of all considerations in planning and execution
 of work. The primary focus for the project is to execute with Zero Harm. Zero Harm is based
 on the understanding that all incidents and injuries can be prevented and that the behavior of
 all personnel on the project will demonstrate this belief.

 6.1 Health and Safety

 The CMT is committed to achieving the highest standards of HSSE performance on the project
 and in order to achieve this will apply the following:
 • Develop a Project Specific HSSE Management System that incorporates all project
 requirements.
 • Assign an experienced and dedicated Health, Safety, Security and Environmental
 Manager who will assist management with the administration and
 implementation of the HSSE program.
 • Create a culture where individuals understand and take responsibility for their
 health and safety as well as the health and safety of co-workers.
 Commitment will be demonstrated through visibility and communication by participation in
 safety program activities such as orientations, training sessions, meetings, jobsite safety walks
 and group safety talks.
 To further everyone’s understanding of their responsibilities, as well as understanding how to
 work safely, the HSSE program includes requirements for preparation of field level risk
 analyses and pre-task safety planning. The KBR Total Safe Task Instruction program (TSTI) will
 be implemented for this pre-task review. Each contractor’s supervisor is required to review
 their work activities and conduct a pre-task safety meeting to address hazards, required
 protective equipment, safe work methods and work area inspections.

 Jobsite and work area safety walks administered by the HSSE group will be scheduled under
 the direction of KBR management and will include subcontractors and area supervision.
 Unsafe acts and conditions will be recorded, and corrective actions taken immediately. If a
 condition cannot be corrected immediately, correction will be completed within the required
 or agreed time frame with a follow up inspection performed to confirm the condition is made
 safe.

 6.2 Security

 The project will use physical security measures and access control processes to protect
 personnel, facilities, and equipment. KBR will apply protective procedures and enforce

regulations within the project area. A KBR Security Execution Plan will be developed during
the EPCC phase and will contain full details of the security policies and procedures to be
implemented.
All personnel entering and exiting the project site will comply with the site security plan.
Managers have a responsibility to ensure that all personnel, contractors, and subcontractors
under their supervision adhere to and comply with security procedures.
All breaches of security procedures or road traffic incidents will be investigated to identify the
cause to prevent recurrence. When appropriate, disciplinary proceedings or contractual

the guard force. For the guard force PCT plans to engage professional security services with a
local security company.

As part of project execution, KBR will undertake a program of environmental monitoring to
assess the environmental impacts of activities during the construction phase as required by
local, state, and federal regulations.

observation and reporting is performed to satisfy all applicable regulatory requirements, and
project policies.
During the EPCC phase KBR will develop and implement a site-specific Construction
Environmental Management Plan (CEMP) to minimize impacts to the environment. The plan
will outline the processes, procedures, and administrative arrangements necessary to
maintain compliance with all relevant project requirements, and Federal, State, and local
regulations. The document will also include management approaches for coordinating with
suppliers and subcontractors, as well as auditing, reporting and review processes, which are
applicable to all aspects of environmental management. The CEMP will be a living document
and will be periodically evaluated and improved, based on lessons learned, potential
regulatory changes, changing operations, input from users and KBRs commitment to
continuous improvement.
 The plan will address:
 • Roles and responsibilities for KBR staff, contractors and third-party environmental

 • Environmental risk management, compliance and monitoring
 • Waste & Wastewater management
 • Spill prevention and clean-up
 • Incident management, reporting and investigation

 • Environmental performance indicators & Auditing

 6.3.1 Air Emissions

 Air emissions during the construction phase primarily consist of exhaust emissions
 from construction equipment, vehicles and dust emissions due to site preparation
 activities (clearing of vegetation and topsoil, excavation, placement and transport of
 soil, etc.) plus vehicle traffic on unpaved surfaces. Construction phase air emissions
 will be mitigated by a combination of site policies and conventional practices. For
 example, measures that may be employed are: a no idling policy for vehicles; the use
 of dust suppressants on active worksites and unpaved roads (either plain water or
 water with commercially available additives); and the use of sediment controls on
 exposed soil faces.

 6.3.2 Liquid Effluents

 Liquid effluents associated with the construction phase of the Project include:
 • Sanitary wastes,
 • Surface runoff,
 • Concrete wash water and leachate,
 • Hydrostatic test water,
 • Spills from temporary generators, re-fueling stations and other construction
 equipment utilized on the Project.
 Disposition of all these liquid effluents will be governed by federal, state, and local
 regulations, along with various construction permits also required.

 6.3.3 Sanitary Waste

 Waste from the job site toilet and washroom facilities will be collected and
 transported via tanker trucks to offsite facilities for proper disposal.

6.3.4 Spill Prevention & Response

The CEMP will detail the methods for preventing and responding to spills that may
occur during construction. These procedures will be consistent with the information
developed for the Local Emergency Response Plan.
Preventative measures include:
• Pre-trip equipment inspections,
• Regularly scheduled compliance audits
• Maintenance of an incident database and trend analysis
• Periodic worker education and awareness campaigns
• Toolbox talks and sharing of lessons learned.
• Project spill reporting thresholds will be established in consideration of KBR
 policies and regulatory requirements. Minor spills to land will be cleaned up by
 Gulfspan or Subcontractor staff.

6.3.5 Waste Management

KBR will work together to meet waste management requirements during all phases of
the project. These requirements are applicable to all personnel generating waste,
including subcontractors. Gulfspan will rely on licensed third-party specialty
subcontractors for the disposal of waste generated during construction, municipal
solid waste, and hazardous materials. A central waste management facility will be
constructed early in the project to support the work activity. Where appropriate,
accumulated wastes will be covered, bonded, or otherwise protected to maintain a
neat orderly site that is safe, sanitary, and protected from vectors and wildlife.
Subcontractors will be responsible for maintaining their work areas in compliance with
the project policies. Upon commencement of the advanced-stage site preparation
work, Gulfspan will provide roll-off containers in the active work areas for collection of
bulk construction debris such as metal, wood waste, and concrete. Subcontractors will
be responsible for transferring their accumulated waste to the central waste
management area where it will be logged with the necessary paperwork and
manifests.
Hazardous materials will be stored in a separate area and incompatible or reactive
materials will be segregated. All hazardous materials will be handled and disposed of
in compliance with applicable local, state, and federal regulations. Waste manifests
and ‘cradle to grave’ documentation will be generated for all hazardous wastes.
Recyclable materials will be segregated at the source, collected separately, forwarded
for further processing, and returned to beneficial use where feasible.
KBR’s waste management procedures for the project will be detailed in the CEMP.

7. QUALITY CONTROL AND QUALITY ASSURANCE
 7.1 QC Plan

 The KBR Quality Plan defines the activities to assure that civil, buildings, structural, pipe,
 mechanical, electrical, and instrumentation, equipment and materials are assembled,
 installed, erected, inspected, and tested in accordance with the applicable engineering
 drawings, specifications, codes, proven practices, local regulations and project specific
 requirements; that conforms to ISO 9001(2008) standards. Site specific Construction QC
 Procedures will be developed for the Project and included in the Construction QC Procedures
 Manual.
 The KBR Quality Manager and associated staff are responsible for administration of the
 Quality Program and will oversee and coordinate with the Construction Team members who
 have the day-to-day responsibilities for installation. KBR field QC inspectors, under the
 direction of the Quality Manager will perform inspections and surveillances to confirm that
 the installation activities meet project requirements including verification of testing
 equipment.
 The KBR quality overview is implemented via a range of activities including:
 • Routine meetings that provide problem and performance comments as well as
 status/progress information
 • Close overview of work performance
 • Observance of inspections and tests per the Inspection and Test Plans (ITP)
 • Conducting independent inspections and tests at random points/events
 • Conducting audits of quality program implementation and documentation
 • Include project specific training and quality workshops
 The basis of quality control is the Inspection and Test Plan (ITP). The ITP lists specific
 witnesses and hold points and QC documentation for a particular work process. The
 management of the QC documentation is crucial to the smooth handover of the completed
 work by system and eventually the entire facility. The KBR Turnover Systems Tracking Report
 (TOSTR) will be used to track the status of the quality control documentation by discipline and
 by system. TOSTR tracks the completion of QC documentation at each milestone by discipline
 including exceptions and/or punch list items.

8.PROJECT COMMUNICATIONS
 The CMT will execute Request for Information (RFI) to clarify drawings and design
 specifications back to engineering team, issue Non-conformance Request (NCR) for any
 discrepancies by supplier or subcontractor and identify scope changes which could potentially
 require a Project Deviation Notice (PDN) to report any costs related to a deviation.

 The RFI/NCR/PDN process will be utilized to provide a uniform communication process,
 tracking system, and documentation between KBR, vendors, and RTI/CEMEX. This process
 satisfies the requirements and necessary approvals for construction, engineering,
 subcontractors, and vendors to request information, resolve non-conformances and to track
 rework and back charge costs. An RFI/NCR/PDN form will be used to request information on
 any construction question or problem, potential non-conformance, supplier deficiency,
 potential scope change, possible material substitution and requests for KBR engineering
 information or clarification. The form will be prepared and processed by CM or Supt.,
 assigned a log number and kept in an active log showing all RFIs/NCRs, including
 need/response dates, resolution, associated drawings, associated equipment, etc. If the RFI
 results in action deviating from original scope, a unique PDN number is maintained with the
 RFI Number referenced.

 The RFI process will be used by the field to modify all IFC documents (P&IDs, data sheets,
 vendor drawings, etc.) All red-line mark-ups incorporating as-built data will be tracked and
 closed via the RFI process with distribution to document control group for incorporation into
 the Turnover and Completion Package.

 A document control program will be put in place to ensure that current engineering drawings
 and documents are available and maintained for craft, subcontractors, and vendors. KBR will
 perform audits to ensure consistency and accuracy of data and quality assurance compliance
 to procedures and project requirements.

10. PROJECT CONTROLS
 10.1 Work Breakdown Structure

 Construction schedules, progress, and cost will follow the defined Work Breakdown Structure
 (WBS) for the project planning and scheduling. Working with Engineering and Procurement,
 the Construction Team will provide input to the project schedule to ensure drawings, material
 and equipment deliverables support construction need dates. CEMEX and KBR CMT will work
 together on a path of construction to define the most optimum sequence in progressing
 through the work areas. Material and equipment delivery requirements will be scheduled to
 support the path. The schedule will be tied to deliverables and will include all activities
 through construction completion to support pre-commissioning. This schedule will be in
 accordance with the agreed WBS and will be resource loaded once quantities and work hour
 estimates are complete.

 The primary tool to be used for the schedules is Primavera. The following are the construction
 schedules that will be prepared and included in the project schedule:
 • Level II Summary Schedule – The Summary Schedule is based on the Project
 Master Schedule and is the road map for the Level III Schedule. This schedule
 shows major milestones, activities per area, discipline, and work phase.
 • Level III Detailed Schedule – The Detailed Schedule is the management level
 control tool for monitoring and reporting the overall construction status. This
 schedule further details the activities in the Level II schedule and identifies critical
 paths and sets priorities for the execution of the work. The Detailed Schedule is
 precedence based, critical path method network. The network is tied to
 engineering and procurement deliverables for early identification of any delay or
 change that requires corrective action.
 • Level IV and Level V Working Schedule – based on the Level III schedule, the
 Working Schedule details activities to be performed daily and weekly.

 10.2 Work Hours and Progress

 Construction progress is milestone and quantity based and will be measured by direct work
 activity, by discipline within the WBS. The quantities of work, by activity, will be assigned unit
 work hours for installation. The total of the work hours by discipline as derived from the
 quantities will establish budget hours for each discipline. The total of all budget work hours
 for all disciplines will be the total direct construction work hours budgeted for the Project.
 The progress and productivity data derived from the progress measurement system will be
 the basis for workforce planning and forecasting.

11 LOGISTICS AND SITE MATERIAL CONTROL
 11.1 Logistics

 In KBR material control system, IPMS, each line item has an associated WBS designation, and
 that designation determines where that material will be dispatched to on-the-job site.
 Material shipments to the site will arrive at either the laydown yard or warehouse. The
 material management team will receive shipment and determine if material needs to be
 dispatched directly to the jobsite or to laydown area.

 11.2 Material Management

 The construction warehouse and storage areas will be established at the on the project site.
 Material control at site will be managed by the construction material control group, headed
 by the material control manager. The KBR material control group will manage the receipt,
 storage, and movement of material.
 All materials arriving at site will be inspected and recorded by the site material control group
 along with the respective construction quality control representative.
 The material control system that will be used is KBR IPMS. IPMS entries are initiated by home
 office procurement where all requisitions and purchase orders are entered. At site, IPMS is
 used to record receipts, record materials issued and maintain an inventory of materials onsite
 against purchase orders and requisitions. The system provides material status of undelivered
 material, material requirements by drawing, onsite storage location, and installation planning
 tools.

 The material control group will prepare all receiving documentation, inspect materials for
 compliance to the requisition specifications and quantities, prepare overage, shortage and
 damage reports, record site storage location, and send reports to and follow up with home
 office procurement on exceptions.
 The construction warehouse and lay down yard will accommodate required indoor storage as
 well as an area for materials which require climate-controlled storage. The warehouse and lay
 down yard are within fenced area of plant site.
 Material preservation measures will be implemented on all materials and equipment
 beginning with arrival onsite, during storage, during installation and through pre-
 commissioning and start up. Material preservation maintenance records will be maintained to
 verify measures taken.
