1. PURPOSE

This Basis of Estimate describes the methodology used to prepare the non-binding Class 3 estimate
(target +15%/-15% accuracy) of the Carbon Capture Plant for cement manufacturing. This estimate
was developed during Front End Loading (FEL-3) engineering and targeted as a Class 3 estimate
based on the Association for the Advancement of Cost Engineering (AACE) International practice
from the published Cost Estimate Classification System (18R-97, page 3, Rev. 8/7/20). KBR
Estimating used the Aspen Economic Evaluation tools to organize, extend, compile, and sort the
estimate data according to the agreed cost breakdowns. Specifically, Aspen Capital Cost Estimator
(ACCE) V14.2 was used for this project.

2. PROJECT INFORMATION

The Research Triangle Institute (RTI) International requested a Front-End Engineering & Design
(FEED) study to build a carbon capture facility at the CEMEX Balcones Plant in New Braunfels, Texas
USA. The facility removes carbon dioxide (CO2) from industrial flue gas streams.

The RTI technology uses a non-aqueous solvent and an absorber tower to capture CO2 from cooled
flue gas streams. The flue gas is washed to remove solvent and may be further treated to remove
emissions (particulates, sulfur, NOx, etc.). The stripped CO2 is then treated and compressed for
export. The study basis is to use the RTI CO2 capture system on a design feed rate of 2.4 MM metric
tons (tonne) of CO2 per year with an average capture of 1.6 MM metric tons (tonne) of CO2 per year
at a lower capacity. This includes the exhaust gas from two cement kilns with 1.4 MM metric tons
(tonne) of CO2 per year, along with the exhaust gas from the natural gas boiler of 0.2 MM metric
tons (tonne) of CO2 per year installed for the RTI process. Total CO2 capture is expected to be 95%
or greater.

3. GENERAL ESTIMATE INFORMATION

The main tool utilized to compile the estimate was the Aspen Economic Evaluation Capital Cost
Estimator, V14.2 version (ACCE). The tool relies on quantities and sizing information to generate
costs and field labor hours from its own database of costs. The estimate is made up of a combination
of sources of cost. The estimate was generated with the ACCE tool using regional cost conditions,
KBR historical costs information, and vendor budgetary quotes.

 3.1 CURRENCY
 The estimate was provided in U.S. Dollars since the project is in the United States.
 3.2 UNITS OF MEASURE
 The estimate was generated using the Imperial system of units.

 Page 4 of 12
 446

                KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                  Rev.: B

3.3 PROJECT WORK BREAKDOWN STRUCTURE (WBS)
 At this stage there is not a formal Work Breakdown Structure (WBS) for the project. The
 cost estimate lists items by unit number, tag number, description, and discipline as
 applicable. The unit numbers are listed below:
 • Unit 01 – CO2 Capture System
 • Unit 02 – CO2 Compression, Dehydration, and Polishing
 • Unit 11 – Raw, Potable, and Demineralized Water System
 • Unit 12 – Boiler & Steam System
 • Unit 14 – Solvent & Chemical Storage Utilities
 • Unit 15 – Cooling Water System
 • Unit 16 – Air / Nitrogen System
 • Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
 • Unit 20 – Stack
 • Unit 22 – Utility Headers
 • Unit 23 – Power Generation
 The unit numbers on the P&IDs were the basis for splitting the piping lines by unit
 number.
3.4 COST BASIS
 The estimate was generated in U.S. dollars ($) with a 4th Quarter 2024 cost basis. The
 ACCE base escalation indices were adjusted based on current market conditions in order
 to bring the system generated costs to “today’s” cost. Equipment quotes or costs
 provided by vendors, licensors, and contractors were brought up to the same overall cost
 basis. Forward-looking escalation was included in the estimate. The escalation is further
 explained in Section 9.
3.5 CONSTRUCTION BASIS
 The construction basis for the estimate was based on stick-built methods (non-modular).
 A Construction Execution Plan (CEP) was generated by the KBR Construction
 Management group where a preliminary contracting strategy was mentioned. Specific
 contractors will not be identified at this time, so the specific construction sequence will
 not be part of the CEP or the estimate. The field labor productivity was adjusted to local
 conditions based on information specific to the project along with experience from past
 KBR projects in the region. The construction work week was based on 5 days x 10 hours
 a day for 40 regular hours and 10 overtime hours with a 50% pay premium. The plan
 was for a single mobilization of the construction contractor, since multiple mobilizations
 of the same contractor would have higher costs and increase the time required to
 complete the project. There is expected to be constraints in the workspace, which may
 require multiple crafts to work at the same time. The contractors fabricating the large
 towers on-site like the absorber mentioned that a certain distance has to be maintained

                                      Page 5 of 12                                         447

                KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                    Rev.: B

 around these towers, which could possibly reduce the area available for other
 contractors to work on other tasks.
 The project execution basis is an EPCM approach, where all procurement is on client’s
 paper. An EPC type arrangement has not been considered in the costs or in the risk
 profiles that such arrangement may signify.
3.6 LABOR RATE AND PRODUCTIVITY
 The construction worker field labor productivity in the estimate is 70% (x1.43 hours
 relative to a baseline project), using local greenfield labor productivity for the work week
 duration, some work at heights, worker skill level, site congestion, worker turnover,
 rigging requirements, and potential weather delays. Even though the project is near San
 Antonio, there is expected to be some difficulty hiring craft workers based on current
 labor surveys published from Alpha Resources (10/4/24) considering the size of the job.
 A craft per diem (50% of workers x $96/day) is expected to attract some of the workers
 who live outside of the region. Delays or increased costs due to future pandemics like
 COVID are not considered in the productivity or in the estimate. These are force majeure
 events that are beyond the scope of the estimate.
 A contractor “All-in Wage Rate” (AIWR) for all construction disciplines was determined
 based labor surveys for the region. The breakdown of what was included in the AIWR is
 shown:
 • Partial All-In Field Wage Rate (AIWR) – The expected non-union / open shop partial
 all-in wage rate (AIWR) was determined based on information provided by
 construction. The wages in the estimate vary by craft type. The average all-in wage
 rate (AIWR) is $105.80/hr and broken down as follows:
 o Direct Labor Wage Rate – Base take home pay to construction worker.
 (30%)
 o Fringe Benefits & Burdens – Includes taxes, unemployment insurance,
 health benefits, vacation, holidays, sick pay, etc. (11%).
 o Overtime / Premium Time – Covers the 50% premium paid for the 10
 overtime hours each week (3%).
 o Craft Supervision– Foreman and overhead excluding construction
 management (15%).
 o Small Tools & Consumables – For items like drills, wrenches, welding
 electrodes, fuel used by workers (3%).
 o Field Services – G&A overhead and home office support (13%).
 o Construction Equipment Rental – For equipment rental (manlifts, small
 cranes, specialty equipment, etc.), mobilization/demobilization &
 maintenance (8%).

                                         Page 6 of 12                                        448

                   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                     Rev.: B

 o Temporary Facilities – Trailers, utilities, furniture, toilets, internet, etc.
 for the construction workers (7%). Construction Management (CM)
 temporary facilities is included in the Construction Management costs
 explained in Section 6.
 o Business Expenses – Covers office supplies and office equipment (1%).
 o Contractor Profit / Fee – Applied to the costs listed above for the
 construction firm (9%).
 *Scaffolding, Construction Management, Vendor Representatives, Start-up,
 Commissioning, and Per Diem are accounted for outside of the AIWR, and explained in
 Sections 6 and 7 of this Basis of estimate.

 3.7 PIPE EXECUTION STRATEGY
 The estimate assumed that typical process piping is fabricated in a local pipe
 fabrication facility and shipped to the site. Any small bore (<0.5” diameter) piping or
 specialty piping is fabricated in the field.

 3.8 STEEL EXECUTION STRATEGY
 The estimate assumed most steel installed in the field will be fabricated in a local steel
 fabrication facility and shipped to the site. The estimate is based on a fabricated
 cost/ton of steel, whether shop or field fabricated. Percentages of field vs shop will not
 be defined in the Basis of Estimate.

4. ISBL AND OSBL PLANT COSTS

 4.1 ENGINEERING DELIVERABLES
 The engineering deliverables were reviewed by estimating and included the key
 information listed below.
 • Equipment List – Number, type, capacity, size, design pressure, design temperature,
 etc.
 • Electrical Equipment List / Datasheets – Number, type, capacity, size, power, etc.
 • Electrical Material Take-Offs – Number, type, material, diameter, length, etc.
 • Lighting Plans – Number, type, mounting type, etc.
 • Control Systems Material Take-Offs – Number, type, location, capacity, material,
 diameter, length, etc.
 • Piping & Instrument Diagrams (P&ID’s) – To determine pipe fittings, valves, and
 associated instruments.
 • Piping Material Take-Offs – Number, material, length, diameter, thickness, fittings,
 valves, insulation, tracing, etc.

                                            Page 7 of 12                                         449

               KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                   Rev.: B

 • Piping Tie-in List – Location, number, material, diameter, etc.
 • Civil, Structural, Architectural Material Take-Offs – Number, location, materials,
 dimensions, volumes, strength, etc.
 • Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) Scope – Type, capacity, size,
 energy output, design pressure, design temperature, etc.
 • Fire Protection Scope – Number, equipment type, piping type, location, capacity,
 size, design pressure, design temperature, etc.
 • Project Schedule

4.2 VENDOR BUDGETARY QUOTES & CONTRACTOR ESTIMATES
 KBR provided the relevant information like the technical datasheets, requisition
 package, specifications, etc., to obtain vendor budgetary quotes. The budgetary
 quotes were checked against the associated design specifications to see what is
 included and excluded. Any items excluded from the quote were estimated with
 allowances. Equipment quotes were obtained on all critical mechanical equipment,
 which make up 94% of the total mechanical equipment costs in the estimate. In
 addition, quotes were obtained on most electrical equipment and instruments. In
 general, the lowest technically viable quotes were considered for the estimate, but
 averages were used in a few cases due to uncertainty in the accuracy of the quote and
 items included by the vendor.
 Contractor quotes were also included for Power Overhead Feeder, Gatehouse,
 Warehouse, ECR Building, Control Room & Change Room Building, Maintenance
 Building, Lab, and Compressor Sheds.
 • The combined value of the vendor budgetary quotes and contractor estimates make up
 66% of the total project equipment & material costs. The remaining bulk materials and
 equipment were estimated by Aspen Capital Cost Estimator (ACCE) indexed based on
 KBR experience.

4.3 CAPITAL SPARES
 The capital spares are included in the estimate as estimated by KBR ($5.6 MM). All
 other spares are excluded and should be determined by the client in the Owner’s costs.

4.4 SITE SOIL FILL & DISPOSAL
 The project area south of Wald Road and the length of the low duct supports area will
 need to be over-excavated by 9’ since the soil (expansive soils) is not suitable for
 structural backfill, as per the CEMEX new Geotechnical Report. This excavated soil will

                                     Page 8 of 12                                          450

                    KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                     Rev.: B

 need to be disposed of at an approved landfill off-site. Imported non-expansive soil fill
 will need to be brought into the site and compacted to replace the over-excavated
 expansive soil. The costs for the imported fill, soil transportation, and soil disposal cost
 were estimated by KBR based on previous projects in the area. The soil fill purchase
 price used in the estimate is $20.98/CY. The soil transportation and disposal costs used
 in the estimate are $42.17/CY assuming non-contaminated soil.

5. DETAILED DESIGN

Engineering & Home Office costs ($50.6 MM) for detailed design were included in the estimate
based on the typical factors employed by KBR for this type of scope accounting for the expected
scope split between the KBR USA offices, and other high value engineering centers such as the
Chennai office (India). Detailed Design cost includes Procurement. The detailed design cost was
based on factors for standard detailed design work and does not include any costs outside of the
design work. Equipment Quality (QA) assurance is excluded (explained in Section 8).

6. CONSTRUCTION MANAGEMENT

The Construction Management Team (CMT) costs are based on the project schedule, field hours,
expected staffing plan, and per-diems ($9 MM). It includes Construction Quality Assurance (QA) for
the supervision of general construction activities with a materials management representative, and
a Health, Environment, and Safety (HSE) representative. The temporary facilities, vehicles, and
indirect supplies associated with the CMT were included. Construction Management costs include
support during Start-up/Commissioning.

7. OTHER INDIRECT COSTS

The following is a list of other costs that are included in the estimate:

 • Scaffolding –Estimated by KBR based on a percentage of the field direct hours times the
 scaffolding rates for labor & materials (11% of direct construction labor costs).
 • Vendor Representatives – Costs are an allowance (1% of equipment costs) based on
 previous projects.
 • Freight – Was added when not covered by the vendor budgetary quotes. For large
 equipment like the absorber, regenerator, and direct cooler that has to be shipped on the
 largest truckable freight sizes, it was estimated at 5% of equipment costs. For other items
 freight was estimated at 3% of equipment and field material costs. There was no ocean
 freight or air freight considered.
 • Heavy Haul / Heavy Lift – Assumed heavy haul/lift applies to a few large equipment that
 could be 150 tons or more with a cost $4,000 / ton based on previous projects.

                                            Page 9 of 12                                          451

                   KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                   Rev.: B

 • Fire / Hole Watch – Assumed at 1% of direct labor costs to cover an extra construction
 person to monitor crews doing welding, trench work, etc.
 • Start-Up & Commissioning –Used an allowance (2.5% of the Total Installed Costs) based
 on previous KBR projects.

8. OWNER’S ESTIMATED COSTS

The Owner might be better able to estimate these. In most cases these costs are excluded from
the capital estimate:

 • Owner’s Staff (Excluded)
 • Equipment Quality Assurance (Excluded beyond what is already covered in the vendor
 quotes)
 • Permits (Excluded)
 • Financing and Interest Charges (Excluded)
 • Land Purchase Cost (Excluded)
 • Initial Stores Stock, Feedstock Costs, and Process Waste Costs (Excluded)
 • Catalyst and Chemicals – Excluded except for the 1st fill of activated carbon.
 • Capital Contributions to Utility Infrastructure or 3rd Party Facilities (Included) – The
 estimate includes the New NBU Utility Transformers / Substation Costs and Feeder Line
 Costs allocation to CEMEX.
 • Owner’s Contingency (Excluded) – Estimate includes only the P50 value of the CRA
 (reference section 10).
 • Road Improvements (Excluded) – If required outside of plant boundaries to facilitate
 shipping.
 • Other Spare Parts (Excluded) – For equipment and bulk materials classified for
 commissioning & start-up, warehouse, operating, etc.

9. ESCALATION

An allowance of forward escalation was applied based on the expected purchase of items like
equipment, materials, labor, etc. from the project schedule. This accounts for the expected increase
in costs from today’s date to when the expenditure is made on the project. Currently, the project
construction schedule has not been finalized. The current escalation forecasts from KBR for this
project are 2.5%/year. RTI & CEMEX has advised using a projected EPC Start Date of the 1st quarter
of 2026, the escalation applied to the project costs are shown below based on the predicted timing
of expenditures:

                                            Page 10 of 12                                      452

                      KBR Job #: N429 Doc. No: DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-ES-G-RPT-001                     Rev.: B

 • Absorber, Regenerator, Direct Contact Cooler (2.9%) – Purchased 20 months ahead (March
 2026) of the midpoint of construction (November 2027).

 • Long Lead Equipment (4.6%) – Other specialty equipment that requires more extensive
 fabrication is purchased 12 months ahead (November 2026) of the midpoint of construction
        (November 2027).

 • Other Equipment, Materials, Construction Labor, & Staffing (7.5%) – Purchased around the
 midpoint of construction (November 2027).

10. CONTINGENCY

A Cost Risk Analysis has been performed for this project through a Monte Carlo simulation with the
results of this analysis provided as one of the tabs in the estimate reports. The analysis included
conversations with the engineering disciplines and other KBR departments involved in the
development of the estimate to assign proper expected variability ranges to quantities and costs.
The P50 value of the results was included as the contingency cost in the estimate summary but the
final selection of the appropriate P value is dependent on multiple factors including the
client’s/owner’s affinity to risk. Clients all differ in their opinions on which P value they are willing
to contemplate for their capital investments, hence why the entire distribution of results was
provided. Note that at this time, a schedule risk analysis has not been performed and discrete risks
have not been identified. These risks normally generate their own components of contingency that
are not currently included in the analysis performed for this project.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This topical report presents the environmental, health and safety (EH&S) analysis for the project
under DE-FE0032220 involving the front-end engineering design (FEED) study of RTI’s Non
aqueous solvent (NAS) CO2 capture technology for Cemex’s cement manufacturing plant in
Balcones, TX. This EH&S risk analysis is conducted to assess the risks associated using the NAS
for post-combustion CO2 capture at commercial scale. The technical data for the NAS are drawn
from multiple DOE projects with the most notable ones being the Emission and Effluent Summary
report produced in this project (ref. DE-FE0032220-KBR-00-EV-C-EV2-001) and recently
completed engineering-scale testing campaign conducted at Technology Centre Mongstad (TCM)
(DE-FE0031590). The FEED study has quantitatively determined the air emissions, liquid and
solid waste streams that would be produced as a result of installing the NAS CO2 capture
technology at Balcones along with potential fugitive emissions that would be generated by the
auxiliary units such as TEG dehydration unit, natural gas boiler, etc. Under the TCM project, the
solvent was tested for > 2800 hours under a realistic flue gas and operating condition. The
secondary emissions, degradation products, and formation of the by-products, corrosivity data
from the TCM test campaign serve as basis for determination of the secondary emissions generated
from the NAS capture plant at Balcones site.
This assessment summarizes the key CO2 capture plant EH&S concepts and approach. Key
EH&S risk factors assessed and addressed as part of the overall approach are provided in
tabular form including the risk mitigation factors. The assessment describes the technology as
applied to carbon capture from flue gas in response to the six specific topics from the Append H
of FOA DE-FOA0002515.
This EH&S analysis also evaluates the potential risks along with their impacts and mitigation
actions to increase the technology viability from the safety and environmental standpoints. It is to
be noted that some of these risks are perceived risks at the current stage of development of our
technology at engineering-scale level. There could be other site-specific risks that will be identified
as we continue the effort in development to a commercial scale of the NAS technology.
Nonetheless, the conventional liquid absorption process and well-established operational
understanding of this process would make it unlikely to discover major safety roadblocks or
identify large risks that were unaccounted before.
Furthermore, this analysis details and quantifies waste streams that will be generated during the
normal operation of a cement plant with 2,400,000 TPY CO2 capture unit. Process modifications
and suggestions that could potentially reduce or recycle the waste generated from these points of
discharge will be discussed. Finally, the compliance and regulatory implications in reference to
applicable U.S. EH&S laws and associated standards of RTI’s NAS technology will be evaluated.
It is to be noted that the compliance evaluations are not fully-conducted at the current stage of the
technology development and certainly require further considerations that are more local and site-
specific that involve stakeholders and EH&S authorities. The cross-reference and comparison
between federal requirements and state regulations need to be conducted to ensure the operating
compliances for emissions, waste generation and treatment, and proper emergency responses.
Both the NAS-amine and the organic diluent used in NAS formulation are commercially available
materials, and the associated risks outlined in the document draws from the Safety Data Sheets
(SDS) of these chemicals. The SDS of the formulated NAS will be made available to the end user
as a controlled document.

 Page 7 of 19
 470

Key findings of the preliminary EH&S assessment process are:
 • No significant EH&S risks to plant operators, other employees, and surrounding area
 environment were identified that would adversely affect the implementation of NAS
 technology.
 • The NAS CO2 capture plant will increase the VOC emissions from the baseline by 6X-7X
 at the site due to the presence of amine and secondary emissions of the organic compounds
 from NAS solvent. However, the PM2.5 and SOx emissions will be eliminated compared
 to the baseline emissions. NO2 emission will also be reduced but overall NOx emission
 remains essentially the same due to the large presence of NO.
 • The toxicological issues related to the NAS amine, as per the SDS, shows that the NAS is
 low in toxicity and highly biodegradable and is, therefore, not expected to bio-accumulate.
 • The NAS flash point has been tested using the Pensky Martin Closed Cup method (ASTM
 D93) by multiple third-party labs and was determined to have no measurable flash point.
 It is stable under normal conditions without possible hazardous polymerization reaction,
 has a relatively low volatility and low corrosivity.
 • The SDS sheet lists hazards associated with the NAS and the recommended precautions:
 Avoid contact with eyes. Avoid inhalation of vapor. Wear protective clothing as necessary
 to minimize contact. The primary employee health hazard is contact exposure to NAS
 during material handling. Appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) is required to
 reduce/eliminate direct exposure. Provide local exhaust ventilation to control vapors/mists.
 Wear respiratory protection if ventilation is inadequate, such as a NIOSH-certified (or
 equivalent) organic vapor respirator.

PROCESS DESCRIPTION
The NAS CO2 capture process is designed similar to an aqueous amine absorption process. The
process is installed downstream of the flue gas desulfurization unit and upstream of the stack
(Figure 1). The process uses low-pressure steam from the power plant’s steam cycle to provide the
thermal energy needed to regenerate the NAS.

 Page 8 of 19
 471

Figure 1. Simplified Flowsheet of the NAS CO2 Capture Process for cement production studied under this
project.

RTI’s NAS process is anticipated to be inherently low in operational risks with the adoption of the
conventional absorption process that is well known and widely used in the industry. However, the
large footprint of the absorption process for such large scale treating of a cement flue gas and the
use of large quantities of organic solvent to capture CO2 require a thorough consideration of the
impact to the environment and the surrounding communities. To this end, we are evaluating the
environmental health and safety impacts on a commercial cement production plant equipped with
RTI NAS’s CO2 capture process at 2,400,000 TPY CO2 capture capacity. The six EH&S analyses
required per FOA are described below.

EH&S Analysis
1. All potential ancillary or incidental air and water emissions, and solid wastes
produced from the proposed technology and their magnitude
The FEED study has determined that there will be only one stack being added to the Balcones site.
The stack will be at the top of the acid wash section of the absorber stack where treated flue gas
stream is a major flow. This single stack also collects gas relief, emissions, and still off gas from
various units within the CO2 capture process. For example, The A-2001 stack, which is separate
from the absorber stack, receives reliefs from PSVs as well as miscellaneous other vents from the
process, including A-0221 and C-0151, but ultimately the discharge merges into the absorber
stack. The outbreathing vent from the Solvent Sump (V-1401) will be discharged to the Absorber
Stack. Other emissions are from direct vent to the atmosphere or fugitive emissions from different
units within the NAS CO2 capture plant. The reported emissions in Table 1includes baseline
emissions (i.e., no capture plant) for minor species namely PM, THC, SO2, CO, NOx, and HCl
from Balcones plant for both cases.

 Page 9 of 19
 472

Table 1: Summary of the air emissions from NAS CO2 capture plant for the design case.

 Species Net Change in Emissions (with CCS – without CCS), Design Case
 mg/Nm3
 VOC 79
 HAP 12
 CO2 net 92.7% reduction
 TEG 0.0
 CO 37
 PM2.5* reduced to nil
 NOx 21
 SO2* reduced to nil

The total liquid effluent to be discharged from NAS CO2 capture plant to wastewater treatment
facility (WWTF) at Balcones site is determined to be 88,243 lb/hr under the max condition. While
the process water bleed from Feed Direct Contact Cooler V-0111 produces the largest amount of
wastewater, that stream is processed to recover water and 185,484 lb/hr, or 95% is recycled to the
CO2 capture process. The remaining 9,762 lb/hr is sent to WWTF. This additional wastewater
generated from the capture plant will be neutralized and processed before discharge to a pond on
site.
While most information on the solid wastes generated from various equipment within the NAS
CO2 capture process are not provided by the equipment vendor, the largest solid waste to be
produced by NAS CO2 capture process is likely to be the spent adsorbent from the activated carbon
beds which translates to 106.8 lb/hr of solid waste to be disposed of from the site.
2. Description of the various toxicological effects of the substances in the NAS
The NAS amine is the main component for which toxicological effects are relevant. RTI’s NAS
consists of two components: an amine specie blended with a hydrophobic organic diluent at a
specific ratio. Though comprehensive toxicity studies of the NAS have yet to be conducted to
determine its impact on the health and environment, we reviewed relevant experimental data from
data generated in the past projects during the scale-up of the technology (DE-FE0013865, DE-
FE0026466, DE-FE0031660) and published literature of the two components to get an estimate on
health and environmental impacts. It is obvious that extensive ecological and toxicological studies
will be required to determine the exposure limits, acceptable contamination level in waterways,
and biological toxicity to understand the extent of the impact from using the NAS at a large scale
when the NAS process is adopted commercially. Overall assessment of the NAS solvent is given
in this section while the subsequent sections provide a more detailed review of the amine and
diluent.
None of the NAS chemicals are included in the General Industry Process Safety Management list
of highly hazardous chemicals, the OSHA list of carcinogens, chemical specific standards, or the
Table Z-1 Limits for Air Contaminants. The components of the NAS solvent and their hazardous
potential against other relevant chemical categories as listed in Table 2 were reviewed. It was
determined that these NAS components are not listed in any of them.

                                              Page 10 of 19
 473

applications. NAS has no measurable flash point. The NAS technology also employs an acid
wash process for emissions control, effectively minimizing potential environmental and safety
risks.
Corrosion coupon evaluations performed during the Technology Centre Mongstad (TCM)
engineering demonstration confirmed the low-corrosivity nature of the NAS solvent [1]. This
finding indicates a reduced potential for material degradation and supports consideration of lower-
grade carbon steel for certain process equipment, subject to detailed design verification.
Complementary testing at the National Carbon Capture Center’s Slipstream Testing Unit (NCCC-
SSTU) in 2018 further corroborated these results, showing metal ion concentrations substantially
lower than those observed during previous MEA baseline studies. These results suggest that the
NAS solvent poses minimal corrosion risk under expected operating conditions, contributing
positively to overall plant safety and maintainability.
Analysis of various metal concentrations, including barium (Ba), selenium (Se), arsenic (As),
cadmium (Cd), lead (Pb), and silver (Ag), in NAS solvent samples confirm compliance with U.S.
Environmental Protection Agency’s Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) limits,
highlighting safe handling and minimal environmental impact.
The favorable physical properties and low corrosivity profile of the NAS solvent substantiate its
application in industrial settings, potentially supporting the use of lower-grade materials while
ensuring adherence to regulatory requirements for environmental safety.
4. The compliance and regulatory implications of the proposed technology
This technology is not materially different from natural gas acid gas removal processes which
contact natural gas with amine solutions to remove H2S and CO2. There are hundreds if not
thousands of such installations in operation across the U.S. and Canada and they can be safely
operated in compliance with all applicable laws.
Since the process is removing components which would otherwise be emitted in the stack gas of
the power plant, the environmental implication is essentially nil. Provisions of the Clean Air Act
and NSPS requiring removal of specified amounts of SOx, NOx and particulates are not affected
since this is accomplished upstream of this system. Emissions of amine and its degradation
products are expected to be very small after implementing the ECT to minimize the environmental
impact and lower the solvent loss over time. amine emissions and other contaminants presented in
the CO2 product will need to be removed through condensation and dehydration to meet the CO2
pipeline standards. These contaminants could post a risk of bacteria growth within the CO2 pipeline
and reservoir.
The Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA),
commonly known as Superfund, applies to closed and abandoned hazardous waste sites. Individual
chemicals of the NAS composition are not included on the hazardous substance list, but certain
glycol ethers are a generic category listed with no Reportable Quantity being assigned. The
components used in NAS's diluent are noted in the EPA’s Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA)
inventory.
None of the NAS chemical components are included individually in the list of Hazardous Air
Pollutants (HAP) in the Clean Air Act (CAA), however the glycol ether category may apply.
Chemical components of the NAS do not apply to the key pollutants list EPA regulates as part of
the air toxics standards, New Source Performance Standards (NSPS) program, for fossil fuel-fired
electric utility steam generating units. None of the components apply to the list of chemicals in the
air toxics section 112(r), prevention of accidental releases (Risk Management Plan, RMP, rule).

                                            Page 13 of 19
 476

Sections 311-312 (Hazardous Chemical Inventory Reporting) of the Superfund Amendments and
Reauthorization Act (SARA) Title III apply to facilities with hazardous chemicals in quantities
above listed thresholds. Depending on quantities on site, these requirements would apply to the
NAS, including TIER chemical inventory reporting. Section 313 requires certain facilities to report
releases and waste management information for the Section 313 listed chemicals that are
manufactured, processed, or used in quantities above listed thresholds. None of the NAS
components are on the individual Section 313 chemical list, however Diethylene Glycol Dibutyl
Ether (CAS 112-73-2) is included in the category code N230 for certain glycol ethers.
5. Engineering analysis of potentially hazardous materials identified
The use of a lower heat of regeneration solvent, such as RTI’s NAS, is essential to the economic
success of this technology. It is not the only solvent that can be used for post-combustion carbon
capture. It can be substituted with other solvents such as monoethanolamine (MEA). However,
replacing it with other solvents will substantially increase the energy needed for solvent
regeneration.
Emissions of NAS amine can be further minimized in any stream which is ultimately to be emitted
to the environment by implementing the ECT such as acid wash. The use of acid wash at TCM
shows highly encouraging test results in controlling the overall amine emissions out of the CO2
capture plant to be below 1 ppm. Environmental releases due to leaks, piping failures, spills, drips
etc. are handled by appropriate containment infrastructure. All such drips will be collected and
recycled or disposed of appropriately. Vessels and storage containers will be designed to meet
applicable codes and use compatible materials so that corrosion with resultant leakage is
eliminated. Solvent offloading from drums or containers will be engineered to avoid improperly
matched connections to transfer pumps, incompatible elastomers or hoses etc. No direct access to
plant sewer from the process skid will be provided. Personnel will employ appropriate PPE to
avoid contact during routine and maintenance procedures.
6. Precautions for safe handling and conditions for safe storage
The precautions for safe handling of NAS are:
- Avoid contact with the skin, eyes and clothing. Avoid inhalation of mists/vapors.
- Use with local exhaust ventilation.
- Wear NIOSH-certified chemical goggles.
- Wear full face shield if splashing hazard exists.
- Wear chemical resistant protective gloves.
- Wear protective clothing.
- Eye wash fountains and safety showers must be easily accessible.
A solvent-side slipstream reclaim process can be used to handle potential nitrosamine formed
during the CO2 capture process. An activated carbon bed process can be used for the removal of
nitrosamine from the solvent in the pilot-scale CO2 capture process.
The solvent is not highly volatile; its releases into the air around the plant by vaporization from
stored solvent is expected to be minimal with appropriate storage practices and solvent handling.
Exposure to NAS is negligible partly due to the low volatility but also because the solvent will be
contained in a closed system. Exposure is most likely when handling solvent offloading from
shipping containers. Exposure is also possible when carrying out certain maintenance procedures
such as changing spent cartridges or filters. Job safety analysis (JSA) will be conducted on all these

                                             Page 14 of 19
 477

procedures. PPE will be worn as needed such as hazard suits, gloves, face shields, breathing
apparatus, shoes safe for chemical exposure and so on. The specifics will be evaluated as these
JSA are developed. Since the solvent is itself a hazardous material, all solid and liquid wastes will
be disposed of in approved hazardous waste disposal facilities and be transported by licensed
hazardous waste haulers. There will be no onsite waste disposal treatment.
Exposure Controls / Personal Protection
Personal Protection
Eye/Face Protection: Use chemical goggles or face shield as needed.
Skin Protection: Use protective clothing chemically resistant to this material. Selection of specific
items such as face shield, boots, apron, or full body suit will depend on the task.
Hand protection: Use chemical resistant protective gloves, nitrile rubber (Buna N), chloroprene
rubber (Neoprene), and polyvinylchloride (Pylox). NOTICE: The selection of a specific glove for
a particular application and duration of use in a workplace should also take into account all relevant
workplace factors such as, but not limited to: Other chemicals which may be handled, physical
requirements (cut/puncture protection, dexterity, thermal protection), potential body reactions to
glove materials, as well as the instructions/specifications provided by the glove suppliers.
Respiratory Protection: Wear respiratory protection if ventilation is inadequate. Wear a NIOSH-
certified (or equivalent) organic vapor respirator. For emergency or non-routine, high exposure
situations, use a NIOSH-certified full facepiece pressure demand self-contained breathing
apparatus (SCBA) or a full facepiece pressure demand supplied-air respirator (SAR) with escape
provisions.
Ingestion: Avoid ingestion. Ingestion may cause gastric disturbances. Do not consume or store
food or tobacco in the work area; wash hands and face before smoking or eating.
Engineering Controls
Ventilation: Use local exhaust ventilation, or other engineering controls to maintain airborne
levels below exposure limit requirements or guidelines. If there are no applicable exposure limit
requirements or guidelines, general ventilation should be sufficient for most operations. Local
exhaust ventilation may be necessary for some operations.
Nitrosamine Exposure
Exposure of any potential nitrosamine to pilot plant operators and employees is mainly possible
through incidental ingestion, dermal exposure, and inhalation. For purposes of this evaluation,
inhalation was selected as the most plausible and conservative route of exposure to potential
nitrosamine. Incidental ingestion of solvents and dermal exposure are considered to be negligible
due to the use of personal protective equipment (PPE).
The possible health and environmental effects of post-combustion amine-based CO2 capture plant
and its associated minor release of amine and amine degradation products have been investigated
extensively through the Norwegian CLIMIT-program, and the CO2 Technology Centre Mongstad
(TCM) prior to the full-scale capture plant operation and reported at GHGT-11.1
Air emissions monitoring of nitrosamines in ambient air has been primarily undertaken in
industrial areas around potential sources of nitrosamine precursors. The analytical methods used
to produce these results however have a low maturity and have not been adopted as national
standards and there is no evidence of successful method validation. Table 4 below summarizes the
data available at various industrial sites and at the carbon capture test site at Mongstad, Norway.
Table 4. Nitrosamine concentration in air at various industrial sites

                                             Page 15 of 19
 478

atmospheric-chamber studies to assess nitrosamine formation, partitioning, and degradation under
post-emission condition. Complementary nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) analyses will be
conducted to identify solvent-borne nitrosamine species, precursor compounds, and mechanistic
pathways responsible for their generation within the NAS matrix. In parallel, a targeted
nitrosamine-management strategy will be developed to mitigate formation and accumulation in the
working solvent, including evaluation of solvent additives, process-condition adjustments, and
purification or polishing approaches. Together, these studies will provide a robust understanding
of nitrosamine behavior and inform the design of effective control measures to ensure safe,
environmentally responsible operation of the NAS process.

                                          Page 17 of 19
 480

CONCLUSIONS
The EH&S risk analysis has been completed that includes possible risk mitigation factors.
This topical report summarizes the key plant EH&S concepts and approach. EH&S risks
commensurate with the state of development of RTI’s NAS technology and the stage of the project
are identified and the mitigating factors which will be implemented for management of the risks
are detailed. Responses to the six DOE-required question areas are separately provided.
Key findings of the preliminary EH&S analysis process are:
 • No significant EH&S risks to plant operators, other employees, and surrounding area
 environment were identified that would adversely affect the implementation of NAS
 technology for post-combustion carbon capture.
 • The NAS CO2 capture plant with the implementation of ECT will not generate
 appreciable new air emissions because all gas streams taken from the power plant and
 exhausted from the capture plant will consist of the same components with ppm levels of
 amine in the treated flue gas only.
 • The toxicological issues related to the NAS amine, as per the SDS, shows that the NAS is
 low in toxicity and highly biodegradable and is, therefore, not expected to bio-accumulate.
 The known degraded amine species derived from NAS will not be bio-accumulated due to
 their volatility and biodegradability in the environment. Many of the nitrosamines such as
 NDMA, NDEA, and NDPA are known to be quickly photo-oxidized and does not bio-
 accumulate. As such, nitrosamines derived from NAS is expected to share the similar
 reactivity towards ozone and sunlight.
 • The NAS flash point has been tested using the Pensky Martin Closed Cup method (ASTM
 D93) by multiple third-party labs and was determined to have no measurable flash point.
 It is stable under normal conditions without possible hazardous polymerization reaction,
 has a relatively low volatility and low corrosivity.
 • The SDS sheet lists hazards associated with the NAS and the recommended precautions:
 Avoid contact with eyes. Avoid inhalation of vapor. Wear protective clothing as necessary
 to minimize contact. The primary employee health hazard is contact exposure to NAS
 during material handling. Appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) is required to
 reduce/eliminate direct exposure. These include lab goggles, nitril gloves, chemical
 resistant clothing. Provide local exhaust ventilation to control vapors/mists. Wear
 respiratory protection if ventilation is inadequate, such as a NIOSH-certified (or
 equivalent) organic vapor respirator.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This life cycle analysis (LCA) is being commissioned for the United States (U.S.) Department of
Energy (DOE) National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL) to satisfy the award requirements
for Funding Opportunity Announcement (FOA) DE-FOA-0002515, titled Carbon Capture
Research and Development for Natural Gas and Industrial Point Sources, and Front-End
Engineering Design Studies for Carbon Capture Systems at Industrial Facilities and Natural Gas
Plants. The Principal Investigator (PI) for this project is Vijay Gupta at RTI International. This LCA
report has been prepared in accordance with ISO 14040/14044 requirements for public release
of comparative assessments for third parties.
The study presents an LCA of RTI International's proposed carbon capture system (CCS) at the
Balcones Cement Plant in New Braunfels, Texas, with a nominal cement production capacity of
2.7 million metric tonnes of cement and 2.2 million metric tonnes of CO2 emissions annually. RTI
International, in collaboration with CEMEX Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., is conducting a Front-End
Engineering Design (FEED) study to develop AACE (Association for the Advancement of Cost
Engineering) Class 3 cost estimates for a commercial CO2 capture system integrated with the
Balcones cement facility. The proposed product system is based on system/subsystem models
and prototypes demonstrated in a relevant environment, with the project currently at TRL 6. It
would capture and geologically sequester 95% of CO2 directly emitted from the plant as well as
additional CO2 produced by the CCS system. This LCA incorporates CO2 saline aquifer transport
and storage, combustion emissions at the facility, process operations and chemical inputs to the
facility, and construction impacts for required materials and equipment.
The goal is to compare and understand the differences between the NETL Tool for Reduction
and Assessment of Chemicals and Other Environmental Impacts (TRACI) 2.1 environmental
impacts, with a particular focus on global warming impacts, from the CEMEX Balcones cement
plant with and without RTI’s proposed CCS system for the U.S. DOE Carbon Utilization Program
and as a public record per the funding opportunity announcement requirement. The scope
includes the proposed product system of the CEMEX Balcones Cement Plant with carbon
capture and storage, with the functional unit being a metric tonne (MT) of Type IL and Type I/II
cement produced. The system boundary is cradle-to-gate, stopping at the gate of a MT of
cement produced. Data representativeness assumes the CCS facility has a lifetime of 25 years,
with global warming potential assessed on a 100-year basis. Electric power requirements will be
modeled from the Texas Reliability Entity (TRE). No credit or allocation will be applied for co-
products, and recycling and recycled content will follow the cut-off rule. See Exhibit ES-1 and
Exhibit ES-2 for a simplified system boundary with reference flows between key processes or life
cycle stages.

 1
 490

 Exhibit ES-1. Process flow diagram for the proposed product system

 Exhibit ES-2. Process flow diagram for the comparison product system

This project was modeled using OpenLCA software as per the guidelines given in the Carbon
Dioxide Utilization Life Cycle Analysis Guidance for the U.S. DOE Office of Fossil Energy and uses
the EcoInvent 3.10 Cutoff System Processes and NETL databases as LCIs. Further, this study uses
the TRACI 2.1 impact assessment method combined with the latest global warming potential
(GWP) factors included in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Fifth
Assessment Report (AR5) report.
The life cycle results for the AR5, 100-year GWP are presented in Exhibit ES-3 and Exhibit ES-4 for
both Types IL and I/II cement products.

 2
 491

  Exhibit ES-3. GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT cement
 basis for Type IL cement

  Exhibit ES-4. GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT cement
 basis for Type I/II cement

An estimated 668.13 kg CO2/MT-cement produced and 725.58 kg CO2/MT-cement produced are
emitted as direct CO2 emissions for Type IL and Type I/II cements, respectively, without the FEED
CCS system. Per CEMEX’s environmental product declaration, the overall CO2 emissions from
production of cement at its Balcones plant is 756 kg CO2eq/MT of Type IL cement and 816 kg
CO2eq/MT of Type I/II cement when including supporting processes. Employing the FEED carbon

 3
 492

capture process is estimated to save 499.98 kg CO2e/MT Type IL cement produced and 542.92
kg CO2e/MT Type I/II cement produced, limiting direct CO2 emissions to 41.00 kg CO2e/MT
cement and 44.59 kg CO2/MT cement at the cement plant for Type IL and Type I/II cements,
respectively. This represents a 66.1% (Type IL) and 66.5% (Type I/II) reduction in overall GHG
emissions for Portland cement production.
Further, this study includes a sensitivity analysis on the impact of higher and lower amounts of
natural gas combustion, electricity demand, and make-up AM20. The sensitivity analysis results
for the AR5, 100-year GWP are presented in Exhibit ES-5. The combustion of natural gas and
electricity demand were varied ±10% and the AM20 production was varied ±25%. The
sensitivity analysis shows that ±10% variation in the combustion of natural gas changed GWP by
±1.4% for Type IL cement and Type I/II cement. The sensitivity analysis also shows that ±10%
variation in electricity demand changed GWP by ±3.2% for Type IL cement and ±3.3% for Type
I/II cement. Changes in AM20 make-up is less impactful overall as ±25% variation only changed
GWP by ±0.5% for Type IL cement and Type I/II cement.
Given the large reductions of approximately two thirds in the Balcones cement plant’s carbon
footprint for both Types IL and I/II cement from the proposed CCS system, the PIs recommend
further project evaluation of the Proposed Product System.
The following proprietary information was obscured from the report:
 • Information pertaining to the identity of components found in RTI International’s non-
 aqueous solvent (NAS) CO2 capture technology.

 4
 493

1 GOAL AND SCOPE
1.1 STUDY GOAL
The purpose of this study is to evaluate the proposed CO2 capture system (CCS) at the CEMEX
Balcones Cement Plant designed by RTI International, CEMEX, SLB, and KBR.
The specific goals of this life cycle analysis (LCA) are described below:
 1. Intended application – The intended application of this LCA is to compare the life cycle
 greenhouse gas (GHG) impact of the proposed project – a commercial 1,600,000 t-
 CO2/yr (nominal capacity of 2,400,000 t-CO2/yr) scale CCS integrated with a cement
 facility, modeled the Proposed Product System, to a Comparison Product System – the
 facility without a CCS.
 2. Reasons for carrying out the study – To understand how the environmental impact
 (measured as life cycle GHG impact) of the proposed CCS life cycle compares to the life
 cycle of a system without CCS that produces the same products.
 3. Intended audience – The intended audience for the LCA described herein is the United
 States (U.S.) Department of Energy (DOE) Carbon Utilization Program.
 4. Public disclosure – The LCAs conducted as part of the U.S. DOE Funding Opportunity
 Announcement (FOA) requirement will become part of the public record for the award
 within the final scientific/technical report.

1.2 STUDY SCOPE
The study presents an LCA of RTI International's proposed carbon capture system (CCS) at the
Balcones Cement Plant in New Braunfels, Texas, with a nominal cement production capacity of
2.7 million metric tonnes of cement and 2.2 million metric tonnes of CO2 emissions annually.
The proposed CCS would capture and geologically sequester 95% of CO2 directly emitted from
the cement plant as well as additional CO2 produced by the CCS system. RTI International, in
collaboration with CEMEX Inc., SLB, and KBR Inc., is conducting a Front-End Engineering Design
(FEED) study to develop AACE (Association for the Advancement of Cost Engineering) Class 3
cost estimates for a commercial CO2 capture system integrated with the Balcones cement
facility. This LCA incorporates CO2 saline aquifer transport and storage, combustion emissions at
the facility, process operations and chemical inputs to the facility, and construction impacts for
required materials and equipment.
The goal is to compare and understand the differences between the NETL Tool for Reduction
and Assessment of Chemicals and Other Environmental Impacts (TRACI) 2.1 environmental
impacts, with a particular focus on global warming impacts, from the CEMEX Balcones cement
plant with and without RTI’s proposed CCS system for the U.S. DOE Carbon Utilization Program
and as a public record per the funding opportunity announcement requirement. The scope
includes the product system of the CEMEX Balcones Cement Plant with carbon capture and
storage.

 6
 495

 Exhibit 1-2. Process flow diagram for the comparison product system

1.2.3 Carbon Dioxide Source
The CO2 source is the flue gas stream from Kiln 1, Kiln 2, and the natural gas boiler.

1.2.4 Technology Representativeness
The proposed product system is based on system/subsystem models and prototypes
demonstrated in a relevant environment. Thus, this project is TRL 6.

The CCS technology has been tested at laboratory scale and engineering scale at RTI
International. The CCS technology has been demonstrated at the National Carbon Capture
Center (NCCC) in the United States, as well as the SINTEF Tiller Plant and the Technology Centre
Mongstad (TCM) in Norway. Work is currently underway to demonstrate the CCS technology at
a cement plant and a pulp and paper mill in the United States.

1.2.5 Geographical Representativeness
The Balcones Cement Plant is located in in New Braunfels, Texas. All life cycle stages are
modeled in the region applicable to New Braunfels, Texas. Where regional data were not
available, United States LCI data were used for modeling. Where United States LCI data were not
available, rest-of-the-world or global representation in OpenLCA was used for modeling.

1.2.6 Temporal Representativeness
The expected production start year is 2026. The CO2 captured in this study was assumed
geologically sequestered.
The study considers a plant lifespan of 25 years. Our LCA modeled the current U.S. grid mix for
the TRE for the year 2021 for high voltage from the EcoInvent v3.10 database.

1.2.7 Life Cycle Impact Assessment Methods for Results Interpretation
This study uses the Tool for Reduction and Assessment of Chemicals and Other Environmental
Impacts (TRACI) 2.1 method combined with the latest global warming potential (GWP) factors
included in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Fifth Assessment Report
(AR5) report. The following impact categories have been modeled and are included in this
report:

 8
 497

 1. GWP (kg CO2e), based on IPCC AR5, 100-year time horizon; accounting for carbon
 climate feedback; abbreviation: GWP-100
 2. Non-GHG impact assessment method
 a. acidification potential (kg SO2e); abbreviation: AP
 b. particulate matter formation potential (kg PM2.5e); abbreviation: PMFP
 c. photochemical smog formation potential (kgO3e); abbreviation: PSFP
 d. eutrophication potential (kg Ne); abbreviation: EP
 e. ozone depletion potential (kg CFC-11e); abbreviation: ODP
 f. optional: water consumption (l); abbreviation: WC

1.2.8 Completeness Requirements
This study uses a heat and materials balance as a proxy for life cycle completeness, as required
in Carbon Dioxide Utilization Life Cycle Analysis Guidance for the U.S. DOE Office of Fossil
Energy. [1]

The carbon balance of the Balcones Cement Plant has been shared with the U.S. DOE Office of
Fossil Energy.

1.2.9 Sensitivity and Uncertainty Analysis
The sensitivity analysis results for the AR5, 100-year GWP are presented in Exhibit 1-3. The
combustion of natural gas and electricity demand were varied ±10% and the make-up AM20
was varied ±25%. The sensitivity analysis shows that ±10% variation in the combustion of
natural gas changed GWP by ±1.4% for Type IL cement and Type I/II cement. The sensitivity
analysis also shows that ±10% variation in electricity demand changed GWP by ±3.2% for Type
IL cement and ±3.3% for Type I/II cement. Changes in AM20 make-up is less impactful overall as
±25% variation only changed GWP by ±0.5% for Type IL cement and Type I/II cement.

information on unit process data generator and data documenter are provided in the metadata
of each unit process in the openLCA database.

4 LIFE CYCLE INTERPRETATION
The life cycle results for the AR5, 100-year GWP are presented in Exhibit 4-1 and Exhibit 4-2 as
waterfall charts. These results show the proposed product system (i.e., with CCS) with the
comparison product system (i.e., without CCS) for each type of cement. The comparison
product system is shown in the first column and is divided into direct and indirect CO2
emissions. The direct CO2 emissions are shown in orange and the indirect CO2 emissions are
shown in blue. CO2 emissions that were added with CCS to the proposed product system are
shown in gray and CO2 emissions that are captured with CCS are shown in green. The proposed
product system is shown in the last column and is divided into direct and indirect CO2
emissions. The direct CO2 emissions are shown in orange and the indirect CO2 emissions are
shown in light blue. The proposed product system shows a 66.1% decrease in GHG emissions
for the Type IL cement and a 66.5% decrease in GHG emissions for the Type I/II cement. Similar
results for the other impact categories have been shared with the U.S. DOE Office of Fossil
Energy and are available in Appendix A: Impact Results for All Impact Categories.
Exhibit 4-1. GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT cement basis
 for Type IL cement

 512

Exhibit 4-2. GWP-100-year life cycle interpretation results for proposed product system on a per MT cement basis
 for Type I/II cement

An estimated 668.13 kg CO2/MT-cement produced and 725.58 kg CO2/MT-cement produced are
emitted as direct CO2 emissions for Type IL and Type I/II cements, respectively, without the FEED
CCS system. Per CEMEX’s environmental product declaration, the overall CO2 emissions from
production of cement at its Balcones plant is 756 kg CO2eq/MT of Type IL cement and 816 kg
CO2eq/MT of Type I/II cement when including supporting processes. Employing the FEED carbon
capture process is estimated to save 499.98 kg CO2e/MT Type IL cement produced and 542.92
kg CO2e/MT Type I/II cement produced, limiting direct CO2 emissions to 41.00 kg CO2e/MT
cement and 44.59 kg CO2/MT cement at the cement plant for Type IL and Type I/II cements,
respectively. This represents a 66.1% (Type IL) and 66.5% (Type I/II) reduction in overall GHG
emissions for Portland cement production.
Given the large reductions of approximately two thirds in the Balcones cement plant’s carbon
footprint for both Types IL and I/II cement from the proposed CCS system, the PIs recommend
further project evaluation of the Proposed Product System.

was varied ±25%. The sensitivity analysis shows that ±10% variation in the combustion of

analysis also shows that ±10% variation in electricity demand changed GWP by ±3.2% for Type
IL cement and ±3.3% for Type I/II cement. Changes in AM20 make-up is less impactful overall as
±25% variation only changed GWP by ±0.5% for Type IL cement and Type I/II cement.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
RTI, in collaboration with Cemex , SLB and KBR Inc, is conducting a FEED (front-end
engineering design) study to develop an AACE (Association for the Advancement of Cost
Engineering) Class 3 cost estimate for CO2 capture using RTI’s Non-Aqueous Solvent (NAS)
technology for retrofit application at the Balcones cement plant in New Braunfels, TX. The project
aims to capture 95% of the total emissions from the two kilns and the natural gas boiler used to
produce steam for regenerating the solvent used in the capture process. The captured CO2 has a
purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, compressed to 15.3 MPa (2,215 psia), ready for sequestration.
The CO2 capture plant nominal capacity is 2.4 MTPA.
Due to limited water availability at the Balcones plant, some design decisions were made which
increased the CAPEX and OPEX contributions of the plant. Hybrid cooling was utilized, where
84% of the total cooling load is met using air cooled heat exchangers, and the remaining 16% of
cooling load met using water cooled systems. The water cooling is reserved for the water-wash
sections for water-balanced operations and for controlling emissions. A heat-integrated CO2
compressor system with boiler feed water to raise steam in lieu of interstage coolers was used to
limit water usage. The larger area requirements due to the use of air coolers for the CO2 capture
plant resulted in re-locating the capture plant site away from the CO2 capture plant, across the
railway line and the road.
The CO₂ capture plant design proposes the use of an acid wash system for emissions control. In its
current configuration, the acid wash removes any residual amine from the treated gas by reacting
the amine with the acid to form an amine-sulfate salt. This salt is typically treated as waste, and
the lost amine must be replenished through the addition of makeup amine. However, this approach
introduces operational inefficiencies in terms of chemical costs and waste handling.
Experimental work conducted by RTI has demonstrated that this amine can be successfully
separated from the acid wash waste stream with a recovery rate of at least 95%. The process entails
neutralizing the amine-sulfate salt to regenerate the parent amine, which then separates out as an
organic phase due to its hydrophobic properties. The recovered organic amine can be recycled into
the NAS solvent loop, creating a closed-loop process. By reducing the loss of amine and limiting
the need for fresh chemical makeup, this recovery process offers significant cost savings.
Experimental testing has demonstrated the technical feasibility and maturity of the amine recovery
from acid wash waste stream technology, thereby eliminating significant implementation risks.
The technology is planned for inclusion as part of the standard design package for subsequent
projects. However, it was not incorporated into the current FEED design, as its development
progressed in parallel and reached completion after the FEED design freeze. The cost and
performance impacts associated with its implementation have been evaluated and incorporated
into the Techno-Economic Analysis (TEA) to inform future design and deployment decisions.
A detailed TEA has been carried out for CO2 capture at Cemex’s Balcones plant with using RTI-
NAS technology. The TEA uses data from the AACE Class 3 level FEED study using DOE’s
approach for estimation of levelized cost of capture for retrofit applications for cement plants.
Three different cases are evaluated. Case A is the base case with the capture plant as currently
designed with hybrid cooling and heat-integrated CO2 compressors. Case B is a hypothetical case
where the constraint for the limited water supply was removed and the plant and CO2 compressors
are water cooled and the plant is sited in proximity to the cement plant. Case C is a comparative

 7 of 44
 527

case that evaluates the impact of co-generation of steam and electricity. A comparative “Cemex”
case is also presented which uses the Cemex financial parameters (except for capital charge factor),
Cemex’s estimate for the owner’s cost, labor estimates based on local wages, and utility prices.
The cost of capture, excluding T&S, using Cemex parameters was estimated to be $101.6/t-CO2.
Using DOE parameters and assumptions results in the cost of capture of $112.9/t-CO2. Relaxing
the constraint of limited water availability reduces the cost of capture to $98.3/t-CO2. Also, using
a cogeneration plant for steam and electricity reduces the cost of capture for the base case from
$112.9/t-CO2 to $103.9/t-CO2. Results from the TEA are summarized in Table 1-1.
The estimated cost of CO₂ capture for the cement plant using RTI’s NAS technology is $112.9/t-
CO2, For the techno-economic assessment (TEA) based on the Cemex parameters and assumptions
(the “Cemex case”), the capture cost—excluding transportation and storage (T&S)—is $101.6/t-
CO₂. When amine recovery from the acid waste stream is incorporated, operating expenses
(OPEX) are reduced, resulting in a lower overall cost of capture. Under this configuration, the
capture cost—excluding T&S—decreases to $104.2/t-CO₂ for the Base case using DOE financial
parameters, and to $93.0/t-CO₂ when applying the Cemex financial parameters.
A sensitivity analysis was performed and the sensitive parameters identified included the capacity
factor and the CAPEX. where a 15% change indicated the highest impact from the capacity factor.
The unit price of natural gas and electricity had the lowest impact, with the cost of capture
increasing by 4% with a 30% increase in the utility prices. The solvent price also had a low impact,
with the cost of capture rising by 5% with a 30% increase in the unit price of NAS.

 8 of 44
 528

 Introduction

RTI, in collaboration with Cemex, SLB and KBR Inc, is conducting a FEED (front-end
engineering design) study to develop an AACE (Association for the Advancement of Cost
Engineering) Class 3 cost estimate for CO2 capture using RTI’s Non-Aqueous Solvent (NAS)
technology for retrofit application at the Balcones cement plant in New Braunfels, TX. The project
aims to capture 95% of the total emissions from the two kilns and the natural gas boiler used to
produce steam for regenerating the solvent used in the capture process. The captured CO2 has a
purity of > 95% CO2, and is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara), ready for sequestration.
The Balcones plant has 2 kilns. Kiln #1 has 4 preheater stages with a design capacity of 3,125
tons clinker/day and kiln #2 has 5 preheater stages with an inline calciner with a design capacity
of 3,600 tons clinker/day. The plant uses selective non-catalytic reduction to reduce NOx, a dry
sorbent injection to reduce SOx and fabric filter baghouse for control of particulate matter. Total
CO2 emissions at nominal capacity is 2.2 MTPA.
The technoeconomic analysis uses data from the FEED study using DOE’s approach for estimation
of levelized cost of capture for retrofit applications for cement plants [1].

 10 of 44
 530

 Evaluation Basis

The study evaluates the use of RTI’s Non-Aqueous Solvent technology for 95% CO2 capture from
a cement plant downstream of the baghouse for particulate control. The captured CO2 has a purity
of > 95% CO2, and is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara), ready for sequestration.
2.1 DESIGN BASIS
The plant is located in New Braunfels, TX, with ambient conditions and site characteristics as
shown in Table 2-1.

 Table 2-1: Ambient conditions and site characteristics for the reference plant
 Parameter Description
 Location New Braunfels, TX.
 Elevation (ft) 645 ft (196.6 meters)
 Barometric Pressure, psia (bar) 14.696 (1.01325 bar)
 Design Ambient Temperature, Dry bulb, °F (°C) 80 (26.7)
 Design Ambient Temperature, Wet bulb, °F (°C) 77 (25.0)
 Topography Level
 Transportation Rail
 Water on-site well water available at average
 rate of 191 GPM
 Access Land locked, having access by rail and
 highway
 CO2 storage Compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara) ,
 transported 20 miles (32 kilometers) and
 sequestered in a saline formation.

2.2 CAPITAL COST ESTIMATION METHODOLOGY
The capital cost estimate was developed by KBR during Front End Loading (FEL-3) engineering
and targeted as a Class 3 estimate based on the AACE International practice from the published
Cost Estimate Classification System (18R-97, page 3, Rev. 8/7/20). KBR Estimating used the
Aspen Economic Evaluation tools to organize, extend, compile, and sort the estimate data
according to the agreed cost breakdowns. Specifically, Aspen Capital Cost Estimator (ACCE)
V14.2 was used for this project.

The main tool utilized to compile the estimate was the Aspen Economic Evaluation Capital Cost
Estimator, V14.2 version (ACCE). The tool relies on quantities and sizing information to generate
costs and field labor hours from its own database of costs. The estimate is made up of a
combination of sources of cost. The estimate was generated with the ACCE tool using regional
cost conditions, KBR historical costs information, and vendor budgetary quotes.
The cost estimate uses a code of accounts into major systems as outlined below:
  • Unit 01 – CO2 Capture System

 11 of 44
 531

  • Unit 02 – CO2 Compression, Dehydration, and Polishing
  • Unit 11 – Raw, Potable, and Demineralized Water System
  • Unit 12 – Boiler & Steam System
  • Unit 14 – Solvent & Chemical Storage Utilities
  • Unit 15 – Cooling Water System
  • Unit 16 – Air / Nitrogen System
  • Unit 18 – Wastewater Treatment Plant
  • Unit 20 – Stack
  • Unit 22 – Utility Headers
  • Unit 23 – Power Generation

2.2.1 Cost Basis
The estimate was generated in U.S. dollars ($) with a 4th Quarter 2024 cost basis. The ACCE base
escalation indices were adjusted based on current market conditions to bring the system generated
costs to “today’s” cost. Equipment quotes or costs provided by vendors, licensors, and contractors
were brought up to the same overall cost basis. Forward-looking escalation was included in the
estimate.

2.2.2 Capital Cost Estimates
Relevant information like the technical datasheets, requisition package, specifications, etc., was
used to obtain vendor budgetary quotes. The budgetary quotes were checked against the associated
design specifications to see what is included and excluded. Any items excluded from the quote
were estimated with allowances. Equipment quotes were obtained on all critical mechanical
equipment, which make up 94% of the total mechanical equipment costs in the estimate. In
addition, quotes were obtained on most electrical equipment and instruments. In general, the
lowest technically viable quotes were considered for the estimate, but averages were used in a few
cases due to uncertainty in the accuracy of the quote and items included by the vendor. Contractor
quotes were also included for Power Overhead Feeder, Gatehouse, Warehouse, Engineering &
Control Room Building, Control Room & Change Room Building, Maintenance Building, Lab,
and Compressor Sheds. The combined value of the vendor budgetary quotes and contractor
estimates make up 66% of the total project equipment & material costs. The remaining bulk
materials and equipment were estimated by ACCE indexed based on experience.
The capital costs are broken down to the five levels that are typically used by DOE. These include
the BEC (bare erected cost), EPCC (engineering, procurement and construction cost), TPC (total
plant cost), TOC (total owner’s cost) and TASC (total as spent capital).

2.2.3 Home Office, Engineering Fees and Project/Process Contingencies
Engineering & Home Office costs for detailed design were included in the estimate based on the
typical factors for this type of scope. The Construction Management Team (CMT) costs are based
on the project schedule, field hours, expected staffing plan, and per-diems. It includes Construction
Quality Assurance (QA) for the supervision of general construction activities with a materials
management representative, and a Health, Environment, and Safety (HSE) representative. The

 12 of 44
 532

temporary facilities, vehicles, and indirect supplies associated with the CMT were included.
Construction Management costs include support during Start-up/Commissioning.
Process contingency is intended to compensate for uncertainty in cost estimates caused by
performance uncertainties associated with the development status of a technology. A 17% process
contingency was applied to the RTI CO2 capture process.
A Cost Risk Analysis was performed by KBR for the project through a Monte Carlo simulation
The analysis included conversations with the engineering disciplines and other KBR departments
involved in the development of the estimate to assign proper expected variability ranges to
quantities and costs. After discussions with Cemex, the P70 value of the results, corresponding to
6.9%, was included as the project contingency cost in the estimate summary.
2.2.4 Owner’s Cost
Owner’s cost was then added to TPC to come up with the total overnight cost (TOC) for the system.
Owner’s costs as defined in the DOE Baseline Study include the following:
 • preproduction costs –
 o 6 months of all labor cost
 o 1 month of maintenance materials
 o 1 month of non-fuel consumables
 o 1 month of waste disposal
 o Start-up and commissioning costs (2% TPC)
 • inventory capital
 o 60-day supply of fuel and consumable at 100% capacity factor (CF)
 o 0.5% TPC (spare parts)
 • initial cost for catalyst and chemicals per design
 • land cost was not considered as the cement plant already has the land available for the
 capture plant.
 • other owner's costs, estimated at 15% of TPC as per DOE baseline study [1]. For the
 Cemex case, the Owner’s cost was estimated to be $24MM and includes the electrical
 substation, air emissions permitting, construction and land use permits, topography,
 soil and water studies, legal fees and technology, environmental and financial
 consultants for the project.
 • financing costs at 2.7% TPC, as per DOE baseline study [1].

2.3 OPERATION & MAINTENANCE COSTS
The operation and maintenance (O&M) costs pertain to those charges associated with operating
and maintaining CO2 capture plants over their expected life. These costs include:
 • operating labor
 • maintenance – material and labor
 • administrative and support labor
 • consumables
 • fuel
 • waste disposal

 13 of 44
 533

There are two components of O&M costs; fixed O&M, which is independent of power generation,
and variable O&M, which is proportional to power generation. Variable O&M costs were
estimated based on 74% CF, which is consistent with a historical value of 1.8 MTPA CO2
emissions from the cement kilns.

2.3.1 Fixed Costs
Operating labor cost was determined based on the number of operators required to work in the
plant. Other assumptions used in calculating the total fixed cost include:
      •   2022 base hourly labor rate, $/hr.                               $40.72

 • administrative/support labor, % O&M labor 25
 • annual maintenance labor, % TPC 0.63
 • annual maintenance material, % TPC 0.96
 • property taxes and insurances, % TPC 2

2.3.2 Variable Costs
The cost of consumables, including fuel, was determined based on the individual rates of
consumption, the unit cost of each specific consumable commodity, and the plant annual operating
hours. Waste quantities and disposal costs were evaluated similarly to the consumables.
The unit costs for major consumables and waste disposal was selected from the DOE/NETL -
2023/3856 Baseline Report, Analysis of Carbon Capture Retrofits for Cement Plants [1] or vendor
quotes. Natural gas fuel is purchased at $4.61/MMBtu, and power is purchased at a price of
$67.3/MWh, and are consistent with the values used in the DOE/NETL 2023/3856 Baseline
Report.
