 535

 RTI’s Non-Aqueous Solvent (RTI-NAS) CO2 Capture Technology

3.1 PROCESS OVERVIEW
RTI’s CO2 capture process uses RTI-NAS, a formulation of hydrophobic, sterically hindered,
carbamate-forming amines in an organic diluent with low (<10 wt%) water content to capture
>95% of CO2 from a cement plant and the associated captive natural gas boiler for providing steam
for solvent regeneration. The solvent is continuously recirculated between an absorber and a
regenerator column. Flue gas from a cement plant and the captive boiler, after scrubbing SO2 to
< 1 ppm and cooling the flue gas, enters the absorber column where 95% of the CO2 is captured.
CO2 absorption occurs at low temperatures in the absorber column. The amine in the RTI-NAS
solvent reacts with CO2 via carbamate chemical reaction pathway. The CO2-rich solvent is
regenerated in the regenerator using LP steam from the natural gas boiler. During the solvent
regeneration process, the solvent is heated, and the reverse reactions predominate to release CO2.
The CO2 released in this process at 65.3 psia (4.5 bara), is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7
bara) and sent for sequestration.
RTI’S NAS technology has been extensively tested at the bench and pilot scale and has the
potential to reduce the thermal regeneration energy demand to ~2.0 GJ/t-CO2 captured [3]. This
low energy demand is due to the solvent formulation and the process configuration of the CO2
capture plant. Key attributes of the RTI-NAS solvent are:
 • Low energy required for solvent regeneration resulting in low steam consumption
 • High working capacity resulting in low liquid/gas ratio
 • High solvent thermal and oxidative stability resulting in longer service life
 • Low corrosivity

3.2 PROCESS DESCRIPTION
The CO2 capture plant is designed to remove at least 95% of CO2 from the flue-gas from the
cement plant, downstream of the baghouse filter unit. The captured CO2 has a purity of > 95%
CO2, and is dried, compressed to 2215 psia (152.7 bara), ready for sequestration. A schematic of
the process flow diagram is provided in

Figure 3-1. A single train is needed to treat the flue gas from the cement plant. The process
equipment comprises a direct contact cooler with integrated SO2 polishing, a flue gas blower, the
absorption column with the intercoolers, an advanced emissions control technology and acid wash
to control amine emissions, the regeneration column, associated pumps, storage and receiver
vessels, and a CO2 drying and compression train.

 16 of 44
 536

 Figure 3-1: PFD of the RTI CO2 Capture Plant

3.2.1 Integrated Direct Contact Cooler and Caustic Scrubber
The combined flue gas from Kiln 1, Kiln 2, and the natural gas-fired boiler is routed to the Feed
Direct Contact Cooler, where it is cooled and conditioned prior to CO₂ absorption. In this unit, the
gas stream is contacted with a caustic solution, which facilitates the removal of sulfur dioxide
(SO₂) to a concentration of less than 1 ppmv. The water in the recirculation loop passes through
the DCC Circulation Filter, the Feed Scrubber Wash Air Cooler, and the Feed Scrubber Wash
Cooler before returning to the top of the DCC. During the cooling process, excess water vapor
condenses from the flue gas. This condensate is collected and sent to the Wastewater Treatment
Plant (WWTP), where it is treated and reused elsewhere in the process.
3.2.2 Flue Gas Blower
After cooling and conditioning the flue gas, its pressure is boosted in a blower to overcome the
pressure drop encountered in the DCC and the absorber column. The caustic scrubber also reduces
the acid gas components in the flue gas, thus reducing the need for use of special alloys in the
blower.
3.2.3 CO2 Absorber and Regenerator
The Absorber Column is a vertically packed, integrated unit designed for CO₂ removal from flue
gas. It consists of three main sections: the CO₂ absorption section, followed by a water wash
section and an acid wash section for downstream treatment. Cooled flue gas from the Flue Gas
Blowers enters the bottom of the absorber and flows upward in a counter-current direction to the
lean amine solvent, which is introduced at the top of the CO₂ absorption section. As the gas rises
through the packed Absorption beds, CO₂ is absorbed into the solvent. Interstage cooling is applied
between the beds to remove the heat of absorption and maintain optimal solvent loading efficiency.

 17 of 44
 537

The partially loaded solvent is collected in the Absorber Interstage Surge Tanks, from which it is
circulated by the Absorber Interstage Circulation Pumps through the Absorber Interstage Air
Coolers. These air coolers reduce the solvent temperature before it is reintroduced into the column
above the next packed stage. At the bottom of the column, the CO₂-rich solvent is collected and
discharged by the Rich Solvent Pumps. The stream is then split: the majority is routed through the
Lean/Rich Solvent Heat Exchanger for energy recovery, while a small bypass stream is sent
directly to the top of the Regenerator Column as cold rich solvent. The treated flue gas, now with
significantly reduced CO₂ content, continues upward into the water wash and acid wash sections
for final cleanup before exiting the absorber.
CO₂-lean solvent from the Regenerator Column is routed through the Lean/Rich Solvent Heat
Exchanger, where it transfers heat to the incoming CO₂-rich solvent from the Absorber, delivered
by the Rich Solvent Pumps. This integration reduces reboiler duty and improves energy efficiency.
After heat exchange, the lean solvent flows to the Lean Solvent Surge Tank, and from there, it is
cooled in the Lean Solvent Air Cooler before being sent to the top of the Absorber Column. During
normal operation, approximately 10% of the lean solvent flow is diverted through the Particulate
Filter and Carbon Filter to remove solids and degradation products, maintaining solvent quality.
The filtered stream is then recombined with the main flow upstream of the absorber.
The Regenerator Reboiler uses a flow of LP steam to provide energy for regeneration. Vapors from
the reboilers return to the column, and CO₂ gas from the column top is cooled in the Regenerator
Overhead Condenser before entering the Condensate Drum. Captured CO₂ is sent to the CO₂
Compressor Package, while reflux is recycled to the column. In the event of downstream upsets,
vented gas is routed to the stack.
The lean solvent, after exchanging heat with the incoming rich solvent is further cooled in a trim
cooler and fed back to the top of the absorber. A makeup amine stream is also added to the lean
solvent, to make up for any amines lost in the absorber or the regenerator.
3.2.4 CO2 Product Gas Dehydration and Compression
The CO₂ Compressor Package compresses CO₂ from the Regenerator Column and integrates
polishing and TEG dehydration to meet pipeline specifications. The system consists of a five-stage
integrally geared compressor with heat exchangers and suction/knock-out drums. Heat from the
intercoolers is recovered using boiler feedwater (BFW), generating LP steam for the utility header.
Intermediate process steps include O₂ Polishing and TEG Dehydration between stages 3 and 4 of
compression. The O₂ Polishing Reactor reduces O₂ content in the CO₂ stream to <10 ppmv using
a catalyst that reacts with hydrogen. Hydrogen is supplied via electrolysis in the vendor-provided
Electrolyzer Package, which uses demineralized water sourced from OSBL.
The TEG Dehydration Package removes moisture from the CO₂ stream, with TEG. The polished
and dehydrated CO₂ is returned to stage 4 of the compressor for final cooling and compression
before being delivered to the pipeline.

 541

5.2 CASE A: BASE CASE
The Cemex Balcones Cement Plant is in a hot humid subtropical region and has limited water
availability from the on-site Cemex wells. The CO2 capture process requires cooling for the direct
contact cooler used to reduce the temperature of the flue gas, absorber intercoolers, trim cooler,
water wash and CO2 product stream condenser. In addition, cooling is also needed to cool the
CO2-product between stages during compression. To address this challenge, it was decided to treat
and re-use the water condensed in the direct contact cooler, and to use hybrid cooling where 84%
of the total cooling load is met using air cooled heat exchangers, and the remaining 16% of cooling
load met using water cooled systems. 80 ℉ was selected as the design temperature for the air
coolers, as a compromise between the CAPEX for the air coolers and capture rate as it allows the
plant to reach 95% capture rate averaged over summer and winter months. The flue gas stream is
cooled using air coolers and then further cooled using water cooling to the designed dewpoint
temperature. The absorber intercoolers, the intercoolers and the CO2-product condensers all use
only air cooling, and the water cooling is reserved for the water-wash sections where it is critical
to maintain final wash temperature to be close to dewpoint temperature of the inlet flue gas for
water-balanced operations and for controlling emissions.
Lack of cooling water resulted in the need for a steam integrated CO2 compressor system where
the interstage coolers uses the boiler feed water to raise steam. The larger area requirements for
the CO2 capture plant with air coolers also resulted in re-locating the capture plant site away from
the CO2 capture plant, across the railway line and the road.

5.2.3 Water Balance
Water demand represents the total water required by a process. Some water is recovered in a
process and is re-used as internal recycle. The difference between water demand and internal
recycle is raw water withdrawal. Raw water that is either incorporated into a product, or is lost
due to evaporation, drift losses etc., and is thus not returned to the source from where it was
withdrawn, is termed as process discharge. The difference of raw water withdrawal and process
discharge is the raw water consumption.
The water balance for the cement plant with CO2 capture is shown in Table 5-4. Water that is
recovered from processes such as direct contact cooler is treated and recycled to the cooling tower.
It is assumed that 95% of the DCC condensate is recycled.

 28 of 44

Total electrical power consumption for the RTI CO2 capture plant is 61.3 MW. The CO2
compressor consumes 27 MW of power. A major driver for this is the limited availability of the
cooling water for the interstage cooling in the CO2 compression train. To overcome this issue, a
steam integrated CO2 compressor is used where the boiler feed water (BFW) is used to raise low
pressure steam in lieu of interstage cooling. This results in higher interstage compressors, leading
to higher CO2 compression power.
Limited availability of cooling water also results in the use of hybrid coolers, with air cooler fans
accounting for 10 MW of electricity consumption.
The air coolers occupy 4 acres of the carbon capture plant location, and result in ~40% additional
NAS solvent as initial fill. The total NAS makeup accounts for the NAS losses due to emissions,
oxidative and thermal degradation as well as losses due to heat stable salts (HSS) and reaction with
NOx. The solvent losses were estimated using data from recent NAS demonstration at TCM.
The CO₂ capture plant design proposes the use of an acid wash system for emissions control. The
current design includes an acid wash system to control emissions requiring ongoing amine makeup
and RTI International has developed an amine recovery process that is planned for inclusion in
standard design packages for future projects. It was not incorporated into the current FEED design,
as its development progressed in parallel with the FEED. The cost and performance impacts
associated with its implementation have been evaluated and incorporated into the Techno-
Economic Analysis (TEA) to inform future design and deployment decisions. When the amine
recovery is implemented, the levelized cost of capture is projected to decrease by at least ~9%.

5.2.8 Cost of CO2 Capture
The estimated cost of CO₂ capture for the cement plant using RTI’s NAS technology is $112.9/t-
CO2, For the techno-economic assessment (TEA) based on the Cemex parameters and assumptions
(the “Cemex case”), the capture cost—excluding transportation and storage (T&S)—is $101.6/t-
CO₂. The total cost of capture for the Cemex case, including T&S, is $135.5/t-CO₂. For the Base
case, the T&S cost is assumed to be $10/t-CO₂, consistent with the DOE Baseline Study for retrofit
CO₂ capture at cement plants [1].
When amine recovery from the acid waste stream is incorporated, operating expenses (OPEX) are
reduced, resulting in a lower overall cost of capture. Under this configuration, the capture cost—
excluding T&S—decreases to $104.2/t-CO₂ for the Base case using DOE financial parameters,
and to $93.0/t-CO₂ when applying the Cemex financial parameters.
5.2.9 Cost of CO2 Avoided
Cost of CO2 avoided for the cement plant with CO2 capture using RTI-NAS technology is $141.6/t-
CO2. When amine recovery from the acid waste stream is incorporated, the cost of CO2 avoided
drops to $130.7/t-CO2.

5.3 COMPARATIVE TEA CASES
5.3.1 Case B: Use of water for cooling with cooling tower
Limited availability of cooling water at the Balcones plant necessitated the use of hybrid cooling,
coupled with a heat-integrated CO₂ compressor, as implemented in Case A (base case). The large
land area required for air coolers also meant that the capture plant had to be sited away from the
cement plant, requiring a longer flue gas duct to convey flue gas to the unit. The alternative
scenario, Case B, assumes water-cooled heat exchangers with a larger cooling tower and siting the

 551
 31 of 44

capture plant closer to the cement plant. Both cases are assumed to achieve the same solvent
temperatures and CO₂ capture performance, and share the same heat and materials balance (HMB).
The differences are limited to equipment type, plant siting, and associated water requirements. A
comparative analysis was conducted to evaluate the impact on cost of capture if cooling water
were available. The process changes and their TEA impacts are summarized in Table 5-10.

  Table 5-10 Process changes resulting from switch from hybrid cooling to water cooling and its
 impacts on TEA.
ID Process Change Impact on CO2 capture plant TEA
B.1 Replacement of air ▪ Reduced CAPEX from lower cost exchangers
 coolers with water cooled ▪ Reduced power consumption from absence of air cooler fans
 heat exchangers ▪ Added CAPEX from larger cooling tower
 ▪ Added power consumption from cooling tower fans
 ▪ Increased water demand from cooling tower losses
 ▪ Solvent initial fills reduced by 40% leading to lower initial fill costs.
B.2 Replacement of ▪ CAPEX is roughly the same for the two cases
 integrated CO2 ▪ Reduced power consumption due to lower interstage temperatures
 compressor with water- resulting from water-cooled exchangers
 cooled interstage coolers ▪ Added CAPEX from larger cooling tower
 ▪ Added power consumption from cooling tower fans
 ▪ Increased water demand from cooling tower losses
B.3 Siting of CO2 capture ▪ Reduced land area requirements(~ 4 acres) leading to shorter flue
 plant near the cement gas ducts and associated CAPEX.
 plant

Results from the TEA for the three scenarios implemented cumulatively are provided in Table
5-11. The switch from hybrid cooling (Case A) to a fully water-cooled design (Case B) reduces
the levelized cost of CO₂ capture by approximately 9%, decreasing it from $112.9/t-CO₂ to
$101.6/t-CO₂. When combined with siting the CO₂ capture plant closer to the cement facility and
implementing water-cooled CO₂ compressors, the cost of capture decreases further to $98.3/t-CO₂,
representing a 13% reduction relative to the base case. These savings are primarily driven by lower
CAPEX for exchangers, reduced fan power consumption, and shorter flue gas ducting, while
additional CAPEX and power for the larger cooling tower partially offset these benefits.
Note: The cost of capture comparison is conservative, as it does not account for potential
reductions in civil works and structural steel associated with the smaller plot area required for
water cooling. Including these savings would likely further reduce the total cost of capture for Case
B.

 552
 32 of 44

5.3.2 Case C: Co-generation of heat and electricity
The CO₂ capture plant currently uses a natural gas boiler to generate low-pressure steam (LPS) for
solvent regeneration. As part of the sensitivity analysis, a co-generation option was evaluated, in
which the existing boiler would be replaced with a larger unit to produce high-pressure (HP, 620
psig) steam. The HP steam would expand through a turbine to generate electricity, with the
resulting LPS used in the CO₂ capture process. Capital cost estimates for the co-generation option
were developed based on vendor quotes for the larger boiler and the turbine, as well as estimates
of the additional natural gas required and the net power generated. Any remaining electricity
demand above that generated on-site would be supplied from the grid. A summary of the cost
estimates for the co-generation option is presented in Table 5-12.

 34 of 44

With the cogeneration option, the total CO2 captured increases due to increase in the natural gas
consumption, resulting in a higher capacity factor. The CAPEX does not change as the increase in
CO2 flowrate is within the 10% margin. The increase in OPEX is accounted for by increasing the
capacity factor from 74% to 80%. The cogeneration for steam and electricity reduces the cost of
capture from $112.9/t-CO2 to $103.9/t-CO2. Including the amine recovery from the acid wash
waste stream, the costs for the Base case and the co-generation case are $104.2/t-CO2 and $95.3/t-
CO2 respectively,

 556
 36 of 44

 Conclusions

The technoeconomic analysis uses data from the FEED study using DOE’s approach for estimation
of levelized cost of capture for retrofit applications for cement plants [1].
The Cemex Balcones Cement Plant is in a hot humid subtropical region and has limited water
availability from the on-site Cemex on-site wells. The CO2 capture process requires cooling for
the direct contact cooler, absorber intercoolers, trim cooler, water wash and CO2 product stream
condenser. In addition, cooling is also needed to cool the CO2-product between stages during
compression. To address this challenge of limited water, it was decided to treat and re-use the
water condensed in the direct contact cooler, and to use hybrid cooling where 84% of the total
cooling load is met using air cooled heat exchangers, and the remaining 16% of cooling load met
using water cooled systems. 80 ℉ was selected as the design temperature for the air coolers, as a
compromise between the CAPEX for the air coolers and capture rate as it allows the plant to reach
95% capture rate averaged over summer and winter months. Lack of cooling water resulted in the
need for a steam integrated CO2 compressor system where the interstage coolers uses the boiler
feed water to raise steam. The larger area requirements for the CO2 capture plant with air coolers
also resulted in re-locating the capture plant site away from the CO2 capture plant, across the
railway line and the road. A detailed TEA has been carried out for CO2 capture at Cemex’s
Balcones plant with using RTI-NAS technology. The TEA uses data from the AACE Class 3 level
FEED study using DOE’s approach for estimation of levelized cost of capture for retrofit
applications for cement plants.
A detailed TEA has been carried out for CO2 capture at Cemex’s Balcones plant with using RTI-
NAS technology. The TEA uses data from the AACE Class 3 level FEED study using DOE’s
approach for estimation of levelized cost of capture for retrofit applications for cement plants.
Three different cases are evaluated. Case A is the base case with the capture plant as currently
designed with hybrid cooling and heat-integrated CO2 compressors. Case B is a hypothetical case
where the constraint for the limited water supply was removed and the plant and CO2 compressors
are water cooled and the plant is sited in proximity to the cement plant. This allows for a more
direct comparison with the DOE Baseline cases of CO2 capture for retrofit applications for cement
plants. Case C is a comparative case that evaluates the impact of co-generation of steam and
electricity using a high pressure (620 psig superheated steam at 721 ℉) and expanding that in a
turbine to generate power, and using the resulting low-pressure steam for solvent regeneration.
A comparative “Cemex ” case is also presented which uses the Cemex financial parameters (such
as property taxes and insurance), Cemex’s estimate for the owner’s cost (that includes the electrical
substation, cost estimates for air emissions permitting, construction and land use permits,
topography, soil and water studies, legal fees and technology, environmental and financial
consultants for the project), labor estimates based on local wages, utility prices and water supply.
The capital charge factor was assumed to be same as for DOE cases.
Results from the TEA are summarized in Table 7-1.

 559
 39 of 44

A sensitivity analysis was performed and the sensitive parameters identified included the capacity
factor and the CAPEX. where a 15% change indicated the highest impact from the capacity factor.
The unit price of natural gas and electricity had the lowest impact, with the cost of capture
increasing by 4% with a 30% increase in the utility prices. The solvent price also had a minimal
impact, with the cost of capture rising by 5% with a 30% increase in the unit price of NAS.

1.0 INTRODUCTION
 Since 2009, RTI International has been developing a process for post-combustion CO2
 capture based on novel, non-aqueous solvents (NAS) from ideation and proof of concept
 (Technology Readiness Level [TRL] 2) to the current front-end engineering design (FEED)
 for a NAS CO2 capture plant at the CEMEX Balcones cement plant in New Braunfels, TX.

1.1 Purpose of the Project
 The objective of this project is to perform a front-end engineering design (FEED) study for
 carbon dioxide (CO2) capture from cement flue gas using non-aqueous solvents (NAS) with
 95% capture efficiency. The desired outcome of the FEED in this project is to develop an
 Association of the Advancement of Cost Engineering (AACE) Class 3 cost estimate for a
 commercial 2.4 MM tonnes-CO2/year (t-CO2/yr.) scale CO2 capture system integrated with
 a cement facility. The detailed cost estimates will be used to perform a techno-economic
 analysis (TEA) of the resulting process configuration. The CO2 life cycle analysis (LCA) will
 account for CO2 being produced/removed/utilized from the cement with CO2 capture facility
 as well as CO2 generated offsite that is required to operate the facility. Lastly, a business case
 analysis (BCA) and an environmental, health, and safety (EH&S) risk analysis will be
 performed to evaluate the commercial viability and impact to the environmental and safety
 of the proposed process.

1.2 Technology Readiness Assessment System

 Technology Readiness Assessment (TRA) System is the integrated RTI’s NAS solvent
 technology for post-combustion CO2 capture from point sources. Critical components of the
 TRA system are:
 (a) Solvent formulation
 • that allows > 90 % CO2 capture
 • has low parasitic energy penalty for CO2 regeneration
 • allows for solvent regeneration at higher pressures up to ~ 4.4 bar
 (b) Process for water balanced stable operations
 (c) Any emission (amine, degradation products) below regulatory requirements.

1.3 Commercial Application
 Commercial applications for the TRA systems being developed under this project include
 CO2 capture from point sources such coal- or natural gas-fired power plants, or an industrial
 site such as a cement plant, a pulp and paper mill or an iron and steel plant. A specific
 application currently being pursued here is the CO2 capture from a cement manufacturing
 plant, where 95% of CO2 from the process, and the associated natural gas boiler for raising
 steam for solvent regeneration are captured.

 Page 2 of 7
 567

2.0 MATURATION OF THE TRA SYSTEM

2.1 Beginning Technology Readiness Level (TRL) of the TRA System
 Development of NAS started at RTI with developing critical data to show proof of concept
 under internal R&D funding. Using this data, RTI was able to secure DOE funding from
 ARPA-E to do a more detailed solvent screening and identify promising solvent formulations
 and evaluate them at lab-scale. Successful completion of that project led to a series of DOE-
 NETL funded projects where the solvent development progressed from lab-scale
 development to bench-scale development at RTI’s bench-scale gas absorption system
 (BsGAS) to small pilot plant testing using real flue gas at the Tiller plant at SINTEF
 (Norway) to engineering-scale demonstration at Technology Centre, Mongstad (Norway).
 Along the way, we also had some additional DOE funding to develop a novel emissions
 control technology for water-lean solvents and to evaluate the solvent using real flue gas at
 the National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC) at a coal-fired power plant in Alabama.
 Under previous DOE projects, RTI established the NAS-based post-combustion CO2 capture
 process at bench scale. Key performance attributes for NAS were to develop an optimized
 solvent formulation and determine solvent stability through detailed solvent degradation
 studies, using SINTEF’s well-established solvent degradation test rig and procedure.
 On the process side, key performance parameters were to establish a process design suitable
 for NAS and demonstrate the potential to achieve solvent regeneration heat duties ≤2.1
 GJ/tonne-CO2 at the bench scale. A pathway to a capture cost of 30/tonne-CO2 was
 demonstrated in a detailed techno-economic assessment. Preliminary emissions studies and
 environmental, health, and safety (EH&S) assessments were completed and warranted the
 further development of the technology.
 A more detailed timeline of the solvent development and ending TRLs at the end of each
 project is provided in Table 1.

2.2 Proposed Research to Mature the TRA System
 The current project is focused on performing a FEED study for the integrated CO2 capture
 plant to AACE Class 3 level (+/- 15% accuracy). Cost estimates at AACE Class 3 level will
 be used by CEMEX in their business case analysis to evaluate the commercial viability of
 the project. If the project economics are favorable, this will be followed by permitting,
 detailed engineering, procurement, and construction and finally operation of the integrated
 CO2 capture plant. At that point, the project will reach TRL 7. Since the current project ends
 with the completion of the FEED study, the start and end TRLs for the project will be at
 TRL6.
 Since this is a FEED study, the research efforts are focused on the design and integration of
 the capture plant and the key performance attributes that will be assessed during the FEED
 study are summarized in the table below:

2.3 Potential Post-Project Maturation and Commercialization of the TRA System
 Assuming the BCA based on the results from the FEED study indicates the technology to be
 commercially favorable, several steps are needed to advance the technology to the next TRL
 (TRL 7 - Full-scale, similar (prototypical) system demonstrated in relevant environment).
 These include:
 (a) Identify a characterized injection well site for CO2 sequestration
 (b) Identify and develop agreements for CO2 transportation to sequestration site.
 (c) complete all local and state permitting, and NEPA review required to initiate
 construction,
 (d) complete detailed engineering and develop datasheets that can be issued for
 construction
 (e) secure all approvals needed to move to construction phase
 (f) complete procurement and build for the capture plant
 From a risk management perspective, steps (a) and (b) would be needed to secure a
 sequestration site before work can begin on steps (c) through (f). Steps (c) through (e) should
 take about 12-18 months. Step (f) could be 2-3 years, depending on time needed for long lead
 items.
 RTI has entered into a licensing agreement with SLB to commercialize the NAS technology
 for post-combustion CO2 capture applications. SLB recently partnered with Aker Carbon
 Capture (ACC) to establish a new joint venture named SLB Capturi (SLBC), focused on CO2
 capture activities. ACC brings extensive experience in CCUS, having successfully executed
 multiple projects. It built the TCM CO2 capture plant and recently completed a 400 TPD
 CO₂ capture facility at the Brevik site in Norway. Additionally, Aker also completed a CO₂
 capture plant at Twence’s waste-to-energy facility in Henglo, Netherlands. The joint
 development and commercialization agreement between RTI and SLB enable the two
 organizations to develop an effective technology package for end-to-end
 As part of the solvent management strategy, RTI, in collaboration with SLBC, is working on
 two key initiatives: (a) developing a solvent reclamation process to remove heat-stable salts
 and degradation products, and (b) recovering amine from the acid wash. Currently, RTI uses
 an acid wash to control amine emissions from the NAS process. In its current configuration,
 the acid wash removes any residual amine from the treated gas by reacting the amine with
 the acid to form an amine-sulfate salt. This salt is typically treated as waste, and the lost
 amine must be replenished through the addition of makeup amine. However, this approach
 introduces operational inefficiencies in terms of chemical costs and waste handling.
 Experimental work conducted by RTI has demonstrated that this amine can be successfully
 separated from the acid wash waste stream with a recovery rate of at least 95%. The process
 entails neutralizing the amine-sulfate salt to regenerate the parent amine, which then
 separates out as an organic phase due to its hydrophobic properties. The recovered organic
 amine can be recycled into the NAS solvent loop, creating a closed-loop process. By reducing
 the loss of amine and limiting the need for fresh chemical makeup, this recovery process
 offers significant cost savings. Experimental testing has demonstrated the technical
 feasibility and maturity of the amine recovery from acid wash waste stream technology,
 thereby eliminating significant implementation risks. The technology is planned for inclusion
 as part of the standard design package for subsequent projects.
 RTI, in collaboration with its commercialization partner SLBC, is advancing the technology

 Page 6 of 7
 571

toward commercial readiness. As part of this effort, RTI is collecting data on the atmospheric
chemistry of the NAS amine and its degradation products. This data will be used to develop
air quality dispersion models to better understand the fate and transport of emitted
compounds, supporting future permitting activities. RTI and SLBC are also collaborating on
further assessment of some degradation products, their classification and relative exposure
in a commercial application.
Cemex is evaluating potential CO2 sequestration sites where the formation reaches
appropriate depth for supercritical CO2 phase. For Balcones plant, CEMEX has prepared a
detailed roadmap to reduce the carbon footprint by 2030 through “traditional” levers like
increased use of biomass-based alternative fuels, clinker factor reduction, energy efficiency
improvements, among others. 60% of the cement plant CO2 emissions come from process
emissions from decarbonization of the limestone. CEMEX intends to use a combination of
these traditional levers and CCUS technology such as the NAS technology to achieve net-
zero operations by 2035.

Background
This topical report presents the summary of environmental justice (EJ) considerations for project
under DE-FE0032220 involving the front-end engineering design (FEED) study of RTI’s Non
aqueous solvent (NAS) CO2 capture technology for CEMEX’s cement manufacturing plant in
Balcones, TX. This FEED study aims to implement the full-scale NAS CO2 capture plant removing
2.4 million tons of CO2 annually from the exhaust cement flue gas for sequestration. The
sequestration site has not been finalized. Specific EJ questions to be addressed are based on
Appendix D of the DE-FOA0002515.

Environmental Justice Analysis
1. How does the technology rely on limited resources such as coal, biomass, freshwater,
 land, and/or low-carbon energy? What is the relationship between the amount of
 resources used versus the amount of product formed?
 One of the process design challenges is the lack of raw water availability and its impact on
 cooling systems. The CEMEX Balcones Cement Plant is constrained to a raw water feed of
 191 GPM from the CEMEX process water pond. An additional ~400 GPM of condensate from
 the Feed Direct Contact Cooler (DCC) is available for use as cooling water. The high summer
 design temperature also poses a challenge because the use of air or water cooling is limited by
 LMTD (log-mean temperature difference). Due to limited water availability, the NAS CO2
 capture plant utilizes a hybrid approach with 84% of the required cooling provided by the air-
 cooled heat exchangers and the remainder provided using the traditional water-cooled heat
 exchangers. This, however, leads to increase in land use to accommodate a fleet of air-cooled
 heat exchangers that are used in various part of the process. FEED study suggests that the total
 of 434 units of air-cooled heat exchangers will be required to sufficiently facilitate the cooling
 for the NAS capture plant. This translates to 4 acres of land required to install the heat
 exchangers while the rest of the process requires 10 acres.

2. If coal is used as a feedstock, where will it be mined and what are the associated near-
 term and legacy environmental impacts of the coal mining, including methane leakage?
 Coal is not used in the proposed CO2 capture technology
3. How does the technology remediate legacy environmental impacts of the energy
 industry, including environmental impacts associated with the use of coal?
The NAS technology aims to remove CO2 produced from the calcination of limestone along with
the CO2 generated from the use of various fuels such as petcoke, natural gas, biomass, and mixed
industrial waste material feeding the furnaces.
4. If coal wastes are being remediated, what is the relationship between the amount of
 coal wastes used versus the amount of product formed?
 No coal wastes are being remediated in this project

Page 3 of 4                                                                      DE-FE0032220
 576

5. What is the project’s waste management strategy and what are the anticipated impacts
 of residual waste on local residents?
 All water collected in the facility from the process and drains/blowdowns is routed to the Waste
 Water Treatment Package for treatment. The treated wastewater is then returned to the Service
 Water Tank for recycle back to the process or discharged to the water pond at Balcones site.
 The water pond is current used to accommodate the wastewater discharge from the cement
 production and has a sufficient capacity to receive the treated wastewater generated from the
 CO2 capture plant.
6. To what extent does the technology provide ancillary environmental benefits, such as
 reductions in NOx and SOx emissions, particulate matter, or hazardous pollutants?
 NOx - The NAS CO2 capture plant requires a boiler package to provide energy for solvent
 regeneration. As a result, there is an additional NOx generated from the boiler package
 compared to the baseline NOx emission. The flue gas from this package boiler is routed to the
 inlet of capture plant to remove the CO2. Based on NAS testing at TCM, the use of NAS
 technology does not lower the NOx level in the outgoing treated flue gas. Although the NO2
 reacts with NAS's amine, NO2 only represents less than 2% of the NOx while the rest is NO.
 NO does not react to NAS or other chemicals presented in the NAS CO2 capture plant.
 SOx - DCC with caustic solution is used to remove SOx from the incoming flue gas. The
 technology has proved to the commercial setting that 90%+ of the SOx can be removed at-
 scale. Furthermore, a result from NAS testing at TCM shows that treated flue gas contained
 33% less SOx than the absorber inlet. Although the cement flue gas at Balcones site does not
 contain SOx, the auxiliary natural gas boiler installed for NAS CO2 capture plant generates a
 flue gas containing small amount of SOx that derives from H2S in the natural gas. The NG
 burner flue gas is routed to the DCC inlet along with the cement flue gas from the cement plant.
 The SOx is removed through the use of caustic DCC as well as the NAS in the CO2 capture
 plant. Overall, 90-93% SOx reduction can be expected.
 PM - A separate test of DCC to remove iron PM-laden flue gas was performed on a commercial
 project for NAS technology. The result shows a 61% reduction of the PM concentration of
 6.05 to 2.31 mg/m3, demonstrating the effectiveness of DCC to remove PM from the flue gas.
7. If the project involves a carbon capture retrofit technology, what are the potential co-
 benefits of the carbon capture technology (e.g., reduction of other hazardous air
 pollutants or reduction of other negative environmental impacts commonly associated
 with existing natural gas power plants or industrial processes)?
 The potential co-benefits of using NAS technology are described in responses to question 6.
8. How is the project incorporating a plan to ensure community and stakeholder input
 and engagement from underserved communities, which include persons of color;
 members of religious minorities; lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, and queer
 (LGBTQ+) persons; persons with disabilities; persons who live in rural areas; and
 persons otherwise adversely affected by persistent poverty or inequality?
 This question is not applicable.

O Workforce Readiness Plan

 578

WORKFORCE READINESS PLAN
Recipient Name: RTI International, CEMEX, Inc., Schlumberger (SLB), and KBR, Inc.
PI Name: Dr. Vijay Gupta, RTI International
Award Number: DE-FE003220
Project Title: Carbon Capture Plant FEED Study for Cement Manufacturing Technology

Availability and accessibility of training programs:
Cemex: The Balcones project will build upon Cemex’s existing workforce development, educational, and
career programs to provide the skills training, certification, and education required for the project and
to help community members access future clean energy jobs. Training requirements will be reviewed by
Cemex and any necessary training on this topic will be developed and implemented as appropriate.
Cemex runs several training and professional development programs for its employees, including a 24-
hour onboarding training for new staff, and providing 40 hours of training per year per employee to
upskill, reskill, and raise climate action awareness.
Cemex University is an award-winning continuous learning program with in-person and online courses
available across diverse platforms, devices, and locations. Employees can access more than 150 courses
within seven different academies and four leadership development programs, such as the Health &
Safety Academy, Cement Operations Academy, Digital Academy and Sustainability Academy. Cemex
University has reached more than 24,000 employees, with an average 90% learner satisfaction rate. In
2023, Cemex University saw over 18,000 active learners and more than 70,000 course completions.
Furthermore, Cemex has collaborations with LinkedIn Learning and the University of Phoenix to offer
additional courses and certifications.
RTI: As the developer of the carbon capture solvent, RTI International has a material safety data sheet
complying with OSHA requirements. This sheet classifies the hazards associated with this specific
chemical, provides precautionary statements on use and storage, and contains first aid measures that
should be taken in the event of accidental exposure. If this project progresses to the demonstration
stage, RTI International, along with other project partners, will engage with facility staff, local fire
departments, first responders, and emergency management personnel to review emergency response
protocols. Emergency response agencies will be encouraged to review their equipment and personal
protective gear resources considering the potential need to respond to an emergency at the Balcones
facility. RTI is creating more detailed environmental health and safety information for the NAS solvent.
As presented below, SLB will train plant operators on the equipment, equipment-specific safety issues,
and equipment maintenance. The people hired to operate the plant may come from Cemex’s existing
workforce or be sourced from similar industries or educational institutes.
Carbon capture plants may be staffed by people from other industries that use similar technologies like
chemical and gas processing plants. Based on analysis of the industrial gas manufacturing industry,
specific occupations with transferable skills could include, but is not limited to, the following:

 579

operators can be trained in maintaining some of the equipment in the plant. Especially for the common
types of equipment / utility systems etc.

The general nature of these jobs can be characterized in the following ways:
 • An operating supervisor should possess a bachelor’s or master’s degree or have 4-6 years of
 equivalent experience in chemical engineering, process engineering, and a comparable
 combination of education and experience. Other preferred education and experience
 includes 5-10 years' experience in industrial gasses, natural gas processing and/or CO2
 capture projects and have experience as an engineer or testing in a cement facility, chemical
 processing, gas, oil, or air separation plan.
 • Control room operators should have a bachelor’s degree in petroleum, power, chemical,
 mechanical, electrical, environmental, or instrumentation engineering and at least 3 years of
 experience in a processing plan. Other preferred skills and experience include an
 understanding of control room operation, technical and soft skills, experience working in
 similar chemical processes, and self-starting.
 • Maintenance technicians do not have to be college graduates but should have technologist
 training or equivalent.
 • Lab specialists do not require any specific certifications or experience, although training in
 the following areas would be advantageous: Carbon Capture, Utilization, and Storage (CCUS)
 training, climate change and energy transition courses, safety and risk management training,
 technical skills in laboratory practices, and regulatory compliance and environmental policy.

Identify any Economically Distressed Communities or state or federal designated Opportunity Zones
or other geographically defined empowerment zones where this activity may occur.
The front-end engineering design (FEED) study is being conducted for Cemex’s Balcones Cement Plant in
New Braunfels, Texas. The facility is in U.S. Census Tract 48091310802, within Comal County. According
to the Department of Energy’s Working Justice40 Disadvantaged Communities database, the census
tract is not designated as disadvantaged, nor does it share a border with any disadvantaged tracts.
However, 150 out of 372 Census tracts in the broader project area meet the Climate and Economic
Justice Screening Tool’s definition of a disadvantaged community (see Table 3), representing 33% of the
broader project area’s (30 mi. radius) population. Thirty-one of these tracts exceed the threshold for
only one category of burden, while 119 exceed the threshold for two or more categories of burden.

 582

 Within the project area (30 mi. radius), 32% of the population is low income.2 The project area is also
 above the national average for the Hispanic population, unemployment, and adults without a high
 school diploma. New jobs created by this project will potentially be filled by people within this area.
 Additional project work is being performed at KBR’s offices in Wilmington, DE, Cemex’s corporate offices
 in Houston, TX, RTI International’s headquarters in Research Triangle Park, NC, and SLB’s facility in
 Houston, TX.

P Economic Revitalization and Job Creation Outcome
  Analysis

 585

ECONOMIC REVITALIZATION AND JOB CREATION OUTCOMES ANALYSIS
Recipient Name: RTI International, CEMEX, Inc., Schlumberger (SLB), and KBR, Inc.
PI Name: Dr. Vijay Gupta, RTI International
Award Number: DE-FE0032220
Project Title: Carbon Capture Plant FEED Study for Cement Manufacturing Technology

Apart from job creation, how will the project and/or the associated technology development and
deployment support economic revitalization?

Cement has a sizable carbon footprint because CO2 is directly released during the production process.
Efforts to decarbonize existing cement plants may extend the life of these facilities and avoid job losses.
In addition to sequestering carbon, NAS technology reduces the amounts of SO2 and particulate matter in
air emissions. These reductions may be necessary in the future to keep Comal County in attainment with
federal air quality standards. When an area is designated as a “nonattainment” area this negatively
impacts businesses that want to locate or expand a business in that area.1

Whereas job creation is a significant aspect, CO2 capture technology can potentially revitalize the
economy beyond that through the fostering innovation and technology leadership of the US. Investing in
CO2 capture research and development can spur innovation in various fields like materials science,
engineering, and chemical processing. This can lead to the creation of new technologies and industries,
positioning the US as a leader in innovative solutions in multiple disciplines. The development of more
efficient and cost-effective CO2 capture technologies also can attract international investment and
partnerships, further boosting the economy. Investments in CO2 capture infrastructure can create new
opportunities for sustainable development and green jobs, attracting talent and investment in
environmentally conscious industries.

How many jobs could potentially be created by the project and/or associated remediated, number of
jobs created per unit of emissions mitigated, and/or another appropriate metric.

The project team’s preliminary estimates for job creation during the installation of the CO2 capture unit
to the Balcones plant shows that it would create more than 300 construction jobs during the 18 to 22-
month duration for construction. The jobs would include direct crafts and associated supervision to carry
out the construction and commissioning of the added CO2 capture unit.

Under the normal operation, approximately 4 or 5 new jobs per shift would be needed, or up to 15 total
new jobs. New positions for each shift would consist of 2 operators in the control room, 1 operating
supervisor/team lead in the plant itself, 1 maintenance operator and 1 laboratory specialist. For other
maintenance work, it is assumed that existing Cemex operators can be trained in maintaining some of the
equipment in the plant. Especially for the common types of equipment / utility systems etc.

What will be the nature of the created jobs, including the extent to which they will include clean energy
jobs?

The general nature of these jobs can be characterized in the following ways:

 • An operating supervisor should possess a bachelor’s or master’s degree or have 4-6 years of
 equivalent experience in chemical engineering, process engineering, and a comparable
 combination of education and experience. Other preferred education and experience includes 5-
 10 years' experience in industrial gasses, natural gas processing and/or CO2 capture projects and
 have experience as an engineer or testing in a cement facility, chemical processing, gas, oil, or air
 separation plan.
 • Control room operators should have a bachelor’s degree in petroleum, power, chemical,
 mechanical, electrical, environmental, or instrumentation engineering and at least 3 years of
 experience in a processing plan. Other preferred skills and experience include an understanding
 of control room operation, technical and soft skills, experience working in similar chemical
 processes, and self-starting.
 • Maintenance technicians do not have to be college graduates but should have technologist
 training or equivalent.
 • Lab specialists do not require any specific certifications or experience, although training in
 the following areas would be advantageous: Carbon Capture, Utilization, and Storage (CCUS)
 training, climate change and energy transition courses, safety and risk management training,
 technical skills in laboratory practices, and regulatory compliance and environmental policy.

All created construction and operation jobs can be classified as clean energy jobs, given their direct role
in the construction and operation of the CCS facility.

To what extent will the created jobs be at the prevailing wage?

During the construction phase of the project, subcontractors would be responsible for ensuring that they
comply with the Davis Bacon Act and pay laborers no less than the locally prevailing wages and fringe
benefits for corresponding work on similar projects in the area. As the lead grant recipient, RTI
International will contract with an experienced third party well versed in prevailing wage work to
determine proper application of the required rates and feeds and ensure compliance by construction
subcontractors.

The plant employees approximately 171 individuals and 95 are members of the Local Teamsters 657. The
union sets wages with the company through a Collective Bargaining Agreement. For workers who are not
union members, Cemex define wage rates based on market benchmarks which include: information
on job boards, compensation surveys, recruiters/headhunter input, and prevailing wages.

To what extent will the created jobs be located in power plant and coal communities that are
economically distressed and/or have been harmed by the adverse environmental impacts of the energy
industry?

Construction and operational jobs are at the Cemex facility in Comal County, TX. The U.S. Department of
Energy has not determined that Comal County is a designated energy community. However, nearby Bexar

 587

County, which is within commuting distance from the Cemex plant, is eligible for the Qualifying Advanced
Energy Project Credit (48 C) Program, which is designed for communities that have been negatively
impacted by the closure of either a coal-fired power plant or a coal mine.2

As presented in the Workforce Readiness Plan, 150 out of 372 Census tracts in a 30-mile radius around
the project site meet the Climate and Economic Justice Screening Tool’s definition of a disadvantaged
community, representing 33% of the area’s population. If this project is constructed, local communities
will benefit from better air quality and workforce development opportunities offered by the project.

What recruitment strategies will be used for: a) workers from the local community, and b) individuals
who belong to groups that are historically underserved or underrepresented?

Cemex recruitment strategies include broad-based strategies such as utilizing hiring banners and
billboards throughout the community and participating in various career fairs and hiring events, such as
the Texas State Technical College Career Fair, Indeed Hiring Event, and numerous other local high school,
college and community-oriented career fairs. Additionally, we offer a Referral Bonus program to our
employees who successfully refer candidates for hire. Furthermore, Cemex maintains partnerships with
Orion HirePurpose® and Society of Women Engineers whose recruitment strategies are geared towards
underserved populations such as women, persons with disabilities, veterans/military, and disparate
race/ethnicity groups. Recruitment at Historically Black Colleges and Universities (HBCUs) such as Prairie
View A&M and Texas Southern University and Hispanic-Serving Institutions (HSIs) such as University of
Houston and Texas A&M also expands the candidate pool to ensure opportunities are granted for
underrepresented race/ethnicity groups. Cemex also recently participated in a Juneteenth HBCU Career
Fair and MLK Parade in Houston, distributing recruitment information. Events such as these attract
candidates throughout the Southwest that have experiences that qualify them for jobs specific to our
Balcones operations.

KBR will post positions in the local area, so the community is aware of the available positions. We will
establish a relationship with the local unemployment offices. KBR prides it’s self on being one of the most
diverse companies in the world. It strives to hire the most qualified candidates, and pulls from a diverse
candidate pool, by posting the positions in various locations such as indeed, LinkedIn, and through HBU’s.
Non-union construction contractors will recruit local craftsmen through local advertising, word of mouth,
and temporary hiring agencies. Union contractors will deploy craftsmen through the associated Union
Hall.

Will the skills possessed by the existing labor force be adequate for the created jobs, or will training be
required for those workers?

Even though the project is near San Antonio, there is expected to be some difficulty hiring craft workers
for the construction portion of the project based on current labor surveys published from Alpha Resources
(10/4/24) considering the size of the job. A craft per diem (50% of workers x $96/day) is expected to
attract some of the workers who live outside of the region.

Carbon capture operations initially may be staffed by people from other industries that use similar
technologies such as chemical and gas processing plants or through upskilling existing workers. Based on
analysis of the existing industrial gas manufacturing industry, many needed occupations are well
represented in the San Antonio New Braunfels metropolitan statistical area (MSA). According to
Workforce Solutions Alamo, the local workforce development board, the region has an unemployment
rate of 3.9% with more than 54,000 people actively looking for work.3 The oil and gas extraction industry
is expected to be one of the top ten fastest growing industries between 2022 and 2023 and the local
workforce development board has identified this sector as one that will require targeted
assistance.4Training for chemical technicians, other production workers, and material moving workers
requires either a high school or associate degree and on-the-job training, whereas the chemical and
electrical engineering positions would require bachelor’s degrees. Several construction occupations such
as carpenters and first-line supervisors on construction also are considered in-demand occupations and
may require training support from the local workforce development board.
